Gates pass trivially; the risk here is *content* accuracy, not build breakage, so the reviewer's attention goes to whether ADR-037's amendment claims against ADR-025/ADR-028 are precisely stated.

## 2. Xcode project and Swift package skeleton

- **[architect]** Base: `7737035` — a generated Xcode project, the `dmon-home` Swift packages, and `make` targets that build and test them headlessly.
- **[architect]** Block 2.1–2.3 — the whole section as one block, revising the two-block carve I sketched in NEXT. Reason: gates are `make build` / `make test` / `openspec validate`, all of which are .NET-side and would pass *trivially* for 2.1–2.2 while proving nothing about the Swift code. The `make` targets in 2.3 are what make this section verifiable at all, so splitting them off would produce a block whose gates are vacuous. One block, one commit.
- **[architect]** Decisions taken for this block, all recorded here because none is in `design.md` yet:
  - **Swift 6 language mode**, not dmonium's 5.9. Toolchain on this machine is Swift 6.3.3 / Xcode 26.6. PRD §4.3 permits `@unchecked Sendable` and `nonisolated(unsafe)` *"at the ring buffer boundary, where a safety property is being asserted that the compiler cannot see"* and warns that their appearance elsewhere means "a concurrency diagnostic has been suppressed rather than a problem solved" — a constraint that is only meaningful under strict concurrency checking. Choosing 5.9 now would silently void PRD §4.3 before the audio phases start.
  - **One `home/Package.swift` with three library targets**, not three separate packages. PRD §2.3's table says "packages"; targets give the same module seam and import boundary, with one manifest to maintain and a single `swift test --package-path home` that mirrors the existing `daemon-app-test` pattern. XcodeGen wires one local package rather than three.
  - **`make dmon-home` / `make dmon-home-test`**, named for the product, mirroring `daemon-app` / `daemon-app-test`. Must not disturb the existing pair (design D9 / spec "The existing Swift app is untouched").
  - **The generated `.xcodeproj` is gitignored**; `home/project.yml` is the source of truth. `.gitignore:90` already ignores `daemon/Daemon.App/.build/`, so the `home/` equivalents go alongside it.
  - **Seeded, not stubbed.** `specs/dmon-home/spec.md` forbids a package existing "solely as a placeholder for a later phase", and SPM requires every target to have at least one source file. Resolution: each target is seeded with a small type its own section genuinely needs and will not delete — `Power.ActivityAssertion` (used by 5.2), `Supervisor.ChildID` + `ChildHealth` (used by 4.1/4.3), `GatewayClient.WireVersion` (used by 6.4). The "no empty placeholder packages" scenario is judged at **change** scope, not block scope: Supervisor, GatewayClient and Power all gain substantial code in sections 4, 5 and 6 of *this* change, which is exactly what distinguishes them from `AudioEngine`/`Speech`/`Directedness` (design D3) — those belong to a later change and are not created.

- **[worker]** 2.1–2.3 done. `home/project.yml` (XcodeGen, `DmonHomeApp`, `ai.daemonic.dmon-home`, macOS 14+), `home/Package.swift` (`swift-tools-version: 6.0`, `.swiftLanguageMode(.v6)` on all six targets), three library targets under `home/Sources/` with tests under `home/Tests/`, app shell at `home/App/DmonHomeApp/` (outside the package's source trees). `make dmon-home` / `make dmon-home-test` added after `daemon-app-test` and to `.PHONY`. `.gitignore` gained `home/.build/` and `home/*.xcodeproj`. Seeds: `Power.ActivityAssertion`, `Supervisor.ChildID`/`ChildHealth`, `GatewayClient.WireVersion` (`current = 0.2`). Gates: `make build` 0 warnings, `env -u MEKO_API_KEY make test` green, `make dmon-home-test` 17/17 headless, `make daemon-app-test` 72/72, `openspec validate --strict` valid. → @reviewer
- **[worker]** Self-caught against brief hazard 3: `ActivityAssertion` was first drafted with `NSLock` + `@unchecked Sendable`, then rewritten as an `actor`. No unsafe concurrency escape hatches anywhere in the block.
- **[architect]** Verified the worker's claims independently before review rather than taking the report: re-ran `make dmon-home-test` (17/17 confirmed), confirmed the generated `.xcodeproj` does not appear in `git status`, and confirmed nothing outside the block moved. The editor's SourceKit diagnostics ("No such module 'Testing'/'GatewayClient'") are **index noise, not build failures** — `Testing` ships with the Swift 6.3.3 toolchain and resolves at build time; told the reviewer not to report them.
- **[architect]** Worker also hit the known dmonium gotcha: `swift test` deadlocking on a stale `.build` lock, ~5 min hang at near-zero CPU on `mach_msg`. Pre-existing (recorded from the `dmonium-mlx-realignment` change), unrelated to this block, resolved by killing the stale PID.
- **[architect]** **Unbriefed divergence sent to the reviewer for a verdict:** the worker used **swift-testing** (`import Testing`, `@Test`) for all `home/` tests, while `daemon/Daemon.App` uses **XCTest**. It works (17/17), and greenfield + modern-default + better ergonomics for value-type tests all argue for it, against the real cost of two frameworks in one repo for anyone moving between the Swift trees. Not waving it through on my own judgement — asked for an explicit recommendation so it lands as a **recorded decision** rather than an implicit one, which is how the D2 drift started.

- **[reviewer]** **Approve.** Verified rather than inferred: deleted `home/DmonHomeApp.xcodeproj`, ran `xcodegen generate`, rebuilt via `xcodebuild` → BUILD SUCCEEDED, proving the regeneration scenario for real. 17/17 Swift tests, zero build warnings, dmonium 72/72 unaffected, `daemon/` zero working-tree diff, no `home.slnx`, no `.slnx` referencing `home/`. Grepped all of `home/` for `@unchecked Sendable` / `nonisolated(unsafe)` / `@preconcurrency` — none; `.swiftLanguageMode(.v6)` confirmed on all **six** targets, and `project.yml`'s `SWIFT_STRICT_CONCURRENCY: complete` is redundant-not-contradictory. No sandbox/entitlement/microphone strings anywhere — section 3's scope correctly untouched.
  - `ActivityAssertion` correctness confirmed at the code, not the test: `begin()` guards `token == nil` so a second call is a true no-op (no leaked handle), and `release()` nils the token **before** calling `endActivity`, so re-entry cannot double-release a stale token. Actor isolation proves the serialisation the doc comment claims.
  - `WireVersion` correctness confirmed: compatibility checks major **and** minor (not the common major-only bug), `current` is `0.2`, and parsing throws on malformed input rather than defaulting.
  - **Directed check on swift-testing: recommend accept, and record it explicitly.** Reasoning accepted in full — the two-idiom cost is real but *bounded*, because ADR-037 D3 retires `daemon/Daemon.App` at parity so the trees are not meant to coexist; swift-testing's async-native `#expect` fits `actor`-isolated state materially better than XCTest plus boilerplate; and nothing in `home/` couples to XCTest-only machinery that would force a mixed approach later.
  - **Nit (not fixed):** `home/App/DmonHomeApp/ContentView.swift` constructs a `placeholderActivityAssertion` it never begins or releases. Harmless — actor init is side-effect-free and no resource is held — but it is the one place the app shell instantiates something with real semantics purely to prove linkage. Left as-is; **raised explicitly with the section-2 supervisor** rather than waved through, since "instantiated but unused" is squarely in the dead-scaffolding family a section review hunts, and 5.2 is the task that wires this type for real.
- **[architect]** Recorded D14 in `design.md` — Swift 6 language mode, the single-manifest/three-target layout, and swift-testing — rather than leaving them in the DEVLOG only. Decisions that live solely in a working log are exactly how the section-1 D2 drift started: `design.md` is what later sections and the supervisor read. Boxes 2.1–2.3 ticked.

- **[supervisor]** Section review of `7737035..HEAD` (`4022192`, tasks 2.1–2.3). **Request changes** — two blockers, both small, one remediation block. Substance sound: no ADR erosion, no artifact/code drift beyond the one noted, no generated or machine-specific leakage (commit is exactly 17 files; `.build`, `.xcodeproj` and `.swiftpm` all ignored; no `Package.resolved` because there are no external dependencies).
  1. **Nothing tracked compiles the app target.** `make dmon-home` builds only `home/Sources/*`; `home/App/DmonHomeApp/` is outside `Package.swift`'s target paths, so both app source files are compiled by **no** `make` target. `xcodegen`/`xcodebuild` appear nowhere in the `Makefile`, `.github/`, or any tracked instruction — only as prose in `home/PRD.md:301`. Four consequences in ascending cost: a fresh clone cannot build the app; the "regenerates from the manifest" scenario is satisfied by tribal knowledge rather than a command; **task 3.3's recipe has no referent** — `make dmon-home` produces no `.app`, and 3.3 is the one task needing the Product Owner's hands; and section 10.1's CI would leave `ContentView.swift` free to break with every gate green.
  2. **`design.md` D3 contradicted D14.** D3 said "four SPM packages"; what shipped is one package with three library targets plus the app target. D14 declared the substitution but D3 was never reconciled — the same cross-artifact drift class that cost section 1 two rounds, at smaller scale.
- **[supervisor]** Ruling on `ContentView.swift`'s `placeholderActivityAssertion`: **remove it.** Three reasons, the third only visible at section scope. (a) It proves *less* than its neighbours — `wireVersion` and `placeholderChildHealth` are read in `body`, so the compiler proves those modules link; an unused stored property emits no Swift diagnostic and proves nothing. (b) **It seeds the wrong ownership for 5.2** — an `actor` stored on a SwiftUI `View` struct, which SwiftUI recreates on invalidation; the obvious next move is `.task { await …begin() }`, binding the assertion to *view* lifetime when the spec requires *gateway* lifetime. Not inert: it models the wrong owner. (c) Its justification is void while nothing repeatable compiles the app. Overturns the reviewer's "not worth a fix", which was defensible on the diff alone — vindicating passing it up rather than accepting it.
- **[architect]** Doc half of the remediation done directly: `design.md` D3 reworded to "four modules — three library targets in one package, plus the app target" with a D14 cross-reference; D14 gained a paragraph stating the swift-testing decision does **not** depend on the retirement schedule (supervisor note 3 — the two trees share no test code or fixtures, so the cost is a reading cost, not a maintenance one; the bounded-window argument explains why the cost is small, not why the decision is right); `tasks.md` 3.3 now names `make dmon-home-app` and records that ad-hoc signing makes a fresh TCC prompt expected on every rebuild, so `tccutil reset` should be unnecessary. Makefile target and the `ContentView` removal briefed to the worker.
- **[architect]** My miss on blocker 1, worth naming: I verified `make dmon-home-test` passed and never asked *what compiles the app*. Gate-passes-green is not the same question as everything-is-gated.

- **[worker]** Remediation done. `make dmon-home-app` added (`.PHONY` + target), with a comment recording the `xcodegen` prerequisite at the point of use. Two corrections to my draft, both right: `--project home` pins xcodegen's output directory deterministically rather than relying on unstated default behaviour, and `-configuration Release` matches what `dmon-home` already does. Scheme name verified via `xcodebuild -list` rather than assumed. `placeholderActivityAssertion` and `import Power` removed from `ContentView.swift`; all three imports in `DmonHomeApp.swift` confirmed genuinely dead and removed; `wireVersion` and `placeholderChildHealth` retained and still read in `body`, so `GatewayClient`/`Supervisor` linkage is still proven.
- **[architect]** Follow-on I sent back before review, from the worker's own report rather than a gate failure: the built `.app` was landing in `~/Library/Developer/Xcode/DerivedData/DmonHomeApp-<hash>/…`, where the hash derives from the checkout's absolute path. Task 3.3 requires the **Product Owner** to launch that bundle from Finder by hand — the one task in this change needing a human — and PRD §1 says to take the lower-friction option where a choice exists. Now pinned via `-derivedDataPath home/.build-xcode`, giving a stable `home/.build-xcode/Build/Products/Release/DmonHomeApp.app`, verified identical after deleting the directory and rebuilding cold. Kept distinct from SwiftPM's `home/.build/` so the two build systems cannot collide; `.gitignore` updated and confirmed via `git check-ignore -v`. Task 3.3's recipe now names the path with an `open -R` to reveal it.
- **[architect]** Verified the app build myself (`make dmon-home-app`, exit 0) rather than trusting the report. The editor's new SourceKit diagnostics — including *"'main' attribute cannot be used in a module that contains top-level code"* — are index noise again, the third such false alarm on this tree; the real `xcodebuild` is clean.
- **[architect]** ⚠️ **Pre-existing flaky test, recorded not dismissed.** The worker hit `WizardEngineTests.InvalidChooseOneAnswer_RePromptsStep` (`test/Dmon.Core.Tests/Rpc/WizardEngineTests.cs`, covering `core/Dmon.Core/Rpc/ProviderSetupHandler.cs`) failing on one `make test` run and passing standalone and on rerun. **Not caused by this block** — its file scope is `Makefile`, `.gitignore` and two Swift files, none of which the .NET test suite touches. Logging it because "passed on rerun" is how a real intermittent bug gets buried: a wizard step that intermittently fails to re-prompt on invalid input is a plausible ordering or async-timing defect, not obviously test-only. Carried to NEXT for a separate investigation; **not** in scope for this change.

- **[reviewer]** **Approve** on the remediation diff — no blockers, no nits. Checked `-quiet` against `xcodebuild -help` to confirm it suppresses only non-warning/error output and still propagates a nonzero exit, so a real failure still fails `make`. Independently assessed `-derivedDataPath` against the alternatives and endorsed it: standard single-flag pinning, repo-relative, and it cannot contend with Xcode.app because a GUI build uses its own default DerivedData. Noted `--project home` is technically redundant (xcodegen already defaults to the spec's directory) but harmless and defensibly explicit. Confirmed `daemon-app`, `daemon-app-test`, `dmon-home`, `dmon-home-test` and `network` byte-identical to `4022192`; `wireVersion` and `placeholderChildHealth` still interpolated in `body` so the linkage proof survives; the three `DmonHomeApp.swift` imports were dead even before the fix; and `Power`'s D3 standing rests on its own four passing tests, untouched by the removal.

- **[supervisor]** Re-reviewed `7737035..HEAD` (`4022192` + `a9c106a`). **Approve.** Both blockers verified closed against the tree. All four consequences of blocker 1 are shut: a fresh clone has a command, the "regenerates from the manifest" scenario is executable rather than remembered, `tasks.md` 3.3 names a real path, and section 10 has something that compiles `ContentView.swift`. `design.md` `## Decisions` now tells one story, and the `Power` paragraph is untouched and still correct because it never rested on `ContentView`. Endorsed both worker corrections as load-bearing: xcodegen's default project directory resolves relative to the spec and would have shifted silently if the spec ever moved, and XcodeGen's scheme names are configurable rather than equal to the target name by construction, so verifying via `xcodebuild -list` was the right instinct.
  - On the derived-data pin: *"I identified that the app was unbuildable; you identified that buildable isn't sufficient when a human executes the recipe by hand. A DerivedData path hashed from the checkout's absolute location is unquotable in a task file and unverifiable by anyone but the person whose machine produced it — for the one task in this change that needs the Product Owner's hands, that is the difference between a recipe and a hint."*
  - Confirmed the new target cannot mask a failure: `-quiet` still prints warnings and errors and still exits non-zero; each recipe line is its own shell with no `-` prefix, no `|| true` and **no pipe** — a pipeline would have been the real hazard, since the exit status would come from the tail. A failing `xcodegen` aborts before `xcodebuild` runs.
  - Regressions clean: `daemon-app`, `daemon-app-test`, `network`, `release-wave`, `dmon-home`, `dmon-home-test` all byte-identical; no `ci.yml`/`release.yml`/`daemon/` file in range; `home/`'s tracked file list unchanged at 13; `home/.build-xcode/` directory-anchored so it cannot swallow a source path.

## 4. Supervision

- **[architect]** Base: `2caeee2` — a configuration-driven supervision model that adopts live children before spawning them, health-checks each on a bounded timeout, restarts crashes with backoff, starts and stops in dependency order, and kills only what it spawned — with `Dmon.Network` as the one child actually started.
- **[architect]** **Block carve — five blocks, and one of them is deliberately out of numeric order.** The section's own dependency graph, not `tasks.md`'s numbering, decides the sequence:
  - **B1 = 4.1** — the child descriptor and the full inventory. Pure model; everything else consumes it.
  - **B2 = 4.3** — bounded health checking and published health. **Pulled ahead of 4.2** because 4.2's adoption decision *is* a health check ("health-check the endpoint first, adopt when it answers"). Cutting 4.2 first would mean writing the probe twice, once throwaway.
  - **B3 = 4.2 + 4.6** — adopt-or-spawn, the process group, group-kill on exit, adoption exempt. These are one code path, not two: 4.6 is not a feature added to spawning, it is *how* spawning and killing work. Splitting them guarantees rewriting `spawn`.
  - **B4 = 4.4 + 4.5** — the policy layer over B3's primitive: crash detection with backoff, dependency-ordered startup and reverse graceful shutdown. B3 owns "terminate this child gracefully then escalate"; B4 owns *in what order* those calls are made.
  - **B5 = 4.7** — the real `Dmon.Network` child, plus verification. Expected to carry a human-in-the-loop component (relaunching the host to prove adoption); assessed when the block is carved, not assumed now.
- **[architect]** **Spawn-mechanism hazard, flagged into B3's brief rather than discovered in it.** Foundation's `Process` gives stdout/stderr pipes trivially — which **section 5.1 needs** — but offers no way to place the child in its own process group: there is no hook between fork and exec, `setpgid` from the parent races the exec, and macOS ships no `setsid(1)`. The likely answer is a small `posix_spawn` launcher (`POSIX_SPAWN_SETPGROUP` with pgroup 0, `posix_spawn_file_actions_adddup2` for the pipes, `kill(-pgid, …)` to signal the group). **This is a cross-section decision, not a B3-local one:** whatever B3 chooses must still be able to hand section 5.1 a pair of pipes, so the brief states both constraints and asks the worker to verify rather than assume.
- **[architect]** Ran `graphify update .` before carving (recorded in section 3) — the `home/` bucket is now on the map the workers consult first.
- **[architect]** **B1 (4.1) briefed.** Four calls made in the brief rather than left to the worker, because each one is the difference between a model that survives 4.2–4.7 and one that gets reworked:
  1. **"No bespoke type per child" is the requirement's teeth, and the test is mechanical:** if expressing the inventory needs a `switch` over child kind, or a subclass, or a protocol conformance per child, the model has failed. Adding a child must be adding a *value*.
  2. **Monitors must be un-spawnable by construction, not by convention.** The spec says they are "health sources that are never spawned, adopted or killed" — so the launch/adoption facet has to be *absent* from a monitor, not merely unused. A `Bool isMonitor` that every call site must remember to check is the wrong shape.
  3. **The health check is declared, not closured.** A closure would make the descriptor un-`Equatable`, un-inspectable and hostile to the tests B2 needs; a declarative case (`.http(URL)` / `.tcp(host, port)` / `.none`) keeps execution in B2 where it belongs.
  4. **Endpoints stay data.** Design D2 is explicit that the gateway endpoint is configuration and may later be a LAN hostname; `http://127.0.0.1:5500` may be a *default value* in the inventory but must not be baked into a type.
  - Also flagged the **divergence from dmonium's precedent** so the worker doesn't copy it wholesale: `ServerProcessConfig` adopts via a **PID file**; this spec adopts via an **endpoint health check**. The config-driven shape transfers; the adoption mechanism does not.

- **[worker]** B1 (4.1) done. Seven types in `Supervisor` — `ChildTransport`, `HealthCheck`, `AdoptionPolicy`, `ChildLaunch`, `ChildDescriptor`, `MonitorDescriptor`, `ChildInventory` — plus seven test files. Monitor distinction taken as a **separate struct with no launch/adoption/order facet at all**, not a flag on a shared type. `HealthCheck` a plain `Hashable` enum (`.http` / `.tcp` / `.process` / `.none`); `.process` added beyond the brief because Tailscale's real health source is a CLI invocation, not an endpoint. Endpoint defaults derived from repo evidence rather than invented. 41 tests / 11 suites green.
- **[reviewer]** **Approve with nits.** Verified at the code rather than reasoned: every endpoint default against its cited source (`DcalHealthMonitor`, `EndpointExtensions.cs`, `MlxRuntimeOptions.cs`, `DaemonController`, `TailscaleMonitor.poll()`); `.process`'s args byte-match `TailscaleMonitor.swift:93-94`; grepped `switch` across all 14 files and found no switch over child kind; grepped `unchecked` / `nonisolated(unsafe)` / `@preconcurrency` / `assumeIsolated` — zero hits; all 7 test files `import Testing`.
  - **Nit taken — `MonitorDescriptorTests.hasNoLaunchOrAdoptionFacet` is theatre.** It reflects over stored-property *name strings* to assert a fact the type checker already enforces absolutely — no code can reference `monitor.launch` whatever the test says — and it false-passes if a launch facet ever returns under a different name (`spawnConfig`). Brittle *and* redundant is the rare combination that argues for deletion rather than repair.
  - **Nit taken:** the extensibility test is named `aHypotheticalEighthChild`; there are six, so it is the seventh.
  - **Concurred with the Architect on `startsInThisChange`** independently.
- **[architect]** **Blocker the review missed, found by checking the precedent rather than the diff: `ChildLaunch` asserts knowledge the repo does not have.** Five of six descriptors carry a fabricated executable, and the sixth carries an unusable one:
  - **Gateway** — `"~/.dotnet/tools/ndmon"`. Nothing expands a leading `~`: `FileManager.isExecutableFile(atPath:)` and `Process.executableURL` both take it literally. dmonium builds the same path as `(NSHomeDirectory() as NSString).appendingPathComponent(".dotnet/tools/ndmon")` for exactly this reason. As written, task 4.7 consuming this field resolves nothing and the gateway never starts.
  - **Dcal / Dmail** — `"dcal"` / `"dmail"`. `ServiceManager.candidates` (lines 99–103) shows dmonium has **no default path for either**: only a settings override and `DMON_DCAL_SERVER_PATH` / `DMON_DMAIL_SERVER_PATH`. The honest value is not a better string, it is *"resolved from an env key"*.
  - **mlx ×2, speech sidecar** — `"mlx_lm.server"`, `"speech-sidecar"`. ADR-034 runs mlx from a uv venv, not a bare command on `PATH`; the sidecar has no implementation at all (D7).
- **[architect]** **Call: `ChildLaunch` carries an ordered candidate list, not a path** — `[ExecutableSource]` with `.environmentVariable` / `.homeRelativePath` / `.absolutePath`. This is not new design, it is **the shape dmonium already uses** (`NetworkManager.networkCandidates`: override → `DMON_NETWORK_PATH` → `~/.dotnet/tools/ndmon`) and the shape task 4.7 asks for verbatim (*"the way dmonium's `NetworkManager` does … overridable"*). It also lets a child with no decided executable say so — an **empty** candidate list — instead of naming one that does not exist. The `~` problem disappears with it: `.homeRelativePath(".dotnet/tools/ndmon")` makes expansion 4.7's mechanical job rather than string surgery over a value that looks already-resolved.
  - Weighed against the cheaper fix of just correcting the strings: there are no correct strings to write for four of them. A model that forces every descriptor to name an executable will get fiction wherever the truth is "not decided", and fiction in a static inventory is indistinguishable from fact at the call site.
- **[architect]** Carried into **B2 (4.3)**'s brief, all three from the reviewer: (1) `.process` health checks must be **run-to-completion-and-inspect-exit**, never a persistent spawn target — otherwise a monitor gains a launch path through the back door and architect call 2 has a hole in it; (2) 4.3 needs a **narrow** shared surface over both descriptor types (`id`, `healthCheck`, `timeout`) that does not smuggle `launch` back in; (3) the dmonium **PID-file vs endpoint-health** adoption divergence wants its explanatory comment where adoption is implemented — block B3 (4.2) — not here.

Verified the candidate values against the precedent line by line — gateway against `NetworkManager.swift:79-86` (correctly omitting the settings-override tier, which does not exist yet), Dcal/Dmail against `ServiceManager.swift:99-103,111-137` (`override` is always `nil` in both factories, so the env var genuinely is the only source). Grepped `home/` for `startsInThisChange`: zero hits.
  - **Reframed the test split, and the reframing is the useful part:** the *old* universal assertion — every child must declare a non-empty launch path — **was itself the root cause of the blocker.** It forced fabricating unusable paths for five children purely to stay green. So the split is a correction, not a loosening: a test demanding something untrue will be satisfied with fiction.
  - **Nit taken:** `theEnabledChildDeclaresLaunchCandidates` hardcoded `networkGateway`, so the invariant the whole round was about — *an enabled child must have real launch candidates* — was not actually guarded; flipping `dcal.isEnabled` later would not have failed anything. Now iterates `children.filter(\.isEnabled)`, with a non-vacuity guard so it cannot silently guard nothing.
- **[architect]** **Block B1 (4.1) closed.** Gates green — `make dmon-home` 0 warnings, `make dmon-home-test` 41/41, `make build` clean, `openspec validate --strict` valid. Box 4.1 ticked. Two rounds: one blocker (mine, found by checking the precedent rather than the diff), four nits.
- **[architect]** **Lesson worth carrying past this change, because it is not about this model at all:** the reviewer's first pass audited the diff thoroughly and cleanly — every endpoint default verified against its cited source — and still passed five fabricated executables, *because the diff was internally consistent and its test suite was green*. The fiction was invisible from inside: the test asserted every child declares a launch path, so five children were given one. **What caught it was reading the precedent the descriptor claimed to mirror** (`ServiceManager.candidates`) and finding it had no default path at all. A diff-local reviewer structurally cannot do that — which is the clearest illustration so far of why the reviewer and supervisor roles are not the same lens at different widths.

- **[architect]** **B2 (4.3) briefed.** Three calls, one of which is the whole point of the block:
  1. **The timeout is enforced *around* the probe seam, never inside the conformer.** dmonium bounds its probe with `request.timeoutInterval = 5` — i.e. the conformer polices itself. That cannot satisfy the spec, whose scenario is *"a check that does not complete within its timeout"* for **any** check: a conformer that hangs, or simply ignores its own timeout, defeats a self-policed bound and the scenario becomes untestable — you cannot write the failing case without a probe that misbehaves. Race the seam against the deadline in the supervisor and the guarantee holds regardless of what the conformer does.
  2. **An unimplemented check kind is `.unknown`, never `.healthy` and never `.unhealthy`.** `.tcp` and `.process` execution may be deferred (only `.http` is needed before 4.7), but a deferred kind must not report a health it did not observe. `.unhealthy` would be just as wrong as `.healthy` — it asserts an observation that was never made, and Tailscale would sit red for a reason that is not about Tailscale.
  3. **The publication seam lands in `Supervisor`, not the app target** — per the section-3 supervisor's tripwire. The spec scenario ("the host's UI reflects that child's new health state") has a package-testable half (the store publishes a change) and a shell half (SwiftUI renders it). Only the second belongs in `App/`, and it belongs in section 5.
  - Concurrency is where this block can go quietly wrong, so the brief names it: checks run **concurrently** — the scenario says a hung check must not block *other children* — with per-child deadlines, no leaked tasks on timeout, and no `DispatchSemaphore` bridging.

- **[worker]** B2 (4.3) done. `HealthCheckable` (the narrow `id`/`healthCheck`/`healthCheckTimeout` surface over both descriptor types), `TimeoutRace.withTimeout`, `HealthChecker`, `ChildHealthStore` (an `actor` publishing an `AsyncStream` of snapshots) and `HealthMonitor` (per-entity fan-out plus an injectable-interval loop). `.tcp`/`.process` deferred to `.unknown` as directed.
- **[architect]** **Blocker found by reading the seam before the audit: `HealthChecker` documented a guarantee the code cannot provide** — *"a hung or **cancellation-ignoring** probe still yields a result rather than blocking forever."* `withTaskGroup` cannot return until every child has actually finished; `cancelAll()` only trips the loser's cancellation flag. A probe that never checks it blocks `withTimeout` forever — the exact failure the bound exists to prevent. Handed to the reviewer as a claim to **confirm or refute** rather than as a verdict, since the semantics are subtle enough to be worth a second read.
- **[reviewer]** **Request changes.** Confirmed the reading and committed to a position: the **code is right and only the comment overclaims.** Under the block's own "no leaked tasks" constraint the sole way to buy an unconditional bound is a detached `Task` never awaited — which is that leak — so bounded-for-cooperatively-cancellable is the correct trade-off, and `Task.sleep` and `URLSession` are both cooperative. It also sharpened *why* this is blocker-grade: `.tcp`/`.process` are deferred to a later block, and their author would read this comment and conclude the race protects a raw socket read or a `Process.waitUntilExit()` with no cancellation hook. **`TimeoutRace.swift`'s own comment already stated the true, narrower guarantee** — the two files disagreed and `HealthChecker` was the one wrong.
  - **Second blocker, which I had missed: `aHungCheckPublishesFailureWithoutBlockingOtherChildren` could not fail for the reason it claimed.** Both "healthy" probes returned *immediately*, so a serial `for` loop also finishes inside the asserted 2s bound. The fan-out is genuinely concurrent (verified at `checkOnce`), so nothing was broken — but a regression to serial would not have been caught.
- **[worker]** Both fixed. The concurrency test now gives the healthy probes **real 150ms latency** against a 300ms bound (serial floor 350ms), and the worker **falsified it rather than asserting it**: reverted `checkOnce` to a serial loop, measured **366ms > 300ms**, restored the concurrent implementation, reran green.
- **[reviewer]** Re-audit: both fixes correct, margins independently re-derived. **But a fourth instance of the same defect class, inside the fix for the third** — the new cancellation test's comment claimed its `withTimeout(2)` wrapper guarded against `run()` regressing to non-cancellable. It cannot, for *precisely* the blocker-1 reason: `Task.value` is not a cancellation checkpoint, so under that regression the test **hangs** rather than failing at 2s.
- **[architect]** Directed the wrapper **removed rather than reworded.** It bounds only a slow-to-notice cancellation — a failure mode nobody here is worried about — and against the regression it names it hangs either way, so it buys no protection while reading as though it does. **Machinery that looks like a guard is worse than no guard: the next reader trusts it.** A plain `await task.value` plus a comment stating the real dependency is shorter and true.
- **[architect]** **Block B2 (4.3) closed.** Gates green — 54 tests in 0.161s, build 0 warnings, `make build` 0/0, validate valid. Box 4.3 ticked. Three rounds; three blockers, all of one kind.
- **[architect]** **The pattern of this section, and the thing the supervisor should weigh: four defects across two blocks, every one a claim its own tests were structurally unable to falsify.** Fabricated launch paths passed a test asserting every child declares one; a cancellation guarantee passed tests that only ever inject `Task.sleep`; a concurrency claim passed a threshold serial execution also satisfies; a cancellation guard passed because the regression it names would hang rather than fail. **None was caught by reading the diff — each was caught by asking what the test would do if the claim were false.** Worth carrying into the remaining briefs as a standing question rather than a lesson already learned: it recurred *inside the remediation for itself*, which suggests it is a property of the domain (structured concurrency, static inventories) rather than of any one worker.

- **[architect]** **B3 (4.2 + 4.6-mechanism) briefed. Four calls, and one scope split I want on the record.**
  1. **`posix_spawn`, not Foundation `Process`** — and the reason is that the two requirements collide. Section 5.1 needs **stdout/stderr pipes**, which `Process` gives free; 4.6 needs **its own process group**, which `Process` cannot do at all: there is no hook between fork and exec, `setpgid` from the parent races the exec (and fails `EACCES` once it has happened), and macOS ships no `setsid(1)`. `posix_spawn` does both — `POSIX_SPAWN_SETPGROUP` with pgroup 0, plus `adddup2` file actions.
  2. **The consequence is child reaping, and it lands on 4.4.** `Process` reaps for you and hands you `terminationHandler`; `posix_spawn` does not, so B3 owns `waitpid`/`DispatchSource` — and *that* is what 4.4's crash detection will be built on. Naming it now because it is the part of this choice that bites a later block rather than this one.
  3. **An adopted child must be structurally unkillable, not merely un-killed.** Same instinct as B1's monitors: rather than a `wasAdopted` flag every kill site must remember to check, only a **spawned** handle carries the pgid — so there is nothing to signal an adopted child *through*. The spec's "Adoption is exempt" scenario then holds by construction.
  4. **Adoption is by endpoint health check, not dmonium's PID file.** B1's reviewer parked the explanatory comment for this block; it belongs where the divergence is actually implemented.
  - **Named as a genuine footgun, because the failure is severe and silent-looking:** if `posix_spawnattr_setpgroup` is not applied, the child inherits *our* process group — and `kill(-pgid, SIGKILL)` then kills the test runner, and possibly the terminal that launched it. The brief requires asserting the child's pgid differs from our own **before** any group signal, in both the code and the tests.
- **[architect]** **Scope split: B3 ticks 4.2 only; 4.6 ticks at B5.** 4.6 says *"kill that group on **host exit**"*, and at B3 nothing in the app constructs a supervisor to hang that off — 4.7 is where the app first starts a child. Constructing one now purely to tear it down is dead scaffolding (design D3's own argument). So B3 builds and package-tests the mechanism, and B5 wires it to app termination and ticks the box. **The alternative — ticking 4.6 on a mechanism nothing calls — is exactly the "green but unfalsifiable" pattern this section has produced four times already.**

- **[worker]** B3 done. `ChildSpawner` (`posix_spawn` + `POSIX_SPAWN_SETPGROUP` + `adddup2` pipes), `ChildStartCoordinator` (the adopt-or-spawn decision over B2's `HealthChecker`), `ExecutableResolver`, `SpawnedChild`/`ChildExitStatus`, `SpawnError`, `ChildStartOutcome`, `ProcessExit.awaitExit`. **Did not take my `posix_spawn` claim on trust** — spiked it standalone, confirmed `getpgid(child) == child && != getpgrp()`, and turned up a fact I did not know: `WIFEXITED`/`WEXITSTATUS` are **function-like macros Clang will not import into Swift**, so the hand-rolled bit-math is necessary rather than stylistic.
- **[worker]** **Adopted the falsification discipline unprompted, on three separate claims** — commented out `POSIX_SPAWN_SETPGROUP` (all spawner tests failed), signalled the bare pid instead of the group (the grandchild test failed with "grandchild survived"), forced the coordinator never to adopt (the adoption test failed) — reverting each. This is the response the section's four-defect pattern was asking for.
- **[architect]** **Blocker found by reading the guard, not the tests: `precondition` was the wrong instrument at both process-group guard sites.** The double-guard *instinct* is right — a signal is irreversible, so re-checking where it fires is sound — but the trap fails differently at each site, and one of them is a genuine defect rather than a style question. Put to the reviewer with my position stated, to confirm or refute.
- **[reviewer]** **Request changes**, agreeing on both and sharpening the fix.
  1. **Spawn site:** if `POSIX_SPAWN_SETPGROUP` silently failed, the child is **already alive in our group**, and a trap does not touch it — it kills the host and leaves the child orphaned under launchd, in a group that no longer means anything. *That is exactly the "orphaned mlx process holding unified memory" failure D6 exists to prevent, manufactured by the guard meant to prevent it.* Recovery: `kill(pid, …)` is unconditionally safe (one pid, never a group), so kill, **reap**, throw.
  2. **`killProcessGroups` loop:** a trap on child 1 means children 2…n are never signalled — a guard against orphans producing a pile of them, at app-exit, when nothing further runs. A crash during quit also reads to a user as a crash-on-quit bug.
  3. **The structural point, which is the durable part:** do not fix these as two ad-hoc conditionals. Express the invariant **once**, non-trapping, and let each site own its recovery — otherwise the two guards drift apart later.
  - Both also trap in **release** (`precondition` is not `assert`), which is why they were blockers rather than nits.
- **[worker]** Fixed as specified, and **re-ran the falsification against the new failure mode** rather than assuming the old experiment still applied: no crash, every spawner test failing cleanly with `childInheritedOurProcessGroup`, and the misgrouped pid verified actually dead after the throw.
- **[reviewer]** **Approve.** Verified the shared predicate is genuinely shared with no inline re-expression; that no path throws between the kill and the reap; that all four `defer`s are function-scope and so cover the new throw; that typed throws reaches `ChildStartCoordinator` with no lossy conversion.
- **[architect]** **Two closers on top of the Approve, which interact — and I declined the reviewer's suggested test.** `SpawnedChild.init` is **public**, so `@discardableResult` on the singular `killProcessGroup` was defending a hazard that still existed: anyone can construct a child with an arbitrary pgid and silently discard a refusal. Dropped the annotation (rather than restricting the initialiser, which would have forced `@testable` on the tests).
  - **The declined test is the part worth keeping.** The reviewer proposed a mixed-array test: a deliberately-colliding `SpawnedChild` alongside a real one, asserting only the collider is refused. Consider its failure mode — if the guard is ever inverted, it does not fail, it `SIGKILL`s **the test runner's own process group**, and plausibly the terminal. **That is the one test whose failure mode is worse than the bug it guards.** Replaced with the safe instrument: `wouldSignalOurOwnGroup` made `internal` and asserted directly (`true` for `getpgrp()`, `false` otherwise), sending no signal at all — so an inverted guard fails as an assertion rather than as a dead runner. It is also *better* coverage, since that predicate is the single thing both recovery paths depend on.
  - Accepted the reviewer's reasoning that `killProcessGroups`' continue-past-refusal property no longer needs a dynamic test: with no `precondition` left in the file, early exit is a five-line control-flow fact. It needed dynamic proof only while a trap could abort the loop.
- **[architect]** **Block B3 closed. 4.2 ticked; 4.6 deliberately left unticked** — its box belongs to B5, where the shutdown is wired to app termination. Gates green: 76 tests in 0.161s, 0 warnings, `make build` 0/0, validate valid. Three rounds.

- **[architect]** **B4 (4.4 + 4.5) briefed. The headline call comes from reading the precedent, which is where B1's blocker also came from.**
  - **dmonium's backoff does not actually back off, and 4.4 must not inherit it.** `ServerProcessManager.start()` line 90 sets `currentBackoff = 2` after **every** successful start, while `handleTermination` doubles it on each crash. So for the failure that matters — a child that **launches fine and then dies immediately** — the sequence is: start (reset to 2) → crash → wait 2 → start (reset to 2) → crash → wait 2 → … **forever, at 2 seconds, never escalating.** The doubling only ever escalates when `start()` itself fails to launch, which is the *less* likely crash-loop. Reset must key on the child having **stayed up**, not on a launch having succeeded.
  - **The two tasks are one block precisely because they interact, and the interaction is a classic bug:** graceful shutdown (4.5) terminates children, and crash detection (4.4) sees a terminated child. Without an explicit intentional-stop path, **quitting the app restarts everything it just shut down.** dmonium carries an `intentionalStop` flag for exactly this. A test must prove shutdown does not trigger a restart.
  - **Restart goes back through `ChildStartCoordinator`, not straight to `spawn`.** Reattach-first is not a startup-only property (D6): if something else has taken over the endpoint by the time we retry, adopting it is the correct behaviour, and calling `spawn` directly would produce the second process adoption exists to prevent.
  - **Backoff is asserted as a *sequence of requested delays*, never as elapsed time.** A test that sleeps real backoff intervals is both slow and — per this section's four-defect pattern — passable by an incorrect sequence under a loose bound. Inject the sleep; record what was asked for.
  - **`awaitExit`'s "call once per pid" contract gets enforced structurally here** — one supervision task owning each spawned pid, not free-standing calls — since 4.4 is what makes double-reap a live failure mode rather than a documented caveat.

- **[worker]** B4 done. `RestartBackoff`, `ChildSupervisionState`, `ChildSupervisionStore`, `HostSupervisor`, plus `ProcessExitTests` (carried from B3). **Confirmed the dmonium backoff bug at the code and did not inherit it** — reset is keyed on uptime past a stability threshold, and the crash-loop case escalates `[2,4,8,16,32,60,60]` in a test that asserts the exact sequence.
- **[worker]** **Found a real production defect in B3's already-committed `ChildSpawner`: spawned children inherited the parent's blocked signal mask.** `swift test`'s runner blocks `SIGTERM`, so every spawned child inherited that block — `killProcessGroup(signal: SIGTERM)` would report success while delivering nothing, **silently breaking the whole of 4.5**. Fixed with `POSIX_SPAWN_SETSIGDEF | POSIX_SPAWN_SETSIGMASK`. The reviewer confirmed it is a genuine production defect rather than a test artifact, that the fix is complete, and that it belongs here rather than being scope creep — 4.5 is unimplementable without it.
- **[reviewer]** **Request changes — and it built a repro rather than reasoning.** `shutdown()` **deadlocked**: it wrapped `await task.value` in `withTimeout` and sent `SIGKILL` only after `withTimeout` returned, but a task group cannot return until every child finishes, and neither `Task.value` nor `awaitExit`'s `DispatchSource` continuation is a cancellation checkpoint. With a child whose whole group ignores `SIGTERM`, `shutdown()` did not return in 20s and the process needed `kill -9`. Compounding: `shutdown()` iterates sequentially, so **one stuck child stops every earlier-starting child from being asked to terminate at all** — defeating D6's own rationale.
  - **Why the escalation test could not see it, which is the instructive part:** the fixture was `trap '' TERM; sleep 30 & wait`. `kill(-pgid, SIGTERM)` reaches **every** group member, including the backgrounded `sleep 30`, which has no trap and dies on the default disposition — unblocking `wait` so the script exits **on its own** in 0.001s. The test claimed the escalation half and exercised the graceful half. The `SIGKILL` branch never ran.
  - Second blocker: the ordering test used two children whose insertion order already matched ascending `startupOrder`, so deleting the `.sorted` would not have failed it.
- **[architect]** Directed the fix at the **seam, not the call site**: `awaitExit` becomes genuinely cancellation-aware, because `withTimeout` "bounding" a structurally unboundable operation is the same fiction B2's comment fix removed. Required the rewritten test to **fail rather than hang** when escalation is removed — a hang is not evidence.
- **[worker]** Fixed, and found the missing piece: **`cancelAll()` only cancels the group's own wrapper, never an externally-referenced `Task`** — so `shutdownChild` also needs `withTaskCancellationHandler(onCancel: { task.cancel() })`. `awaitExit` now arbitrates via an `OSAllocatedUnfairLock`-backed `ExitWaitBox`. **Two further real bugs found while fixing:** `.repeatedFailure` was instantly overwritten by `.normal` on the next spawn, so it could never be observed; and the `waitUntilTrue` helpers in **both** test files swallowed `Task.sleep` cancellation with no `isCancelled` check, so a false condition polls forever regardless of its stated timeout — **the section's signature defect living in the instruments used to verify everything else.**
- **[reviewer]** **Third blocker, and the purest instance of the pattern yet — found by printing the value instead of trusting the test.** `reap()` ignored `waitpid`'s return, so a second `awaitExit` on an already-reaped pid returned **`exitCode: 0`, fabricated** (on `ECHILD` the untouched `status` of `0` reads as "exited normally"). And the test written specifically to prove that scenario safe did `_ = ...`, discarding the value — proving "doesn't hang" and nothing about honesty. **A test that structurally cannot observe what is wrong with the code it guards.** Plus: the post-`SIGKILL` reap was itself now cancellable with no fallback — *a new exposure created by the previous round's own fix* — and `.repeatedFailure` still flickered to `.normal` on every restart attempt.
- **[worker]** All three fixed. Replaced `ChildExitStatus?` with **`AwaitExitOutcome`** (`.exited` / `.cancelled` / `.reapFailed(errno:)`) rather than overloading `nil` a third time; shielded the final reap in an unstructured `Task`; gated `.normal` on `consecutiveUnstableCrashes == 0`. Each falsified.
- **[reviewer]** **Approve.** Re-ran every falsification itself: the shield test passes live (confirming **both** halves — unstructured tasks do not inherit cancellation, *and* awaiting `.value` on a `Task<_, Never>` is not a cancellation checkpoint); `errno` is read in the expression immediately after the failing `waitpid` with nothing intervening; the `AwaitExitOutcome` ripple has **no inversion** at any call site. Rated the flicker test a **structural invariant rather than a race** — with an always-crashing fixture the counter is monotonically non-decreasing, so the property holds regardless of scheduler load.
- **[architect]** Two non-gating notes taken anyway, both one-liners on ground B5 builds on: `.reapFailed` at the shutdown site was **discarded while its sibling failure three lines up published** — inconsistent with the requirement set's own "surface failure" principle; and the flicker test spent its full poll window, accounting for essentially the entire suite runtime. Now breaks out after 20 consecutive confirming samples: **0.603s → 0.088s**, suite back to 0.325s. Kept the honest `Issue.record` for the window-elapsed case.
- **[architect]** **Block B4 closed.** 4.4 and 4.5 ticked; **4.6 still deliberately unticked** for B5. Gates green: 98 tests in 0.325s, 0 warnings, `make build` 0/0, validate valid. Four rounds — each found something real, which is why the loop ran; the reviewer was told explicitly to approve with notes rather than manufacture a fifth, and did.
- **[architect]** **Block B5 brief — 4.7 + ticking 4.6: "the app owns a supervisor."** Everything B5 needs already exists as parts; **nothing composes them**. `ChildInventory.networkGateway` is already the enabled child with dmonium's candidate order (`DMON_NETWORK_PATH`, then `~/.dotnet/tools/ndmon`), `HostSupervisor` already starts/restarts/shuts down, and `ChildSpawner.killProcessGroups` already exists — **with no caller anywhere in the tree.** No `HostSupervisor` is ever constructed outside tests, `start()` is never called, and `ContentView` renders a hardcoded `ChildHealth.unknown`. So B5 is composition and lifecycle, not new supervision mechanics.
  - **Three architect calls, all made to keep the app target a shell (design D4) and to respect the section-3 testability tripwire.**
    1. **The exit sweep is a `Supervisor` method, not app-target code.** Add `HostSupervisor.terminateSpawnedProcessGroups() -> [ChildID]`, which passes every child still holding a `SpawnedChild` to `killProcessGroups` and returns the refusals. This gives `killProcessGroups` its first real caller and makes the app-exit guarantee assertable by `swift test` — an app-target-only kill loop would be exactly the untestable growth D4 forbids.
    2. **Exit is graceful-then-sweep, in that order.** `shutdown()` first (reverse order, SIGTERM→SIGKILL per child, already built and reviewed), then the sweep as a **backstop** for anything still recorded as spawned. After a clean `shutdown()` the sweep finds nothing — `handleExit` clears `spawnedChild` — and that is the point: the sweep only earns its keep when shutdown was cut short. Do not reimplement graceful termination inside the sweep.
    3. **A `HostRuntime` composition root in the package.** It owns `HostSupervisor` + `HealthMonitor` + both stores over `ChildInventory`, and exposes `start()`, `shutdownForTermination()` and a merged snapshot feed. The app target gets only an `NSApplicationDelegateAdaptor` and a thin `@MainActor @Observable` mirror.
  - **The app-termination hook must be `applicationShouldTerminate`, not `applicationWillTerminate`.** The latter is synchronous and cannot await an actor; returning `.terminateLater`, awaiting `shutdownForTermination()` under an overall budget, then calling `NSApp.reply(toApplicationShouldTerminate: true)` is the only shape that lets a bounded async shutdown complete before the process goes away. A budget is mandatory — a hung child must not wedge quit forever.
  - **Refusals must be acknowledged, per B4's carry.** `killProcessGroups` returns `[ChildID]` and is deliberately not `@discardableResult`; the delegate must at minimum log a non-empty result. That `[ChildID]` remains sufficient only while `wouldSignalOurOwnGroup` is the sole refusal reason — if this block finds a second, the return type changes with it.
  - **Scope call — a minimal health surface lands here, the log pane does not.** `ContentView`'s placeholder becomes a real per-child row (id, `ChildHealth`, `ChildSupervisionState`) fed by the stores. Two reasons: the supervision spec's *"Health is visible"* scenario has no UI anywhere in the tree today, and **4.7's human verification is not performable without it** — the Product Owner has to see adopted-vs-spawned to check the thing being verified. Streaming stdout/stderr remains 5.1's.
  - **Scope boundaries:** no changes to `ChildInventory` descriptors (4.1 settled them), no new supervision policy, no gateway *client* work (sections 6–7), no log pane (5.1), no power assertion (5.2).
  - **4.7's box does not tick on green gates.** It carries the section's only human verification — a live `ndmon` coming up on `127.0.0.1:5500` and a relaunch adopting rather than double-spawning. Worker delivers the code and a copy-pasteable recipe; the Product Owner ticks it. 4.6 ticks on gates as normal.
- **[architect]** **Running count for the supervisor: seven defects across four blocks, all one shape — a claim its own tests could not falsify.** The last three are the sharpest: an escalation test that exercised the opposite branch; a "does not hang" test that discarded the fabricated value it should have caught; and cancellation-swallowing poll helpers, i.e. **the defect inside the instruments used to verify the other defects**. Two were introduced *by remediations for earlier instances of the same shape*. The response that works is not more review passes but a specific question — *what would this test do if the claim were false?* — and the B3/B4 workers now run revert-and-confirm experiments unprompted.

- **[supervisor]** **Request changes — four blockers, and the first is a production defect on the app's real quit path.**
  1. **Requirement 5 is unimplemented on the path the app actually takes.** `shutdownChild` sends `SIGTERM` to the *group* but then waits on the **leader's pid only**; the group `SIGKILL` at `HostSupervisor.swift:317` lives inside `if !exitedGracefully`. So when the leader exits promptly and a group member does not, no group kill ever fires — and `handleExit` has already cleared `spawnedChild`, removing that child from `terminateSpawnedProcessGroups()`'s input set, so the backstop cannot reach it either. **Both mechanisms miss the same case, for different reasons.** Production shape: ADR-037 has `Dmon.Network` doing per-session core spawning (`CoreProcessManager.cs:67`), so `dmoncore` children inherit `ndmon`'s group; on quit `ndmon` — with less to flush — can exit first, the host declares graceful success, and any `dmoncore` still writing `messages.jsonl` is orphaned to launchd. **Verified by the architect against the source before accepting.**
  2. **The accepted coverage gap and blocker 1 are one defect seen from two ends.** `terminateSpawnedProcessGroups()` backstops a state the app *as composed cannot produce* — `AppDelegate` deliberately never cancels the shutdown task, so the sweep's triggering condition is unreachable in production. The reviewer's race analysis was correct **given the composition**; the conclusion drawn from it was wrong. The untestable thing was not a race but **a path with no caller**. Consequence: the only test of the app's exit guarantee passes against a sweep stubbed to `return []`.
  3. **Four monitors polled forever, results consumed by nothing** — including a 30-second HTTPS request to `generativelanguage.googleapis.com`. Each half was locally right (`HostRuntime` folds monitors into the health loop; it excludes them from the status feed because a monitor has no meaningful `ChildSupervisionState`); **the combination was never decided.** `calendar-sync`, `mail` and `egress` appear nowhere in section 4 or `## NEXT` — Tailscale is the only monitor named anywhere in the section, and it declares `.process`, which `HealthChecker` returns `.unknown` for without executing. Permanently inert.
  4. **`ChildHealth.starting`, `.stopped` and `isRunning` are unreachable** — no publisher, and `isRunning` has no production reader at all.

- **[supervisor]** **On the generalisation — the human-facing rule is *stronger* than the test rule, and the reason is asymmetry.** For a test the observer is the runner; for a recipe it is a human. A person told to "confirm it shows `starting`" who sees nothing **assumes they mistimed the check**: absence reads as user error, not as system falsity. A failing test shouts; a failing recipe step is silently absorbed by the person running it. Two-part rule: every step names the falsifying observation *and* what it looks like when the claim is false; and **before a recipe ships, trace the publisher of every state it asks a human to look for** — mechanical, and exactly what would have caught instance eight. Belongs in standing guidance beyond this change, because **the recipe is the one artifact in the loop with no automated auditor**. Also: three of nine defects were introduced by remediations for earlier instances, which argues a remediation block should be audited against **the original claim**, not only its own diff.

Wire a `SIGTERM` handler with its own cancel-and-timeout, or change the `.terminateLater` strategy, and a child's process group can survive host exit again **with no compiler or test signal** — because the mechanism that used to catch that regression class is precisely the one this precondition justified deleting. - **[supervisor]** **Corrected my risk framing, and this is the substantive finding of the re-review.** I documented the deletion's precondition as "cancelling the shutdown task re-opens the hole". **It does not.** Traced with the enclosing task already cancelled: `shutdown()`'s `for` loop is not a cancellation checkpoint so it completes; the synchronous `SIGTERM` runs; inside `withTimeout` the sleep throws → `nil` → escalation branch group `SIGKILL`, *or* the operation returns `true` → graceful branch group `SIGKILL`. **Both outcomes signal the group**, so cancellation degrades to *kill everything fast*, not *skip the kill* — a direct consequence of R1 putting the kill on both branches, which means the deleted sweep's job is genuinely gone rather than merely unlikely to be needed. The real residual exposure is **aborting the loop part-way** (someone adding `try Task.checkCancellation()` inside it), which the old sweep would have caught because it iterated `states.values` rather than following the loop. Ruled: **not blocking, comment sufficient to ship** — consistent with its own refusal of a speculative seam for the termination budget — but the structural fix is one line and belongs in `## NEXT`. **`AppDelegate`'s comment rewritten to the corrected scoping**; the version I wrote would have sent the next reader chasing the wrong thing.

## 5. Log pane and power assertion

- **[architect]** Base: `a12f923` — every spawned child's stdout and stderr streamed into the app, attributed by child and retained across a restart, plus a `ProcessInfo` activity assertion held while the gateway is enabled.
- **[architect]** **Block carve — two blocks, in `tasks.md` order, because they genuinely share nothing.** B1 = 5.1 (log pane), B2 = 5.2 (power assertion). No dependency runs either way: 5.1 lands in `Supervisor` + the app's view layer, 5.2 in `Power` + two lines of app-lifecycle wiring. Cut in numeric order only because 5.1 is the larger one and the one that closes a live defect (below).
- **[architect]** **5.1 is not additive — it closes a latent deadlock that exists today.** `ChildSpawner.spawn` already wires stdout and stderr to `Pipe()`s (section 4, B3) and `SpawnedChild` already carries both read ends, but **nothing reads them.** A spawned child that writes more than a pipe buffer's worth (~64 KB) blocks in `write(2)` forever, and `ndmon` is a logging .NET host. The section-4 comments call the pipes "section 5.1 needs"; they are also section 5.1's *bug*. The brief asks for a test that proves draining, not just a test that proves attribution.
- **[architect]** **Three calls made in B1's brief rather than left to the worker.**
  1. **Buffering lives in `Supervisor`, only the SwiftUI view in the app target** — the section-4 supervisor's testability ruling, verbatim: the spec asserts *"the log pane retains the output preceding the crash"*, and that assertion must be evaluable by `swift test`.
  2. **Adopted children have no pipes and never will** — the host did not launch them, so their stdout belongs to whoever did. The pane must say so once, attributed, rather than render an empty section that reads as *"this child is silent"*. This is the Product Owner's adopted-vs-spawned discriminator arriving where it is load-bearing rather than decorative.
  3. **The buffer is bounded and the bound must be visible.** A per-child ring buffer that silently drops the oldest lines is indistinguishable, at the pane, from a child that never logged them. Dropped lines get a count.
- **[architect]** **Decision owed by this section, and my call: `ChildHealth.unhealthy` stays collapsed.** `## NEXT` parked *"timed out" vs "responded but refused"* here because the log pane was expected to be the first consumer with a reason to tell them apart. It is not one: the log pane carries **the child's own output**, and a health-check result is an observation *the host* makes about a child, not something the child said. Nothing in 5.1 reads `ChildHealth` for anything but a row label. Section 4's rule applies unchanged — **observation states earn no "built for the full inventory" shelter**, because a new state needs a new writer. Revisit at 8.3, which is the first task that genuinely asks the UI to explain a failure rather than name it.
- **[architect]** **Where 5.2's policy lives, decided ahead of the block because design D3 forbids the obvious answer.** D3 keeps `Power` a separate package with the explicit rationale that *"Supervisor should not grow a power-policy responsibility"* — so `HostRuntime` holding the assertion is out, however convenient. The shape instead: the **policy** (assertion held ⇄ gateway enabled, plus the options and reason string) lives in `Power`, driven by a plain `Bool`, with no dependency on `Supervisor`; the **fact** ("is an enabled gateway being supervised") is `Supervisor`'s and already derivable from the descriptor set; the **app target holds only the join** — a forward at launch and at terminate, no policy value of its own. Both halves of the spec scenario stay package-testable, which is what the section-4 tripwire actually demands; the two-line wire in the shell is the same category as `AppDelegate`'s existing shutdown call.

- **[architect]** **B1 (5.1) briefed.** Store shape pointed at `ChildSupervisionStore` explicitly (actor, snapshot, `updates()` with `onTermination` unregistration) so a second publishing idiom does not appear in the same package; the observer pointed at `ChildStatusObserver`'s doc comment, because the window-reopen subscriber leak it records is exactly the trap a second `@Observable` mirror walks into. Hazards called out as the reviewer's targets: the pipe-drain deadlock and its falsification, **reader tasks must never be awaited by the shutdown path** (a grandchild holding the write end means EOF never arrives), fd lifetime across restart generations, no reading inside the actor, and no claim that stdout and stderr interleave in a defined order. `ChildHealth` and the whole health/supervision model declared off-limits.

- **[worker]** **B1 (5.1) done.** `ChildLogSource`/`ChildLogLine` and a `ChildLogStore` actor (1000 lines/child, `droppedCount`) in `Supervisor`, reader tasks wired into `HostSupervisor.apply`, `logStore` exposed on `HostRuntime`, and a `ChildLogObserver` + pane in the app target. 116/116 green. **Two implementations rejected on measured evidence before the shipped one:** `FileHandle.bytes.lines` stalled mid-stream on a >64 KB write, and its replacement — a *blocking* synchronous read inside `Task.detached` — starved the cooperative thread pool, visible only when the **full** suite ran (unrelated tests stalling ~30s apiece), never under `--filter`. Shipped `DispatchIO.read` bridged through `withCheckedContinuation`. Falsified the drain test by disabling reader-task creation and watching both assertions fail. Also reported a heap-corruption crash bisected to a `[Task<Void, Never>]` field in `ChildState`, diagnosed as a Swift 6.3.3 toolchain issue and worked around with two named optional fields.
- **[architect]** **Tested two hypotheses about the new read primitive myself while the review ran, and one of them is the block's blocker.**
  - **Disproved, and worth recording because it would have been invisible:** I suspected `DispatchIO.read(fromFileDescriptor:maxLength:)` might withhold data until `maxLength` accumulated, which would mean a long-lived child like `ndmon` logging a few lines shows **nothing** in the pane until it exits — a spec failure every test in the block would still pass, because they all end at EOF. Measured against a still-running child: first line delivered in 0.00 s. The primitive is fine.
  - **Confirmed — `readChunk` is not cancellable.** Against a pipe whose write end is held by a live process that never writes: `convenience DispatchIO.read + bare withCheckedContinuation — ended within 2s of cancel: false`; `DispatchIO channel + withTaskCancellationHandler + close(flags: .stop) — ended within 2s of cancel: true`. `withCheckedContinuation` is not a cancellation checkpoint, and `drainOutput`'s `while !Task.isCancelled` only runs *between* reads.
- **[reviewer]** **Request changes — one blocker, found independently with its own repro**, and it is the same one: cancellation is inert in **exactly the case `cancelLogReaders` and both new `shutdownChild` comment blocks cite as its reason to exist** (a grandchild re-parented outside the group, so no EOF ever arrives). The reader task and its fd leak with it, because `defer { handle.closeFile() }` sits in a function body that never returns. Everything else verified rather than reasoned: reproduced the drain-test falsification itself (disabled `startLogReaders`, both assertions failed, restored byte-identically, 116/116); confirmed `dispatch_read`'s once-per-call handler contract against Apple's documentation; grepped the diff clean of `@unchecked Sendable` / `nonisolated(unsafe)` / `assumeIsolated` / `@preconcurrency`; confirmed the observer cannot reintroduce `ChildStatusObserver`'s window-reopen subscriber leak; confirmed the cap is per-`ChildID` and `droppedCount` accumulates rather than being computed once; and found **no tautological tests** — the shape section 4 shipped eight of.
  - **On the toolchain claim, which the brief singled out as the least-established thing in the block:** no active overlapping-access defect found, and the `states[id]` read-await-writeback pattern **predates this block**, so the shipped two-field shape is correct on its own merits. But the comment asserts as confirmed fact that an inert, never-written field corrupted the heap. Neither of us could establish that.
  - **Architectural note, raised for the Architect rather than blocked on:** `handleExit` nils both reader fields without cancelling, so an orphaned generation becomes unreachable — a sustained crash-restart loop with a lingering grandchild accumulates a task and two fds per crash, bounded by nothing short of app shutdown.
- **[architect]** **Fix round briefed — four items, and one reviewer suggestion overruled.**
  1. **Fix the cancellation, do not just correct the comments.** Ruled **against** the reviewer's `ExitWaitBox`-style alternative (resume the continuation early from `onCancel` behind a settled flag): it settles the continuation but leaves GCD's read outstanding on the fd, so **the leak survives the fix**, and closing the handle underneath a pending read is a double-close. The channel shape is the one I measured working. Named the fd-ownership hazard explicitly with it — `Pipe`'s `FileHandle` closes on dealloc, so a channel `cleanupHandler` that also closes is a double-close onto a possibly-recycled fd number.
  2. **Folded the reviewer's architectural note into the same round rather than parking it.** The worker's reason for not cancelling at exit-detection is sound and stands — cancelling the instant an exit is *detected* really does race genuine still-buffered trailing output. But with cancellation actually working, a **bounded drain grace** then cancel keeps the trailing output and turns an unbounded leak into a windowed one. The grace is a policy value, so it lives in the package, injectable.
  3. **Soften the toolchain comment to "suspected, not root-caused".** The failure mode of an overconfident one is symmetrical and both are expensive: a future reader spends a day chasing a toolchain bug that may not exist, or dismisses a real defect because the comment sounded settled.
  4. Surface partial data delivered alongside a read error instead of discarding it.
  - Both new tests must be falsified by actually reverting the fix, and the fd test must be deterministic or reported as not-achieved — **not tolerance-tuned into meaninglessness**.

- **[worker]** **Fix round delivered, and the blocker's root cause was worse than the shape.** `DispatchIO.read(length:)` resolves only on *length bytes or true EOF* — so a live child writing one short line then going quiet (literally the `outputSurvivesARestart` fixture's second generation) never resolved the read **at all**, cancellable or not. Rebuilding on `DispatchIO` channels fixed cancellation but not that stall. Dropped `DispatchIO` entirely for a bare `DispatchSourceRead` — fires on *any* readability — with a `ReadWaitBox` mirroring `ProcessExit.swift`'s `ExitWaitBox`. Fd ownership stated and correct: a read source only *watches* an fd, so `handle.closeFile()` in the `defer` is the sole closer. Drain grace added as an injectable `logDrainGrace` (2 s). Both new tests falsified — and falsifying the cancellation test exposed that `withTaskGroup` **cannot** bound a permanently-stuck task (it will not return until every child completes), so the bound was rebuilt as an unstructured race that does not wait on the loser. The fd test fails at exactly `initial+10` (2 fds × 5 generations) against its `initial+4` bound when the grace cancellation is removed.
- **[worker]** **Stopped on a red test rather than improvising: `repeatedImmediateCrashesRequestAnEscalatingCappedDelaySequence` now fails deterministically (5/5).** Bisected to *merely creating two `Task.detached` instances* — reproduced with empty closures, so independent of any reader code — widening a timing window in `apply`'s `.spawned` case enough for a **reentrant** `handleExit` to read `states[id]` before the outer call's writeback lands. Observed `[2, 2, 2, 4, 2, 8]` instead of `[2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 60]`. Assessed as pre-existing, requiring the exact invariant the brief declared off-limits, and asked for the Architect's call.
- **[architect]** **Call: fix it, in this block — and the defect is materially worse than the symptom that surfaced it.** Traced the lost update to its end: inside `apply`'s `.spawned` case the new child is recorded on the **local copy**, then `await store.publish(.normal,…)` releases the actor. A generation-2 crash in that window re-enters `handleExit`, which reads the stale entry and runs a **whole restart cycle to completion**, spawning generation 3 and storing its state — then generation 1's `handleExit` resumes and executes `states[id] = afterDelay`, overwriting generation 3 with a copy holding generation 2's **dead** `SpawnedChild`. Generation 3 is then live with no reference anywhere in the supervisor: `shutdownChild` reads the dead pid, returns early, and **generation 3 survives host exit and re-parents to launchd**. That is requirement 5 failing silently — **the orphaned-runtime failure design D6 exists to prevent** — and the corrupted delay sequence is the same lost update seen through its cheaper symptom (the reentrant call also reads a stale `startedAt`, so its stability verdict is computed against the wrong generation). **The instant `sleep` makes it deterministic; it does not make it possible.** In production the window is "a child that dies within a few actor hops of being spawned", which is exactly what a misconfigured `ndmon` does.

The defect class is one pattern: **a whole `ChildState` captured, carried across `await`s, and written back wholesale.** Delete the pattern (mutate `states[id]` in place, every mutation after the await preceding it) and the race has nowhere to live. Sweep scoped **by where the pattern is, not where the test pointed** — the section-3 lesson, applied to code.
  - **Test must assert the consequence, not the symptom:** the tracked child after a crash-restart storm must be the *newest* generation (`currentPID(for:)` matching the last pid actually spawned) and shutdown must signal *that* pid. A test that only re-checks the delay sequence passes against a fix that still loses the `SpawnedChild`. The existing sequence test is the canary and must go green untouched.

- **[reviewer]** **Request changes — the blocker is in the exact spot the brief sent it, and it was confirmed by repro rather than reasoning.** `posix_spawn` runs file actions strictly in order; the shipped order is `addopen(0, /dev/null)` → two `adddup2`s → four `addclose`s. Whenever the host's **own fd 0 is free** at `Pipe()` time the *read* end lands on 0, so the trailing `addclose(readEnd)` is an `addclose(0)` and **closes the `/dev/null` the earlier `addopen` just installed** — the child gets no fd 0 at all, which is precisely the empty-slot failure the wiring exists to prevent, reintroduced by the ordering.   - Everything else confirmed, including the two judgement calls the brief asked about: the kept `addclose`es are genuine defence-in-depth rather than divergent mechanisms (**file actions run in the forked child before `exec`; the flag only takes effect *at* `exec`** — for these four fds both produce an identical observable result), and `O_RDONLY` is the more precise choice than a shared read-write `/dev/null`. Endorsed the worker's own framing on both counts: "same class, not same instance" for the differing stall site, and "not proof" for a 1-in-5 timing sample.

So: `addopen` moves last **and** no `addclose` may ever name 0, 1 or 2. For those slots the close is **simultaneously unnecessary and harmful** — unnecessary because `adddup2`/`addopen` onto a slot already replaces what was there, so nothing leaks; harmful because it can destroy what the file actions deliberately installed.
