Confirmed the hoist permanently closes **the exact duplicate-default collision that caused the shared-`sleep`-double bug two rounds ago**, and that the storm-test comment is untouched (`git diff` empty on that file).
- **[reviewer]** **Investigation: the heap-corruption signature is a Swift Concurrency toolchain defect — evidenced, not asserted — and my own prime suspect was wrong.** I sent it after `drainOutput`'s `defer { handle.closeFile() }` racing the `DispatchSourceRead` on the same fd. **It audited that and found no such window**: `resumeFromReadability()` and `cancel()` both gate the only fd-touching call behind the same lock-protected `settled` check, so a late event-handler invocation returns before touching `read()`; and `defer` cannot run until `readChunk` has returned, which requires the continuation to have already resumed. Also cleared the `Data` slicing (`Data.SubSequence` is `Data`, real COW) and `ChildSpawner`'s `strdup`/`free`.
  - **What it did instead of auditing: it pulled the actual crash reports.** The fault is **not** libmalloc at all — it is `swift_task_dealloc`, Swift Concurrency's *own* stack-disciplined task-context allocator, which reuses malloc's wording when a context is freed out of its expected LIFO order. Backtrace: `swift_task_dealloc` → `handleExit(id:)` at `await sleep(delay)` → the `Task { [weak self] in … await self?.handleExit(id: id) }` closure in `apply`.
  - **Then it reproduced the *first* sighting the same way** — reintroducing the unpopulated `[Task<Void, Never>]` field with the constant already in its safe form, to isolate the variable — and got **the identical runtime function, fatal-error path, source statement and caller**. Two semantically disjoint triggers landing on the same line is the pattern a compiler-side context-size miscalculation produces, **not** the pattern a wild pointer write produces, which would surface wherever the shifted layout happened to reuse a block.
  - **It stated what would change its mind** — a trace naming an application `free`/`Data`/`FileHandle` call, or a crash site that *moved* between triggers — and found neither. **Negative result recorded honestly:** `--sanitize=address` runs clean, because ASan's instrumentation perturbs the layout past the trigger, so it is not a usable tool here; the plain debug build is what reproduces.
  - **Confidence level ruled deliberately:** both existing comments say **"suspected"**, and this finding *strengthens the evidence without upgrading the label* — no upstream Swift issue was matched, so "suspected" stays. Reproduction is now mechanical (`swift test --filter HostSupervisorTests`, deterministic 3–5/5 either way), so a future toolchain upgrade can re-test it in one command.

## 6. Gateway client — transport and frame codec

- **[architect]** Base: `981596b` — the Swift ADR-012 client's lower half: a transport abstraction with a WebSocket and an in-memory conformer, the `gw` control-frame codec, liveness, the wire-version check, and device-key authentication.
- **[architect]** **Product Owner decisions opening the section (2026-08-06), recorded as design D16.** Three, taken together because the first two are one question and the third was found while reading the wire for it.
  1. **`GatewayClient` is shaped as a shareable Swift client package** — the section-3 supervisor's *"decide before task 6.1/6.2"* is answered **"same code", not merely "same protocol"**. Extraction into a package the iOS client consumes is deferred (ADR-028 D5's `dmon-swift` question is that change's job), but nothing in this section may foreclose it: no dependency on `Supervisor`, `Power` or the app target; no macOS-only framework; and **host-only behaviour stays out** — device-key self-provisioning (D13) reads and writes `~/.dmon/network/devices.json`, which is co-located-only *by construction* and can never be shared with a phone.
  2. **Portability is enforced by a build, not by convention** — an iOS platform in `home/Package.swift` and a `make` target that builds `GatewayClient` for a generic iOS destination, picked up by section 10's CI job. Chosen over discipline-plus-review for this change's own recurring reason: *"extractable to iOS" written in a doc comment is a claim of readiness the system does not actually make*, and the first macOS-only API would be found by whoever eventually did the extraction. **The task that adds the gate must prove it bites** — introduce a macOS-only dependency, watch the build fail — not assume it does.
  3. **The module keeps the name `GatewayClient`.** Renaming to `DmonGatewayClient` for import-stability across extraction was offered and declined: it churns section 2's landed artifacts and a ticked task's wording for a cosmetic gain.
- **[architect]** **Task 6.4 had nothing to check against, and this was found by reading the wire rather than by writing the code.** The task and its requirement were written against a *negotiated* wire version. **There is no negotiation.** Evidence, all four legs checked before the finding was written down:
  - `protocolVersion` exists on exactly one frame — the core's ADR-003 `agentReady` event (`core/Dmon.Protocol/Events/LifecycleEvents.cs`).
  - `CoreLauncher.ReadAgentReadyAsync` **consumes** that event inside the gateway during the create handshake; `NetworkConnectionEndpoint.cs:506` constructs the `SessionHandler` *after* the launcher has eaten it, so it never reaches the forwarding loop, and the standing spec independently excludes handshake results from the seq stream.
  - `app.MapGet("/ws", …)` is the **only** endpoint the network host exposes. No header, no query parameter, no control frame carries a version — `AttachedFrame` has `generation` and `headSeq` and nothing else.
  - ADR-012 does not contain the word "version".
  So 6.4 as written could only ever have shipped a constant, a mismatch error type, unit tests, and **no live check** — a requirement met on paper and absent in fact. Put to the Product Owner because the fix is .NET work plus a standing-spec delta inside a Swift-shaped change; **decision: advertise it.** `attached` gains `wire`, sourced from `ProtocolVersion.Current`, landing as new task **6.6** which must precede 6.4. The iOS client gets the same check from the same field.
- **[architect]** **Carried into the section from the wire read, load-bearing for 6.2/6.3 and decisive for 7.3: the client cannot observe an event's `seq` — it must count.** The gateway forwards each core stdout line **byte-unchanged** (`INetworkConnection.SendAsync(string frame, …)` takes the raw JSONL line; `_seqLog` holds `(Seq, Line)` and sends only `Line`). The `seq` is gateway-local (ADR-014 D2: "never written to `messages.jsonl`", the core stays byte-for-byte unchanged), so the only sequence number the client is ever *told* is `headSeq` on `attached`. Everything after that is arithmetic: **`attached.headSeq` is the origin, and every subsequently received ADR-003 frame — replayed or live — increments it by exactly one.** This holds because the server's cursor is `clamp(lastSeq, 0, headSeq)` and its drain loop delivers strictly in `seq` order, one line per advance, including the re-added outstanding permission requests it re-seqs at attach. Two consequences for this section: **control frames must not be counted** (an `ack`, `ping` or `pong` between two events would desynchronise the count), which makes 6.2's routing the thing the whole resume story rests on; and **the counter belongs to the connection layer that owns the codec**, not to §7's session logic bolted on afterwards.
- **[architect]** **Block carve — seven blocks, cut small because six of them are contract-touching.** B1 = 6.1 (transport abstraction + both conformers + the iOS gate). B2 = 6.2 (frame codec, pure value types, no I/O). B3 = 6.3 (the connection actor where transport and codec meet, answering `ping` with `pong`). B4 = 6.6 (the .NET `wire` advertisement — a different codebase and a different review lens, so its own block and its own commit). B5 = 6.4 (the Swift-side check, which B4 unblocks). B6 = 6.5's portable half (credential, hex-SHA-256 with the pinned digest, bearer presentation, the unauthenticated loopback path). B7 = 6.5's co-located half (Keychain conformer and `devices.json` self-provisioning, outside `GatewayClient` per D16). 6.5 ticks at B7.

- **[architect]** **B1 (6.1) briefed — the transport abstraction, both conformers, and the iOS gate.** Four calls made in the brief rather than left to the worker.
  1. **The WebSocket conformer is an `actor`, not a struct with `@unchecked Sendable`.** `URLSessionWebSocketTask` is not `Sendable`, and D14/PRD §4.3 reserve `@unchecked Sendable` and `nonisolated(unsafe)` for the audio ring buffer alone. This is the *same trap that caught the first `Power` draft* — drafted with `NSLock` + `@unchecked Sendable`, rewritten as an `actor` before review. Named in the brief so it is not rediscovered.
  2. **Pull-based `receive()`, not a stream property.** It mirrors `URLSessionWebSocketTask.receive()` one-to-one, keeps the in-memory conformer trivial, and leaves the read loop's ownership with B3's connection actor — where the seq counter has to live anyway. A stream-shaped abstraction would put buffering below the abstraction, where the counter cannot see it.
  3. **Text frames only, and close codes are typed.** The gateway rejects binary outright (`4400 binary frames are not supported`) and uses application close codes deliberately — 4400 protocol violation, 4404 unknown session, 4409 fenced by a newer attach, 4500 core failure, 1009 message too big. A transport that collapses those into "connection closed" throws away the only material §7 has for an actionable error, so the code and reason are surfaced as a typed error at this level.
  4. **No live-network test, and no invented fake one.** The in-memory conformer is what tests drive; the WebSocket conformer stays thin enough that what is left untested is a `URLSession` call, not logic. Anything with a decision in it — endpoint/URL assembly, header assembly, close-code mapping — is pulled out and tested directly.
- **[architect]** **The gate must be proven, not asserted** — the brief requires the worker to add `import AppKit` to a `GatewayClient` file, watch `make dmon-home-ios-check` fail, and report the failure before reverting it. A portability gate that has never failed is indistinguishable from one that does not run.

The conformer's two pieces of real logic — close-code mapping and request/header assembly — factored into pure `static func`s so they are tested directly rather than through a socket. In-memory conformer in the test target.
  - **The gate bites, proven not asserted:** `import AppKit` added to `GatewayTransport.swift` produced `error: unable to resolve module dependency: 'AppKit'`, exit 65; reverted. `xcodebuild -list` resolves `GatewayClient` as a first-class scheme straight off `Package.swift` — no `.xcodeproj` needed for the SwiftPM targets, and the brief's invocation worked unchanged.

SourceKit's "No such module 'Testing'" noise confirmed as index-without-build-context artefact against a passing build.
- **[architect]** **Contract settled at three facts, not the reviewer's two.** It proposed collapsing a self-close into `.closed(code: .normal, …)`. That is one fact short: **never connected**, **closed by us**, and **closed by the peer with its code** are three things the transport can genuinely distinguish, and B3's read loop plus section 7's reattach policy have to branch on all three. A self-close reported as a normal peer close is indistinguishable from the host hanging up — and one of those must trigger a reattach while the other must not. It is also what 8.3 needs to attribute a failed turn to the right layer. Directed with the parity proof attached: all three transitions are reachable **offline**, because `URLSessionWebSocketTask.resume()` does not need a reachable server — so the real conformer could be tested without a live socket and without inventing a fake one.

Six new tests against the **real** conformer, 164/164.

But `InMemoryGatewayTransport` and its test claimed the never-connected-close transition was *"unrepresentable rather than merely avoided"*, and **it is a guard, not a structural property** — `hasConnected` and `closedLocally` are independent `Bool`s and nothing in the type prevents them contradicting; the invariant holds because the single write site is preceded by a `guard`. Confirmed the new prose is **falsifiable** — each rewrite names the specific line a future edit would have to delete — rather than one unfalsifiable claim swapped for another, and confirmed the diff is comment-only by line-by-line comparison against the prior round plus an unchanged test count.

Gates re-run by me, all five plus the .NET suite:** `make dmon-home`, `make dmon-home-test` (164/164, 29 suites), `make dmon-home-ios-check`, `make dmon-home-app` (arm64-only `lipo` guard holding), `openspec validate --strict`, `make build` (0 errors) and `env -u MEKO_API_KEY make test` (green across all 20 projects; the one skip in `Dmon.Memory.Tests` is the pre-existing Nomic embedding test). **Noted, not actioned:** `make dmon-home-ios-check` emits `IDERunDestination: Supported platforms for the buildables in the current scheme is empty` — cosmetic xcodebuild noise on a generic destination, and the build succeeds and compiles the right three files, but it will read as an error to whoever first sees it in a CI log. Carried to section 10.

- **[architect]** **B2 (6.2) briefed — the control-frame codec.** Four calls made in the brief.
  1. **Events are carried as raw `String`, never round-tripped through a Swift model.** ADR-003 frames are forwarded byte-unchanged by the gateway and must stay that way through the client; decoding and re-encoding an event would break byte-compatibility for anything the client does not model, and the client models none of it yet. The codec's event case carries the original text.
  2. **Three routing outcomes, not two.** A frame with a usable string `gw` is a control frame; a frame without one is an event; **a frame with a `gw` value this client does not recognise is its own outcome** — logged and skipped, not treated as an event and not fatal. That is forward-compatibility with a gateway that adds a control frame later, and it costs one enum case now. Mirrors the C# side's type-tolerance for the same reason it exists there (`ControlFrameSerializer.GetGwDiscriminator`: *"one malformed client frame must never crash the forwarding loop"*) — the client's read loop has the identical exposure.
  3. **`attached` decodes `wire` as optional now, ahead of 6.6 putting it there.** The field's shape is decided (D16); leaving it out would force B5 to reopen the codec. The codec's job is the shape — **what an absent value *means* is B5's policy call**, not B2's, and the brief says so.
  4. **Only the event outcome may increment the sequence counter.** B3 owns the counter, but B2's routing is what it rests on: an `ack` or a `pong` counted as an event desynchronises the client's `lastSeq` and 7.3 then replays from the wrong place. Named in the brief because it is invisible from inside B2.

- **[worker]** **B2 delivered — and it found a platform behaviour worth the block on its own.** The eight frame types with **hand-written** `Codable` conformances rather than synthesised ones, so `"gw"` is a literal baked into `encode(to:)` mirroring the C# side's get-only `Gw => "literal"`. `GatewayFrame` carries the three routing outcomes; `ControlFrameCodec` routes via `JSONSerialization` and `(json as? [String: Any])?["gw"] as? String`, which makes the C#'s type-tolerance fall out **naturally rather than by special-casing** — absent, `null` and non-string `gw` all yield "no discriminator" from one expression. 192/192, up from 164.
  - **`JSONEncoder` on this platform does not preserve `encode(to:)` call order** — its intermediate representation is an unordered dictionary. The worker found this the honest way: the literal-JSON tests **failed non-deterministically** on multi-field frames until it was fixed. `.sortedKeys` makes the bytes deterministic without claiming an ordering guarantee the wire does not have.
- **[architect]** **The sorted-keys fix is right, and it quietly bought a cross-language dependency the worker did not flag.** With sorted keys `gw` is no longer first in what the client *sends* — `create` goes out as `{"agent":"…","gw":"create"}` — so the client now **depends on the host tolerating an out-of-position discriminator inbound**, where before that tolerance was only something the client offered. Sent to the reviewer as the block's headline check with the instruction to **trace the C# parse paths rather than reason from the spec text**, because a host that parsed positionally would break this client with **no Swift test able to see it**.
- **[reviewer]** **Approve — and the dependency holds, traced not assumed.** Every path that sees the four frames the client sends: `GetGwDiscriminator` → `JsonNode.Parse` (a DOM read), and `ParseAttach`/`ParseCreate` → `JsonSerializer.Deserialize` (order-independent by contract). **No path scans positionally.** Also verified field-name and type parity against the `[JsonPropertyName]` attributes one by one (`string`↔`String`, `long`↔`Int64`), confirmed the three routing outcomes are separated **in the routing code** rather than only in the tests, confirmed the decode tests compare literal JSON against constructed values rather than `decode(encode(x)) == x`, and — applying B1's lesson — read the doc comments as claims to falsify: it checked `ControlFrame.swift`'s "seq is gateway-local and never on the wire the client sees" against `SessionHandler.cs` itself and found it true. No B1-style overclaim.

Extended it to name the dependency and the two C# parse paths the reviewer verified by hand, so the next reader does not re-derive it — and to say plainly that a host parsing these positionally would break the client invisibly.

Roughly one failure in eight full runs today. **The test's identity was not captured** — my gate command grepped for the assembly summary line, so the failing test name went to `/dev/null`, and it has not fired since. **Deliberately not attributed** to the `WizardEngineTests.InvalidChooseOneAnswer_RePromptsStep` sighting parked in `## NEXT` on 2026-08-02: that is the obvious candidate and this change has already paid once for treating a plausible mechanism that predicts an observed failure as its cause. It is now a **second sighting of *an* intermittent failure in `Dmon.Core.Tests`**, and that is all the evidence supports. **Unrelated to this block by construction** — no .NET file is touched by 6.2, and the same .NET tree passed the full suite twice at B1. **Process fix so the next occurrence identifies itself rather than needing another hunt: gate runs now tee full output to a file instead of grepping the summary.**

Left alone, that reads as B2 having under-delivered. **Split the claim across 6.2 and 6.3** rather than letting the tick land against a scenario the block did not cover. This is the second time this section that a task's parenthetical has over-claimed its scenario coverage; **worth checking each remaining task's parenthetical against what its block can actually reach, at brief-writing time rather than at tick time.**

- **[architect]** **B3 (6.3) briefed — the connection actor, and the block's real hazard is not the pong.** Answering `ping` with `pong` is three lines; what makes this block worth cutting on its own is **what must never be allowed to stop those three lines from running.** Read off the host: `HeartbeatIntervalSeconds` defaults to **25s**, and `NetworkConnectionEndpoint`'s loop treats the connection as dead **if no frame of any kind arrives within 2× that** — so a client read loop that stalls for 50 seconds is reaped, and reaped looks exactly like a network fault from the app's side. Four calls made in the brief.
  1. **The read loop answers `ping` without depending on the consumer draining.** If frame delivery to the consumer can block the loop, a slow or parked consumer stops the pongs and the host reaps a connection that is not actually dead. The liveness reply is the loop's own business, not the consumer's.
  2. **Delivery must never drop, which forces unbounded buffering.** The obvious way to keep a loop responsive under a slow consumer is a bounded buffer with a drop policy — and **here that silently corrupts resume**: the client can only know an event's `seq` by counting received event frames from `attached.headSeq`, so a dropped event shifts every subsequent number and 7.3 reattaches from the wrong place, replaying or losing turns with no error anywhere. Unbounded growth under a parked consumer is the lesser risk **and** the visible one. Named in the brief with the reasoning, because the wrong choice here looks like the prudent one.
  3. **The typed close errors from B1 propagate to the consumer rather than terminating quietly.** `.closedLocally` is a normal exit; `4409` means a newer attach superseded this connection; `4500` is a core failure. That is the entire reason B1 carried the codes, and section 8.3 needs the distinction to attribute a failed turn to the right layer.
  4. **The sequence counter is *not* built here.** It is 7.3's, and a block never spans sections. B3 owes only that the loop be the single place events pass through, so 7.3 has somewhere correct to put it. **My earlier note said the counter "belongs to the connection layer" — that was placement, not timing**, and the brief says so explicitly to stop the worker building it early.
  - Also flagged to the worker: **read `home/TOOLCHAIN-NOTES.md` before writing the loop's `Task`** — the suspected Swift 6.3.3 `swift_task_dealloc` defect was worked around twice in section 5, and both sites are the same shape as what this block needs to write.

- **[worker]** **B3 delivered.** `GatewayConnection` actor owning the transport and one named `Task<Void, Never>?` (the toolchain note's prescribed shape, not a collection), a read loop routing through B2's codec, ping answered directly on the transport rather than through the consumer stream, `.unrecognizedControl` logged and skipped, malformed frames logged and non-fatal, and an `AsyncThrowingStream` surfacing everything else plus B1's typed close errors. 200/200.
- **[reviewer]** **Request changes — `close()` can hang forever on the real transport, and the evidence is external rather than inferred.** `close()` awaited the read-loop task after cancelling it, and the loop is parked in `receive()` essentially all the time. `InMemoryGatewayTransport` wakes it — traced by hand through both interleavings — but `WebSocketGatewayTransport.close()` calls `task.cancel(with:)` and returns without awaiting the outstanding receive, and whether a pending async `receive()` is ever woken is Foundation's behaviour, not ours. Cited as an Apple-acknowledged `URLSessionWebSocketTask` bug with the closure-based API as the documented workaround. **Also killed the stall worry I had briefed** — checked against SE-0314 that `Continuation.yield` is not `async` and never suspends under *any* buffering policy, so no policy can stall the loop; bounded ones drop instead.
  - **My brief's framing was wrong, and the code had inherited it.** I told the worker unbounded buffering was needed to keep the loop responsive. The real and only reason for `.unbounded` is **no drops**. Third instance this section of my language landing in the source as a claim.
- **[architect]** **Fix directed: bound the join, do not switch to the closure-based API.** Reasoning recorded because it is the interesting half — there is no live socket in this suite by design, so adopting the closure API would have **swapped one untestable claim for another** on a citation neither of us could verify from here. Bounding is robust regardless of *why* a receive fails to wake.
- **[reviewer]** **Request changes — the directed fix does not work, proven by running it.** `withTaskGroup` cannot exit while any child is still running, cancelled or not; cancellation is cooperative. So a group-based `withTimeout` cannot bound the one thing this module says it must tolerate. Two throwaway scripts: `withTimeout(1.0)` around a never-resumed continuation **did not return within 5 seconds**; the same operation raced by two *unstructured* `Task`s resuming a shared continuation **returned at ~1.0s**. - **[architect]** **B4 (6.6) briefed — the .NET `wire` advertisement, and three calls made in the brief rather than left to the worker.**
  1. **`Wire` is a computed get-only property, not a `required` init one** — `public string Wire => ProtocolVersion.Current;`, mirroring `Gw` two properties above it in the same record. The task says "populate it where `Dmon.Network` constructs the frame"; **the construction site does not change**, and that is the point. The value is a property of the host build, not of the attach, so a default-by-construction field is correct at every site that exists and every site that ever will, whereas a `required` field is correct at one site and merely reviewed at the next. This matters here more than it usually would: the reply is built inline in `HandleAsync(HttpContext)` (`NetworkConnectionEndpoint.cs:294`), which **no test drives** — the attach path has never been exercised through the endpoint (`PerDeviceKeyE2ETests` reaches an attached state by calling `handler.Attach` directly, bypassing the reply entirely). So under a `required` field the spec scenario's *actual* assertion — "the network host replies `attached` carrying the version" — would rest on a DTO test plus review, which is the shape 6.4 was just rewritten to avoid. Correct-by-construction is the stronger instrument, and it costs one line instead of an `IHttpWebSocketFeature` harness.
  2. **The generated schema must not understate the guarantee, so the exporter gains a second fix-up.** `ProtocolSchemaExporter` already documents this exact wart for `gw`: `JsonSchemaExporter` emits a computed get-only string as `{"type":["string","null"]}`, and `PruneOmittedNullablesFromRequired` then drops it from `required`. Left alone, `docs/protocol/schema.json` would publish `wire` as **nullable and optional** on the one frame that always carries it — a false weakening in the contract document, in the same commit as a Swift client that will treat it as mandatory. `wire` gets `{"type":"string"}` and a `required` entry, applied where `FixGwDiscriminator` is applied. `{"const":"0.2"}` was considered and rejected: a const is right for a discriminator and wrong for a version, because a schema that rejects a *different* host's frame as a const violation reports a version mismatch as a malformed frame — precisely the confusion this task exists to remove.
  3. **`ProtocolVersion.Current` does not bump.** Adding a field to a `gw` control frame is additive and backwards-compatible, and `Current` versions the **ADR-003 contract**, whose shapes are untouched. It is also the value being advertised, so bumping it to describe its own arrival is circular — and since the Swift `WireVersion.isCompatible` is an exact `Major.Minor` match, a bump would make B5's just-written `WireVersion.current` mismatch on the first live connect for no semantic reason. ADR-033's "renaming wire strings would need a protocol bump" is about renaming, not adding.

Four calls made in the brief.
  1. **The check is enforced inside `GatewayConnection`, not offered as a function §7 must remember to call.** This is 6.6's lesson applied to 6.6's own follow-up: a predicate with no caller satisfies "compatibility is checked on connect" on paper and not in fact, and §6 closes with a supervisor review that should not have to take that on trust. `GatewayConnection` is the only type that sees **every** `attached` frame — first attach and every reattach — so enforcing there makes 7.1's handshake correct without 7.1 doing anything, the same correct-by-construction move as B4's computed property. 7.1 still reads `generation`/`headSeq` off the frame; it just never receives one from a host the connection has not vetted.
  2. **An absent `wire` is a refusal, and it must not be allowed to become a decode error.** The requirement is explicit — *"the client SHALL NOT assume compatibility from a version it did not receive"* — so absence is its own error case, not a pass. The trap to avoid is the tempting one: making `wire` non-optional in the decoder so absence "fails loudly". It would fail **silently** — `route()` logs a decode failure and drops the frame, by deliberate design (one malformed frame must not kill the connection), so the client would sail on with a host it never checked. That is precisely the *"malformed-frame error"* the requirement names as the wrong outcome. B2's `String?` + `decodeIfPresent` stays.
  3. **Strict `Major.Minor` parsing stays; C#'s leniency is deliberately not mirrored.** `WireVersion(parsing:)` requires exactly two integer components. `ProtocolVersion.MajorMinor` on the C# side accepts a three-part string and truncates — but the host sources `wire` from `ProtocolVersion.Current`, a **two-part constant**, so a three-part advertisement is a host shape this client has never been told how to read. Refusing it by name beats silently truncating it into a match. This is also the task's own instruction: `WireVersion(parsing:)` is wired, not edited.
  4. **Three error cases, not one** — mismatch, not-advertised, unparsable. The requirement says "actionable", and the three have different actions: upgrade one side; the host predates the field; the host sent something that is not a version. Every case names the client's version; mismatch names both, per the task.

- **[architect]** **B6 (6.5's portable half) briefed — and the defining constraint is that this block does no I/O at all.** design.md line 368 already carves it: *"the portable half (the `secretHash` computation, holding a credential, presenting it on connect) stays in [`GatewayClient`]"*. Four calls.
  1. **No store protocol, no secret generation, no provisioning — B6 is a value type, a pure function, and header construction.** The caller supplies the credential. Planting a `load()`/`save()` seam in `GatewayClient` now would be speculative API *and* would quietly pre-empt the placement question B7 still owes an answer to: a protocol in `GatewayClient` presuming where the store lives is a decision, however it is worded.   2. **The credential redacts its secret in every string conversion, and that is tested.** The "the secret does not appear in logs" scenario is this half's to make true, and the mechanism is a redacting `CustomStringConvertible` **and** `CustomDebugStringConvertible`, because the leak path is not deliberate printing — it is `String(describing:)` on a struct, which prints every stored property. This is concrete rather than hypothetical: `GatewayConnection` logs errors with exactly that call. A test must assert the secret is absent from `"\(credential)"`, `String(describing:)` **and** `String(reflecting:)`.
  3. **The hash is pinned to the C# side by construction, not by resemblance:** SHA-256 over the token's UTF-8 bytes, hex-encoded **lowercase**, no salt. `DeviceKeyAuthenticator` hex-decodes with `Convert.FromHexString` (case-insensitive, so case cannot break auth) but the C# test helper writes `Convert.ToHexStringLower(SHA256.HashData(Encoding.UTF8.GetBytes(token)))`, and matching the writer is what keeps a future byte-comparison of two stores honest. The pinned-digest test must use **`"token1"`** — the same token `PerDeviceKeyE2ETests` uses — and carry that C# expression verbatim in its comment, so a reader can regenerate the expected value in the other language rather than trusting the literal.
  4. **The doc comments must not claim the client checks the bind or the store — it can see neither.** The honest statement is that with no credential the client connects unauthenticated, and whether that is *accepted* is the host's decision (ADR-036: empty store is auth-disabled on a loopback bind and fail-closed on a non-loopback one). A sentence like "connects without a key when the store is empty" reads as a check the client performs and does not perform. Under D13's self-provisioning the two coincide by construction — the host's own entry is in the store precisely when it holds a credential — but that is an invariant to *state*, not a lookup to imply.

The lesson is not "write truer comments": it is that **a claim is only as good as the case that could disprove it**, and a test suite that never builds that case leaves the claim unguarded no matter how carefully it was worded.
- **[architect]** **Product Owner decision (2026-08-06): 6.5's co-located half gets its own new small target**, not `Supervisor`. This closes the placement question D16 left open and `## NEXT` has been carrying since B6 was briefed. Supervision and auth provisioning are different responsibilities, and the `ndmon`-child adjacency that made `Supervisor` tempting is proximity, not cohesion.
- **[architect]** **Named `DeviceKeys`, and the reasoning matters more than the name.** It mirrors `frontends/Dmon.Network/DeviceKeys/` — the code it must stay in byte-level agreement with, which is worth real money to whoever next checks the two sides agree — and it covers **both** halves of the responsibility (holding the host's own credential, provisioning one when the store requires it) so it needs no rename when the second block lands. `DeviceProvisioning` was the alternative and was rejected: it names only half of what the target does, and it encodes a *deployment* property. **Names that encode a deployment constraint go stale when the constraint moves, and design.md already records this one moving** — under the split topology, provisioning leaves this machine entirely for the `ndmon` verb. Co-located-only is a fact to document at the target, not to bake into its name.
- **[architect]** **Re-carved 6.5's remainder into two blocks, by behaviour rather than by layer.** The first draft was "B7 = Keychain conformer + `devices.json` self-provisioning", which would have made B7 a **layer** — a store protocol and two conformers with no caller until B8. That is the exact shape B5's brief called out as satisfying a requirement on paper, and the shape a supervisor reads as dead scaffolding. So:
  - **B7 — the read path, end to end:** the new target, the credential-store seam with its Keychain and in-memory conformers, the `devices.json` reader, and the policy that decides *present a key or connect unauthenticated*. Every abstraction it adds has a caller inside the same block. Satisfies the unauthenticated-loopback scenario and the secret-not-exposed scenario.
  - **B8 — the write path:** token generation, the `{keyId, name, secretHash, createdAt}` append, and the recovery cases. Completes "the client presents its own device key, distinct from any other client's". **6.5 ticks at B8.**
- **[architect]** **Three facts read off the C# side rather than assumed, all binding on B7 and each capable of silently diverging.**
  1. **"Empty" means no *active* entry, not no entry.** `DeviceKeyStoreReader` filters `revokedAt != null` before building the set, so a `devices.json` containing only revoked rows is `DeviceKeySet.Empty` — **auth disabled**. A Swift reader that tests `devices.isEmpty` would present a key where the host expects none, and the failure would look like an auth bug rather than a parsing one.
  2. **`schemaVersion` must be exactly `1`** — the host *throws* on any other value rather than tolerating it, so the client must not be more lenient than the thing it is agreeing with.
  3. **Malformed must not degrade to unauthenticated.** The host's reader throws and its watcher fails closed to last-good. A Swift reader that treated a parse failure as "no devices, connect without a key" would **fail open** against a store that requires auth — a security-relevant divergence dressed as robustness. Propagate the error.

It also reported, unprompted and precisely, which Keychain behaviours its suite leaves unproven — which is what made the reviewer's check of that list possible rather than notional.

The Swift reader never looks at the hash field, so it is *stricter* than the host: the safe direction, but a real divergence that can produce a spurious `.credentialRequiredButMissing` for a file the host treats as auth-disabled.

The genuinely untestable surface should be `SecItemCopyMatching` and the `AnyObject → Data` cast, nothing more.

- **[architect]** **The U+200B footnote taken in my own lane, and it is worth the ten lines it costs.** The docstring claimed to mirror `string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace`; that claim is true for every input that can occur and false for inputs that are merely expressible, and the difference now sits in the comment along with *why* it cannot bite — `secretHash` is a `[0-9a-f]` digest written by the provisioning path, and a hex string cannot be composed of zero-width spaces. **This is the good case of the standing rule, not the punitive one:** the sentence was not sloppy, it was a reasonable shorthand that a code-point enumeration proved imprecise, and the fix is to say precisely how far it holds rather than to delete the useful comparison.

- **[architect]** **Product Owner decision (2026-08-06): a host whose key has been revoked does NOT self-provision a new one.** Revocation is an operator act and the client honours it. D13's "it grants no capability the host did not already have" is true about *capability* and beside the point about *intent* — the file is the operator's instrument for withdrawing a key, and a client that quietly writes itself a fresh one turns that instrument into a suggestion.
- **[architect]** **Extending that decision to the adjacent state, flagged as an inference the Product Owner can overturn rather than a second question.** There are two ways a held credential can fail to be usable: its `keyId` is in `devices.json` but **revoked**, or its `keyId` is **absent from the file entirely**. The decision names the first. I am applying it to the second as well, because the honest generalisation of "do not silently re-grant access the operator withdrew" is **"do not silently re-grant access the store does not currently record"** — and an anomalous state (we hold a key the store has never heard of: a replaced file, a restored backup, a Keychain carried from another machine) is precisely where acting silently is worst. Both refuse and surface; **neither provisions**.
  - **The two must still say different things**, because the operator's next step differs: revoked is *"you did this deliberately"*, absent is *"the store may have been replaced"*. Same action, different message.
  - **The escape hatch must be named in the message, or this is a dead end:** deleting the Keychain item returns the host to "holds no credential", which *is* the provisioning path. That is recoverable and destroys nothing, which is what makes refusing acceptable rather than brittle.
  - **This does not touch first-ever provisioning**, which flows from holding *no* credential at all — the only state that provisions.

B9 = the **action** — token generation, the Keychain write, and the `devices.json` append. Giving the security-sensitive write its own block means it gets a review of its own rather than riding in behind a pile of enum cases. **6.5 ticks at B9**, after the Product Owner has run the Keychain verification recipe.

That is the gap B8 exists to close, sitting inside the tests that were supposed to cover the area.

The correction is comment-only, changes no behaviour, and the change has already touched .NET once (6.6). **Recorded as an exception rather than taken silently.**

The worker is directed to **determine empirically which it is and pin the answer with a test**, not to paper over the diagnostics. Nit 1 (messages should name the `keyId`) is taken as written.

It kept the test anyway, as directed, and separately made the diagnostics safe *independent* of that answer via a `caseLabelForDiagnostics` that never prints a payload — belt and braces, correctly reasoned: a future case whose payload does not redact cannot leak through a failing test's log.

That establishes three things at once: the claim is true, it is a property of the toolchain rather than an artefact of the test, and **the kept test would genuinely fail if the redaction were removed.** It also traced the corrected C# comment line by line against `Authenticate`, confirmed the .NET diff is eight lines all inside a comment block with the `if` condition byte-identical, and confirmed the repo-wide grep for the old phrasing returns zero.

- **[architect]** **What B8 is worth remembering for: a false sentence outlived every process that should have caught it, because each step only looked at its own diff.** It was written in `Dmon.Network`, reviewed and merged there; mirrored into a Swift test in B7, reviewed and merged there; mirrored again into a Swift header in B8. **Three reviews, three approvals, one claim that was backwards the whole time** — and it survived because a diff-local review asks "is this diff right?", and copying a wrong sentence faithfully is, diff-locally, right. What broke the chain was not a better review of the third copy: it was **grepping for the sentence instead of reading the site**. Generalisation worth carrying past this change: *when a comment explains why another component behaves as it does, verify it against that component, not against the code it sits beside* — and when one turns out to be wrong, **search for its copies before fixing the instance in front of you**.
- **[architect]** **B9 (6.5's write path) briefed — the last block of section 6, and the only one that writes a security-sensitive file. Five calls.**
  1. **The Keychain write happens BEFORE the `devices.json` append, and the ordering is the decision, not an implementation detail.** Either half can fail. **Keychain first, append fails** → the host holds a credential the store has no record of → B8's `.credentialUnknownToStore` refusal, which names the keyId and the cleanup command: **visible and recoverable**. **Append first, Keychain fails** → an orphan row in the operator's file and a host holding nothing → next start it provisions *again*, and the rows accumulate **silently**. Same two failures, and only one of them tells anyone. Order for the failure you can see.
  2. **The append must not decode-and-re-encode.** Other rows can carry fields this client does not model — `name` on another device, or the `expiresAt` `DevicesFileEnvelope`'s own comment anticipates — and a round-trip through a lossy DTO would **delete them**, silently, from a file this client does not own. Parse generically, append, re-serialise. The reader is deliberately lenient about unknown fields; the writer must be actively protective of them, which is a stronger obligation than symmetry.
  3. **Replace atomically.** The host hot-reloads `devices.json` through a `FileSystemWatcher` and fail-closes to last-good, so a torn read degrades safely — but "the failure mode is survivable" is not a reason to write a file non-atomically when the operator's auth store is what is being rewritten. Temp file in the same directory, then rename.
  4. **Provisioning is reachable only from the one state that warrants it.** `.credentialRequiredButMissing` requires active entries, so the empty-store case cannot reach it *through the policy* — but a provisioner callable directly is a provisioner someone will call directly, and **writing into an empty store would switch auth on for every client as a side effect of this host booting**. Make it unreachable by construction rather than by convention.
  5. **The strongest test available is a round trip through the reader that already exists.** Provision against a temp directory, then read the result back with `DevicesFileReader`/`DeviceAuthPolicy` and assert the decision is now `.presentCredential` with the generated credential. That closes the loop B7 and B8 built, in-module, without a real Keychain — and it is the direct answer to the fixture-fidelity lesson: the file the test reads was **written by the code under test**, not hand-rolled to match it.
- **[architect]** **What automated tests cannot reach here, stated before the block starts rather than discovered at its end:** the real Keychain, and whether `Dmon.Network` actually accepts a key this client provisioned. The second is the whole two-language contract — hex digest, field names, timestamp format, header form — and **no Swift test can prove it**, because the thing that must agree is a .NET process. That is what the human-verification recipe is for, and it is why 6.5 ticks after the Product Owner runs it rather than when the code goes green.

It re-derives both halves of the precondition inside `provision()` instead, which cannot be spoofed by a caller. It also forced the append-failure path with a real `chmod 0o500` rather than a mock, so the ordering-dependent failure is exercised for real.
- **[reviewer]** **Request changes — one blocker, and it is a citation it checked rather than accepted.** The token comment claimed standard base64 is *"exactly the `token68` character set RFC 6750 §2.1 defines"*. It **fetched RFC 6750**: the production there is named **`b64token`**, not `token68` (that name is RFC 7235's for a same-shaped but separately-defined rule), and the sets are **not equal** — `b64token` also admits `-`, `.`, `_`, `~`, which base64 never emits. Base64 is a *subset*, which is why the functional conclusion survives while the sentence does not. Exactly the failure mode I warned the block about: a claim about another component, verified against the code beside it rather than against the component.
  - **Probe A answered:** `appendEntry` **does** re-read the file rather than reusing the earlier parse, so there is no stale-copy bug — but the three file touchpoints have no locking or compare-and-swap, and **the single-writer assumption is stated nowhere**.
  - **Probe B answered, and better than my framing:** the duplicate case is *unreachable* from `provision()`, because `loadCredential()` **throws** on an undecodable-but-present item rather than returning nil — so `provision()` exits before touching the Keychain. The only path to `errSecDuplicateItem` is a genuine race, in which the item that landed **could be usable** and `SecItemUpdate` would overwrite it. The doc's claim is true as literally written and does not cover that window.
  - **Probe C:** the blocker above. **Probe D:** the actor conversion is clean, no assertion weakened.

That is expected rather than dead scaffolding: section 6 builds the client's foundations and the connect flow that calls this arrives later. But "unreachable today" is precisely the state in which an unstated assumption gets built on, so the assumption gets **written down now**, and the decision it forces — whether the eventual caller must serialise provisioning attempts — goes to `## NEXT` where the section that wires it will read it.
- **[architect]** **A cross-language golden test was considered for the C# side and rejected, which is worth recording because it looked like the obvious answer.** The reviewer named an unprovable: whether `Dmon.Network`'s reader actually accepts a row this Swift writer appends. The tempting fix is a fixture produced by the Swift writer, committed, and parsed by a `Dmon.Network.Tests` case. **It would be a false guard.** Nothing regenerates it, so a Swift-side field rename would change the Swift tests, leave the golden untouched, and the C# test would keep passing against a file no longer representative — **the exact "fixture agrees with itself rather than with reality" failure this section has now found three times.** The honest instruments are the two already in play: a Swift-side pin on the exact key set, and the human recipe, which proves more than any golden could because it exercises the real host, the real hash and the real header.
- **[worker]** **Remediation delivered, and nit 4 was taken further than asked.** The citation rewritten against both RFCs; token-shape test; the append-failure test now asserts the file byte-for-byte unchanged; the single-writer assumption and the Keychain race window documented; the appended-entry test now pins an **exact key set**. For the temp-file error it replaced `FileManager.createFile` + `chmod` with `open(O_CREAT|O_EXCL|O_WRONLY, 0o600)` / `write` / `close` — which captures the real `errno` **and** removes the window where the file briefly existed with looser permissions.
- **[reviewer]** **Request changes — `close()`'s return value is discarded, and the argument for why that matters is the good part.** The rest of the POSIX path checked out: short writes are treated as failure rather than success, the fd is closed on every path, the temp file is removed on both failure paths, `O_EXCL` genuinely blocks a symlink swap, and the permissions-window claim is true (`mode & ~umask` can only narrow). But `close()` runs with its result dropped — so on a filesystem using delayed allocation, **`ENOSPC` surfacing at close time is invisible**, `bytesWritten == data.count` already passed, and the code proceeds to atomically swap a file whose persistence was never confirmed into the operator's auth store.
  - **Why this is more than a checklist item:** the refactor's own doc justifies hand-rolling POSIX because *"disk-full and permission-denied are distinguishable this way, unlike `createFile`'s bare `Bool`"* — and disk-full is exactly the failure most likely to arrive at `close()`. **The stated rationale and the unchecked path name the same failure mode.**
  - Its recommendation, which I accept: fix locally rather than revert. `O_EXCL` and the closed permissions window are real gains worth keeping, and the defect is one unchecked return.
  - Nit: no `EINTR` retry on `write`. Cannot corrupt anything (the replace happens only after success) but can fail a call that would have succeeded.
- **[architect]** **Fix directed, with the `close`-on-`EINTR` trap called out explicitly rather than left to be discovered.** The obvious symmetry — "retry both on `EINTR`" — is **wrong for `close`**: POSIX leaves the descriptor's state unspecified after an interrupted `close`, and on macOS/BSD the descriptor **is** deallocated, so a retry can close an unrelated file that has since been handed the same number. Retry the `write`; never retry the `close`. The worker is required to verify that against the platform's own man page rather than take it from me — the block has already been sent back once for a claim about another component taken on trust.
- **[worker]** **Final fix delivered, and the verification came back better than the instruction.** `close`'s own `errno` captured at the failure site; temp file removed; a **distinct** error case `temporaryFileCloseUnconfirmed(errno:)`, because "every byte reached the kernel but the commit was never confirmed" is a different claim from "the write never completed". `write` now retries `EINTR` and accumulates short writes; `close` never retries. **On the man-page check I required:** Darwin's `man 2 close` turns out to be **silent** on the descriptor's disposition — it lists `EINTR` only as a possible error — so rather than attribute my claim to a page that does not make it, the worker cited **FreeBSD's** `close(2)` for the explicit wording and named the libc lineage as the bridge. Asked to verify a claim, it found the obvious source did not support it and went and found one that did.
- **[reviewer]** **Approve.** It ran Darwin's man page and fetched FreeBSD's, confirmed both quotes verbatim, and confirmed the comment **attributes each to its actual source** rather than blurring them — the specific defect class this block had already been sent back for twice. It then checked the retry loop for the bug that would matter most: `totalWritten` advances only by the kernel-reported count, the pointer and remaining-length arithmetic stay in bounds by construction, a persistent error cannot spin, and a short write with no error still cannot be mistaken for success. One nit: the sentence said *"unconditionally … regardless of which error"* and then quoted FreeBSD's *"except [EBADF]"* one clause later — self-contradicting.

- **[supervisor]** **Approve — "the strongest section of the change", with no cross-block drift, no duplicated abstraction, no dead scaffolding, no ADR erosion.** It verified rather than accepted, twice over: it **re-derived all three pinned SHA-256 digests** from `shasum` (a digest pinned from the implementation's own output is circular — these are not), and it **ran the U+200B whitespace claim across `U+0000..U+3100` against both runtimes**, getting exactly the asymmetry the comment states — `[]` one way, `[U+200B]` the other. Its verdict on the prose: *"the density is not the liability — it is load-bearing and it is checkable."*
  - **On the uncalled provisioner, my main worry: deferred wiring, and it made the distinction testable rather than tasteful.** The credential path is a chain in which **every link but the last is already consumed by the next** — `provision()` → `store()` → `status(ofKeyId:)` → `decide()` → `headers(for:)` → `GatewayEndpoint` → `WebSocketGatewayTransport`. Only the outermost caller is missing, and speculative construction looks different: a type nothing downstream consumes. It named `provisioningThenDecidingPresentsTheGeneratedCredential` as the decisive evidence — B9 writes a file B7's code reads and B8's code judges, in one test. It also credited the B7/B8 re-carve *by behaviour rather than by layer* as the reason this came out clean.
  - **On the three-type factoring:** not duplicated policy. `DeviceKeyProvisioner` does not re-derive the active-entry rule, it **calls the same `hasActiveEntries()`** the policy calls. One predicate, three consumers: facts, decision, action.
  - **Its sharpest finding, which the iOS gate structurally cannot see:** `GatewayEndpoint.defaultURL = ws://127.0.0.1:5500/ws` is reachable through a **chain of zero-argument defaults** — `WebSocketGatewayTransport()` → `GatewayEndpoint()` → `defaultURL` — so a **co-location assumption is the default path in the module that is supposed to be topology-agnostic**. Its doc says it is "never a loopback assumption baked into the transport's logic": true of the *logic*, not of the *default*. **The gate proves portability of syntax, not of assumptions**, and this is the first place that distinction costs anything.
  - **On ticking 6.5:** the section-4 precedent applies, but for a **narrower reason than "thin wrapper"** — it applies because everything decidable was moved above the line (`KeychainCredentialCodec` made `internal` precisely so it is testable). That the artefact is a credential rather than a UI grant does not change what a test could reach.
  - **A naming hazard no block could see:** `DeviceCredential` now names **two different things** across the languages — Swift's holds the plaintext secret, C#'s holds the hash and never sees a secret. In a two-language mirror whose entire safety argument is fidelity, the same name for opposite contents is where a future reader assumes wrongly.
  - **On the guards:** all four mirrored rules are faithful, but **only the digest has a tripwire that would fire if .NET moved.** The active-entry rule, the envelope field names and the header form would all break silently. It agrees the golden file was rightly rejected, and notes the live `ndmon` run proves *today's* agreement and installs no tripwire either.

## 7. Gateway client — session lifecycle and turns

- **[architect]** Base: `88e5424` — the client's upper half: create→attach handshake, rejection as a named error, a client-derived sequence cursor with reattach-and-replay, turn submission with streamed rendering, and the device-key connect flow §6 built every mechanism for but could not wire.
- **[architect]** **Product Owner decisions opening the section (2026-08-07).** Three, all taken on the recommended option.
  1. **Swift's `DeviceCredential` becomes `DeviceKeySecret`**, and its stores follow (`DeviceKeySecretStore`, `KeychainDeviceKeySecretStore`, `InMemoryDeviceKeySecretStore`). This closes the supervisor's naming finding on the cheaper side (nothing outside `home/` consumes it). The chosen discriminator is the **real** one and not merely a different word: this side holds the plaintext secret, C#'s `DeviceCredential` holds only the hash and never sees a secret. `OwnDeviceKey` was offered and declined for reading less precisely; renaming the C# side instead was declined for putting a .NET diff inside a Swift-only section. Note the near-miss avoided: `DeviceKeyStore` would have been the obvious short name and is **wrong** — C# already has `DeviceKeyStoreReader`, which reads the *registry*, so that name would have replaced one cross-language collision with a worse one.
  2. **7.3 builds the reattach *mechanism* only.** An explicit `reattach()` that opens a fresh connection and attaches with the tracked cursor. **No backoff, no retry loop, no give-up policy** — *when* to reattach is §8's call. Rejected: §7 owning an automatic reconnect policy, because no task or scenario specifies its values and it would stand up a second backoff implementation beside `Supervisor/RestartBackoff.swift`.
  3. **The device-key connect flow is a §7 block**, closing the two scenarios §6 deferred here ("no client connects until §7 wires the flow"). No §7 task names it, so this is recorded as scope taken deliberately, not scope crept: the spec requirement is in this change's own delta and its scenarios are unclosed. It also has to land for the deferred live `ndmon` verification to be runnable at all.
- **[architect]** **`create` and `attach` cannot share a connection — the server hangs up after `created`, and 7.1 as written implies otherwise.** Read directly from the code, not inferred: `NetworkConnectionEndpoint.HandleAsync` accepts the socket under a `using` declaration (`:220`), reads exactly one frame, and on `gw == "create"` calls `HandleCreateAsync(...)` **and then `return`s** (`:245-249`). That return exits the `using` scope and disposes the socket. `HandleCreateAsync` itself sends `created` and returns; **it sends no close frame** and never loops for a second frame. After an attach, the forwarding loop's `switch (gw)` handles only `ping`/`pong` and drops everything else to a `default:` that logs and ignores (`:748-765`) — so a `create` or a second `attach` on a live connection is a **silent no-op**, not an error.
  - **Consequence, binding on 7.1 and 7.3 alike:** the client opens a **throwaway connection** to create, reads `created`/`createRejected`, lets it die, then opens a **second** connection to attach. Reattach after a drop is a third. That is three connections in a session's life, all built the same way — which is why the session type takes a **connection factory**, not a connection, from its very first block. 7.1 and 7.3 turn out to need the same structure for different reasons.
  - **Not a spec defect.** "A session is established by create then attach" never says *on one connection*, and neither does D2. The task text merely reads as though it does. Flagged to the Product Owner rather than treated as a stop-and-ask, because nothing normative had to change.
  - **The existing .NET tests could not have caught this**, and that is worth knowing before trusting them as a model: `NetworkCreateE2ETests` calls `endpoint.HandleCreateAsync(socket, ...)` **directly**, with a fake socket it owns. The socket-lifetime behaviour lives one frame up, in `HandleAsync`, which those tests never enter.
- **[architect]** **The `seq`-cursor question §6 handed forward is settled by the wire, in the client's favour.** `seq` is **never** on the wire: `SessionHandler.DrainAsync` sends the core's raw stdout line unchanged, and the only sequence number the client is ever told is `headSeq` on `attached`. So the cursor is necessarily derived — **seed from `attached.headSeq`, then +1 for every received frame with no `gw` field** — and control frames (`ack`, `ping`, `pong`, `attached`) must never advance it. §6 parked the question *"does `lastSeq` track yield-availability or actual consumption?"*; deriving the cursor **in the consumer, as each `.event` is handled** answers it with the stronger of the two, and is strictly compatible with §6's binding "count at yield, never at receive" rule. Server-side facts that bound it, all read from `SessionHandler.cs`: `lastSeq` is **exclusive** (delivery is `(lastSeq, headSeq]`, `nextSeq = _sentSeq + 1`); an out-of-range `lastSeq` is silently `Math.Clamp`ed to `[0, headSeq]` rather than refused; the seq log is **never truncated** (ADR-014 D3), so there is no replay-window-expiry path to handle and `lastSeq: 0` replays a whole session; and **there is no marker between replayed and live events** — one loop serves both, so "the replay seam" is not observable from the client and cannot be tested by looking for it.
- **[architect]** **Three server behaviours recorded now because they will bite a later section, not this one.**
  1. **Outstanding permission prompts are re-emitted at a fresh seq on every attach.** `SessionHandler.Attach` walks `_outstanding` and, for any pending `tool.confirmRequest`/`ui.inputRequest` whose original seq is at or below the new cursor, **appends a fresh copy of the same raw line above `headSeq`** (`:274-284`) — and `headSeq` is captured *after* that loop, so the `attached` frame already counts them. Any client that renders permission prompts **must key them by request `id` and treat a re-appearance as the same request**. §7 models no prompts, so it cannot get this wrong; whichever section first does, can.
  2. **`ack` is a receipt, not a window.** Nothing gates sending on it; a duplicate id is re-acked **without** re-writing to the core (`TryAdmitCommand` → `CommandAdmission.Duplicate`), so a resend is safe. 7.4 must not build a wait-for-ack turnstile.
  3. **Several terminations carry no close code at all** — the post-`created` teardown, eviction by a newer attach (`evicted.Abort()`), and revocation (`conn.Abort()`) are abrupt aborts, not close handshakes. §6's three-fact transport reporting still holds, but 8.3 should not assume every disconnection arrives with a code to name.

No task numbers; no behaviour change.** Both are section-6 supervisor carry-forwards, landed together and *first* so the four behavioural blocks after them contain no rename noise. Two hazards called out in the brief because neither is visible from the rename itself: **the Keychain service/account/label strings and `deleteCommand`'s shell text are persisted identifiers and must not change** — renaming a Swift type must not orphan an already-stored Keychain item — and **the pinned SHA-256 digest literals in the credential tests must survive verbatim**, since they are the only tripwire on the two-language hash contract (design risk 2). Vocabulary is renamed as a whole rather than half: `DeviceAuthDecision`'s cases go `.presentCredential`→`.presentKey`, `.credentialRequiredButMissing`→`.keyRequiredButMissing`, `.credentialRevoked`→`.keyRevoked`, `.credentialUnknownToStore`→`.keyUnknownToStore`, because a type renamed under cases that still say "credential" is the "two shapes for one contract" shape a supervisor would rightly flag next section.

- **[architect]** **Scope check on 7.1's parenthetical claim, done at brief-writing time as section 6's rule requires — and it overreaches.** 7.1 claims it also satisfies *"The host reaches its session through the gateway, not through stdio"*. That requirement's scenario asserts *"WHEN the **host** establishes a session THEN it does so **over a WebSocket** connection AND it **spawns no core process** of its own"*, and **B2 can reach neither half**: a handshake driven by `InMemoryGatewayTransport` is by construction not over a WebSocket, and "spawns no core process" is an *absence* property about the whole `home/` tree, not something any handshake test can assert. Disposition: **B2 satisfies only the create-then-attach scenario**; the WebSocket half completes when B5 wires the production transport and §8.1 drives a real turn through it, and the absence half is closed by inspection — verified now, so it is not left as a claim: `ChildInventory`'s only spawn target is `.homeRelativePath(".dotnet/tools/ndmon")`, and its one other executable reference is `tailscale status --json`, a run-to-completion health check rather than a spawn target. **No core is spawned anywhere in `home/`.** That is the fourth time this section-6 rule has caught a task's scenario claim.
- **[architect]** **Block B2 = tasks 7.1 + 7.2 — the create→attach handshake and `createRejected` as a named error.** Cut together rather than apart: `created` and `createRejected` are the two arms of one `switch` on one awaited reply, so splitting them would land a handshake that ignores a frame it must already handle. Four architect decisions carried into the brief, recorded here with their reasons.
  1. **The session takes a *transport* factory, not a connection and not a transport.** Forced by the two-socket finding — create, attach and (B3) reattach each need a fresh one. Transport rather than connection because `GatewayConnection` has no configuration of its own, so making the caller build connections would leak connection-lifecycle knowledge outward for no gain; and the session must own connect/close ordering regardless. The factory closure is also **exactly where B5 injects an authenticated transport**, which is why B5 needs no change to this type.
  2. **One session-scoped output stream, fed by a pump — not the connection's own stream handed through.** The returned stream must outlive any single connection or B3's reattach would force every consumer to re-subscribe. The pump mirrors `GatewayConnection`'s already-reviewed shape in this same module (a singular named `Task` plus an actor-isolated `route` method), so it is a precedent being followed, not a new pattern.
  3. **`createSession` does not use the pump.** Nothing else consumes a create-only connection, so it is plain request/reply and then closed. Keeping the pump out of it is what keeps the continuation machinery confined to one path.
  4. **No client-side handshake timeout, deliberately.** The host bounds create itself (`CreateHandshakeTimeoutSeconds`, default 30) and answers `core_timeout`, and a peer close surfaces through the transport — so the uncovered case is narrow: a host holding the socket open and replying *nothing*. A client timeout would need a policy value no task specifies, and the one timeout utility in this tree (`Supervisor/TimeoutRace.swift`) is both in the wrong module and **already carries a proven defect**. Documented at the call site and parked in `## NEXT` rather than left to be discovered.
- **[worker]** B2 done — `GatewaySession` actor + `GatewaySessionError` + a recording transport factory in test support. Public surface as briefed. `createSession` deliberately never sets `self.sessionId`, so that property can never mean "created but not attached". Peer close propagates as `GatewayTransportError.closed(code:reason:)` and a wire mismatch as `WireVersion.CompatibilityError`, both **unwrapped** — wrapping either would discard the only actionable material. Flagged an ordering fix it made itself: `connection`/`sessionId`/`outputContinuation` are assigned **before** the pump `Task` exists, or an item arriving immediately after `attached` would route into a still-`nil` continuation and vanish. 298/298. → @reviewer
- **[reviewer]** **Request changes — one blocker: overlapping `attach()` calls.** Everything else verified clean, including that the tests falsify rather than merely pass (the happy path uses a session id that only reaches the `attach` frame if it was genuinely read back from `created`; the rejection test asserts *both* `factory.count() == 1` and the absence of any `attach` frame). **The blocker:** `attach` suspends twice — `connect()` and `send()` — **before** writing to `self`, and actor isolation does not serialise across suspensions. Three failures follow, and **none trips a runtime diagnostic**: (1) a second call overwrites `attachWaiter`, orphaning the first continuation — the caller **hangs forever**, and the leak-on-`deinit` diagnostic never fires because the continuation is held by a `Task` that never completes (`InMemoryGatewayTransport` already documents that exact behaviour); (2) **cross-wiring** — if A's connection answers first, `routeFromPump` resumes **B** with **A's** `generation`/`headSeq`, and B's own later `attached` then falls through to `outputContinuation.yield` and reaches the consumer as ordinary session content; (3) the failure `catch` nils `connection`/`sessionId`/`outputContinuation` unconditionally, so a *failing* call tears down a *different, successful* call's live session. Explicitly noted that the worker's cited evidence — "the runtime traps loudly on a double resume" — **does not apply**, because this is not a double resume; it is one continuation misdirected and one silently leaked.
  - **How this was found matters more than the finding.** The block's own suite structurally cannot see it — every test drives one `attach` at a time — so a green 298/298 said nothing either way. It came from the architect reading the landed code and putting it to the reviewer as a **hypothesis to confirm or refute**, not as a finding to accept. **Rule: after a block lands, read it for what its own tests are structurally unable to observe, and hand that to the reviewer as a question.**
- **[architect]** **The fix shape is mine, not the worker's, because it pre-decides B3 — and the obvious fix is wrong.** "Refuse if already attached, let B3 do close-then-attach" would break **D2**: `close()` finishes `outputContinuation`, and D2's whole point is that the session stream *survives* a reattach. So the guard goes on the **handshake, not the session** — `attachInFlight` set synchronously before the first suspension (an actor body is atomic up to its first `await`), two *distinguishable* errors (`.attachAlreadyInFlight` = "you raced yourself" vs `.alreadyAttached` = "you want reattach, not attach"), and the connect→send→await sequence factored into a private `performAttach` that B3's `reattach()` can drive while tearing the old connection down **without** finishing the output stream.
- **[worker]** Fixed. Guard wrapper + `performAttach`; `isAttached` deliberately **not** collapsed into `connection != nil`, since `connection` is set mid-handshake and would misdiagnose a race as "already attached". Two tests added, and **the regression test was falsified rather than assumed**: reverting the guard made it **hang** (the exact silent leak), not fail — so it demonstrably bites. 300/300. → @reviewer
- **[reviewer]** **Request changes — the blocker this time is in a doc comment the architect dictated.** Guard mechanics all verified sound: the synchronous-prelude premise, `defer` covering every exit, the two errors being mutually exclusive, the redundant-`finish` removal being redundant on *every* path (not just the traced one), and both new tests falsifying — including the **cross-wiring** case specifically, via distinctive `generation: 7, headSeq: 11` values. **But `performAttach`'s comment promised B3 that two steps suffice, and they do not.** `runPump`/`routeFromPump`/`finishPump` carry **no pump-generation identity** and read `self.outputContinuation` *dynamically, at call time*. So on a reattach, tearing down the old connection ends its stream **normally**, the superseded pump's loop exits without throwing, and its `finishPump(throwing: nil)` terminates **whichever continuation is current** — killing the very stream the reattach preserved. A third mechanism is required, and the comment disclaimed anything beyond the two named steps as not belonging.
- **[architect]** **Second instance of a rule section 6 already learned: a brief that states a *property* comes back as a *claim* in the source.** Section 6's instance was "make the transition structurally unrepresentable"; this one is my own *"so the returned stream survives the swap, per design D2"*, faithfully written down about code that cannot do it. **This instance is worse in a way worth naming: the claim points *forward*, at a block that does not exist yet.** B3 would have inherited a comment asserting sufficiency, and the missing piece is a *concurrency* guarantee — which surfaces as a silently dead stream, never as a compile error. **Disposition: do not build the mechanism in B2.** No second pump can exist until B3 adds reattach, so generation-scoping now would ship untestable concurrency machinery — the precise trade this change has repeatedly been caught making. Instruction to the worker was therefore *"make the comment match the code, not the code match my sentence."*

Gates: `make dmon-home-test` 300/300 · `make dmon-home-ios-check` · `make dmon-home-app` · `make build` 0 warnings · `env -u MEKO_API_KEY make test` 0 failures · `openspec validate --strict` valid. **7.1 is ticked for the create-then-attach scenario only** — its second parenthetical claim was dispositioned above and is **not** covered by this block.
- **[architect]** **Block B3 = task 7.3 — the sequence cursor and `reattach()`. Opening it required withdrawing a claim of my own, and the withdrawal is the substantive part.**
  1. **"The session stream survives a reattach" is withdrawn — it is not merely unbuilt, it is unachievable in that shape, and it was never a design decision.** Traced before briefing: on a drop, the pump calls `finishPump(throwing:)`, which finishes `outputContinuation` **with the error**; an `AsyncThrowingStream` that has finished is terminated and nothing can revive it. Making it survive would require the session to **swallow** the drop and surface connection state through a *separate* channel — which no §7 task asks for, and which would discard the 4409-vs-4500-vs-local-close distinction §6 deliberately built and 8.3 needs. **Checked against `design.md` D2 itself: D2 says only that `lastSeq`/`headSeq`/`generation` make "replay and resume after a dropped connection come free". It never claims stream continuity.** That phrase was mine, invented on top of D2 in B2's brief. **So: `reattach()` returns a NEW stream, and the consumer re-subscribes.** B2's already-softened comments must be corrected again — "necessary but not sufficient" still implies the goal is reachable, and it is not.
  2. **The pump-generation gap is real anyway, and B3 must close it.** It does not depend on the withdrawn claim: `finishPump` reads `self.outputContinuation` *dynamically*, so a superseded pump completing after `reattach()` has installed a **new** continuation finishes the new one. Design: a `pumpGeneration` counter, incremented **before** the old connection is torn down so the old pump is already stale when it completes, threaded through `runPump`/`routeFromPump`/`finishPump`, which no-op when their generation is not current.
  3. **`reattach()` is refused while a connection is live** — it is for *after* a drop, per the task's own wording. This is not fussiness: allowing it live would mean tearing down a stream holding **counted-but-undrained** events, which would desynchronise the cursor by exactly the amount buffered. Refusing closes that door by construction rather than by documentation.
  4. **Section 6's parked question — "does `lastSeq` track yield-availability or actual consumption?" — is answered: yield-availability.** Counted in `routeFromPump` as each `.event` is handed to the session's consumer. It satisfies §6's binding rule (count at yield, never at receive), and tracking true consumption would need the consumer to acknowledge every event back — a far heavier API than anything here justifies. **The residual, stated rather than swept:** an event yielded into the buffer but never drained is counted and will not be replayed. That is safe here for two independent reasons — §6 verified `finish()` does **not** clear buffered elements, so a consumer draining after a drop still receives them before the terminal error; and decision 3 removes the only path that would discard a live buffer. What remains is a consumer that abandons its own stream mid-drain, which has thereby also abandoned whatever it rendered — a loss no replay protocol has ever been able to repair.
- **[worker]** B3 done — `lastObservedSeq`, `reattach()`, `pumpGeneration` threaded through `runPump`/`routeFromPump`/`finishPump`, three new error cases, withdrawn claim corrected at five sites. **Reported an honest failure to falsify the stale-pump test**: removing the generation guard did *not* make it fail, across ~40 runs and several forcing strategies, because `GatewayConnection.teardown` finishes its stream **synchronously** before `await transport.close()`, so `closeDelay`/`isReceiverWaiting()` cannot gate the moment that matters. Documented the limit in the test rather than claiming a pass. **Deviation reported, not hidden:** `close()` also bumps `pumpGeneration`, and its `finishPump` was reordered to run *before* awaiting teardown. 306/306. → @reviewer
- **[reviewer]** **Request changes — the blocker is the negative's *scope*, not its honesty.** Both `close()` deviations judged **sound and kept**: the generation bump is required for D2 to hold when `close()` is followed by a plain `attach()` (nothing else advances the counter between them), and the reorder matches `GatewayConnection.close()`'s own precedent — B2's `closingWhileAnAttachHandshakeIsStillPendingUnblocksItWithADistinctError` was traced through *both* orderings and still means what it meant. **But the test parks the stale pump idle with nothing buffered — the wrong precondition for the other door.** `routeFromPump` is generation-scoped too, and a stale pump **yielding buffered items** into the new stream over-advances `lastObservedSeq` past what the consumer received — worse than a spurious finish, and needing no scheduling luck. Its reachability turns on the worker's own reorder: via a natural drop it is **structurally impossible** (`isAttached` flips only inside `finishPump`, which runs only after the pump has drained), but `close()` now flips it synchronously *before* the drain, and `pumpTask?.cancel()` does not stop an already-buffered `for await` — cancellation is cooperative and `AsyncThrowingStream` does not check it.
