- **[reviewer]** **Approve.** Verified `enqueueBatch` is a pure superset that changes no existing test's semantics. **Confirmed the overwrite claim and traced its exact mechanism**, which is sharper than the comment's phrasing: the *only* window in which a stale item reaches the unconditional `lastObservedSeq += 1` is the gap between `close()`'s `finishPump` (which nils **both** `attachWaiter` and `outputContinuation`) and `performAttach`'s reassignment — where the yield is a no-op against a `nil` continuation, so the corruption is silent *and undelivered*; and the new `attached` frame's `lastObservedSeq = headSeq` is an **unconditional overwrite**, not a delta. **So any assertion taken after `reattach()` returns structurally cannot observe this channel — the success path that makes the assertion reachable is the same path that resets the value.** That is a property of the design, not a gap in the test-writing. One theoretical exception recorded and judged not worth a test: `lastObservedSeq` is `public private(set)` and readable mid-`reattach()` via actor reentrancy.
- **[architect]** **I proposed replacing the unfalsifiable guard with a provable one and was corrected — recording that, because the correction is the useful part.** My proposal: have `close()`/`reattach()` `await pumpTask?.value`, so a superseded pump is *provably stopped* rather than out-voted by a counter, converting an untestable guard into the testable property "`close()` does not return while its pump is still running". The reviewer confirmed the wait is **genuinely bounded** — categorically unlike the wait `GatewayConnection.close()` refused, which was on an external, possibly-never-unblocking `URLSessionWebSocketTask.receive()`, whereas `pumpTask` blocks on `AsyncThrowingStream.next()`, which the runtime guarantees resolves once `finish()` is called. **But it does not replace the guard**, and my framing was wrong: it delays only `close()`'s *return*; `isAttached` still flips *before* the wait, so a caller reaching `reattach()` by actor reentrancy while that wait is suspended still finds `isAttached == false` and can still race a live stale pump. **Both existing tests exercise exactly that reentrant shape**, not the sequential one. The variant that *would* close the reentrant window — delaying `isAttached`'s flip until after the drain — reintroduces the promptness regression this block's reorder just fixed, scaled by backlog size. **Disposition: land as-is; `await pumpTask?.value` is a clean follow-up with its own deterministic test, not a gate on this block.**

They are genuine regression checks with honestly disclosed limits. **The generalisable rule: a test whose falsification attempt failed is worth keeping when its doc comment says so; the defect is never the negative result, it is a comment scoped wider than the attempt behind it.**

- **[architect]** **Block B4 = task 7.4 — turn submission and incremental rendering. Two facts checked in the core before briefing, both of which change what the block must build.**
  1. **`turnEnd` is NOT guaranteed after a submit, so "complete the turn on `turnEnd`" is an incomplete rule.** `TurnHandler.SubmitAsync` (`core/Dmon.Core/Rpc/TurnHandler.cs:112-122`) fails `_turnGate.WaitAsync(0)` on a concurrent submit, emits `ErrorEvent{code:"turnInProgress", recoverable:true}` and **returns** — *before* `RunTurnAsync`, which is where **both** `turnStart` (`:291`) and `turnEnd` (`:518`) are emitted. So that turn produces **an error event and no turn lifecycle events at all**. A renderer completing a turn only on `turnEnd` wedges forever on the most ordinary user action there is: **typing while the agent is still replying.** Also checked, because assuming either way would have been guessing: a **cancelled** turn *does* still emit `turnEnd` (the `OperationCanceledException` catch breaks out of the loop and falls through to the emit), but an exception escaping `SubmitAsync` does not — its `try` has a `finally` and no `catch`. **So the projection must treat an ADR-003 `error` event as turn-terminating, not merely informational.**
  2. **The client cannot send an ADR-003 command today.** `GatewayConnection.send(_:)` accepts a `GatewayControlFrame` only. B4 is therefore **explicitly permitted** to add a raw-frame send to `GatewayConnection.swift` — the first block of section 7 allowed to touch it. It must be a *separate, clearly named* method, never a widening of the control-frame one, so the two wires stay distinguishable at every call site.
  - **Design: the projection is stateless.** A pure `GatewayInboundItem -> TurnEvent?` mapping, holding no "is a turn in flight" state of its own. Stateful projections are how a renderer's model silently desynchronises from the wire, and this section has spent three blocks on exactly that class of bug in the session actor; there is no reason to grow a second instance of it in the renderer. §8 holds whatever UI state it needs.
  - **Design: unmodelled events project to `nil`, not to an `.unrecognised` case.** Nothing is lost — the raw `.event` items remain on the session stream, and the projection is applied *by* the consumer rather than replacing the stream. That is the difference from §6's `.unrecognizedControl`, which had to exist because a mis-routed control frame would have corrupted the sequence count; here the raw item is still right there.
  - **Design: no wait-for-ack turnstile.** `ack` is a receipt, not a window — nothing on the host gates on it, and a duplicate id is re-acked *without* re-writing to the core. Building a turnstile would invent flow control the protocol does not have.
  - **`submitTurn` carries the not-attached gate**, which is where `## NEXT`'s standing tripwire says it belongs: *"8.1/8.2 — the attach-state gate is what the spec asserts. That belongs in `GatewayClient`."* §8.2 consumes it rather than reimplementing it in the app shell.

- **[architect]** **A new cross-language coupling, second of its kind, and the near-miss is the point.** `TurnCommandCodec` emits **sorted keys**, so `"type"` lands **last**. §6 verified the *gateway's* `ControlFrameSerializer` tolerates an out-of-position discriminator — but **ADR-003 commands never reach that parser.** They are forwarded byte-unchanged to the **core**, which deserializes them through `System.Text.Json` polymorphism (`CommandDispatcher.cs:201`), a parser with a historic first-property requirement. It works **only** because `WireSerializerOptions.cs:34` sets `AllowOutOfOrderMetadataProperties = true`, whose own doc comment says commands carry `id` before `type` and the deserializer must tolerate it. **If that flag ever flips, every turn this client submits fails and no Swift test can see it** — the Swift side is correct in both worlds. Now recorded in `TurnCommand.swift` at the point the sorted-key decision is made. **Generalisation: §6's discriminator-tolerance finding was not one fact about one parser — it is a standing hazard at every wire boundary this client writes to, and each boundary must be checked against the parser that actually reads it, not against the last one that was checked.**
- **[architect]** **A .NET-side defect found while documenting, parked as `tech-debt/turn-submit-can-produce-no-event-at-all.md`.** A `turn.submit` cancelled before `TurnStartEvent` reaches the wire yields **nothing at all** — `SubmitAsync` has a `finally` and no `catch`, so the `OperationCanceledException` reaches `RunGuardedAsync`'s `catch (OperationCanceledException) { }` and is swallowed. Every other outcome puts *something* on the wire; this one is silence, and **a client cannot distinguish "this submit produced nothing and never will" from "this turn is slow."** Reachable ordinarily — `turn.abort` dispatches inline against the same `_turnCts`. The asymmetry that makes it worth fixing at source: **the host knows the submit is over — it is sitting in a `catch` — and chooses to say nothing.** Protocol-visible to fix, so it wants a spec delta, not a quiet change. Out of scope here (this change adds no .NET behaviour).

Corrected to name disk I/O. **A tech-debt note is read by someone budgeting work against it, so an overstated mechanism there is more expensive than an overstated comment in code — the register says so itself, and it took an auditor to catch me applying it.**

- **[architect]** **Block B5 — the device-key connect flow. No task numbers; it closes the two scenarios §6 deferred to "when §7 wires the flow".** Three decisions, one of which changes B2's API and needs its reasoning on the record.
  1. **The transport factory becomes `async throws`.** `GatewaySession` currently takes `@Sendable () -> any GatewayTransport` — synchronous and non-throwing — but resolving a credential reads a file and the Keychain, and may refuse. The alternative was resolving auth **once** before constructing the session and capturing the headers in the closure. **Rejected, and for the reason §6 built five outcomes in the first place:** a credential revoked between connections would then be re-presented on every reattach and come back as a bare **401 with nothing naming the cause** — precisely the failure `DeviceAuthDecision`'s five states exist to prevent. Making the factory `async throws` means every connection re-resolves, so a revocation surfaces as `.keyRevoked` with its escape hatch instead of an unexplained rejection. The single-flight guard is unaffected: `attachInFlight` is set in the `attach`/`reattach` wrappers **before** `performAttach` is entered, so moving a suspension point inside it changes nothing.
  2. **The `secretHash` half of the credential-mismatch gap closes here** — §6's supervisor narrowed it and said plainly not to let the record read as though B8 finished it. A held credential whose `keyId` is active but whose `secretHash` in the file has been rotated or restored still returns `.presentKey` today and 401s at connect. `status(ofKeyId:)` already has `entry.secretHash` in hand and `DeviceKeySecret.secretHash` is available, so **this is a comparison, not a design.**
  3. **`DeviceKeyProvisioner` moves to an injected `DevicesFileReader`.** §6's supervisor parked the two-styles-for-one-collaborator question with "injected is the one to keep, decided when a third consumer appears" — **B5 is that third consumer.**

The dependency runs one way (`DeviceKeys` → `GatewayClient`) and D16's iOS build gate is what enforces it; putting the join in `GatewayClient` would drag `devices.json` and the Keychain into the module that must lift onto iOS unmodified.

- **[supervisor]** **Request changes on section 7 — five blockers, `88e5424..7c818db`. All five span blocks; none is visible in a single diff, and two defeat a requirement 7.3 explicitly claims in both scenarios.**
  1. **The derived cursor double-counts every replay window.** `lastObservedSeq = attachedFrame.headSeq` then `+1` per `.event` — but **the host sends `headSeq` *and then* replays `(lastSeq, headSeq]`**, so a reattach with `lastSeq = L` against `headSeq = H` seeds at `H` and counts the `H − L` replayed events on top: final cursor **`2H − L`**. The **second** reattach then sends a `lastSeq` above `headSeq`, the host silently clamps it and replays **nothing** — every event missed in that drop is lost, with no error anywhere. **Reattach-and-replay works exactly once, with an empty backlog.**
  2. **Events racing ahead of `attached` are silently discarded.** While `attachWaiter` is set, any non-`attached` item is dropped — justified by a comment claiming the host always answers `attached` first. **`NetworkConnectionEndpoint.cs:299-303` says the opposite**, and says why: `Attach()` has already released the pump's wake, so the first buffered event may be draining concurrently. On **every reattach**, a replayed event can beat `attached` and this client throws it away. Blocker 1 currently masks it.
  3. **A failed handshake wipes `sessionId`, making reattach one-shot.** `performAttach`'s rollback is correct for a first `attach` and destructive for `reattach()`, which drives it over state that already exists — one dropped connection before `attached` (*the* transient case reattach exists for) and every later `reattach()` throws `.reattachWithoutPriorAttach`, an error that also misdescribes what happened.
  4. **`attach()` after a drop orphans the dead connection; `reattach()` doesn't.** `finishPump` clears `isAttached` but leaves `connection` non-nil; `reattach()` closes it, `attach()` overwrites it. Two sibling public entry points, same precondition, different teardown discipline — and the leak shape already in `tech-debt/websocket-receive-cancellation-leak.md`.
  5. **The comment-accuracy defect is patched, not fixed — "whack-a-mole".** Four rounds closed four instances and **two more of the identical shape shipped in the same section**, one binding on §8: `TurnProjection.swift` claims `internalError` arrives *after* `turnStart` and any deltas, but `RunTurnAsync` calls `GetCurrentConfig()` at `TurnHandler.cs:256`, **35 lines before** `turnStart` at `:291`, and it throws on an empty registry (pinned by `ProviderRegistryTests.cs:201-205`). **`error` with no `turnStart` is an ordinary outcome — the most likely first-run one.** The *set* of four turn outcomes survives; the *characterisation* of outcome (2) does not, and that is what §8 keys off.
  - **Rulings requested:** 7.1's disposition **upheld**, verified independently (`ChildInventory.swift:18` is the only spawn descriptor; `tailscale` is a `.process` check and those are unimplemented). `GatewayConnection`/`GatewaySession` duplication **earned** — different abstractions, since the session needs generation-scoping the connection has no use for; **trigger for extraction: a third "task + continuation + finish-once" actor.** No two state fields encode the same fact, **but the invariant "which connection is current" is held in three places that can disagree** — `connection`, `isAttached`, `pumpGeneration` — and Blocker 4 is that disagreement already costing something.
- **[architect]** **Blocker 1 is my error, and it is the most instructive thing in this section.** `## NEXT` recorded *"seed from `attached.headSeq`, then +1 per received frame with no `gw`"* as **"SETTLED IN B3, binding on anything that touches the cursor"** — stated with the confidence of a traced fact. It is **right for a first attach** (`lastSeq: 0`, `headSeq: 0`, no replay) and **wrong for every reattach**, because I reasoned about the seed and the increment separately and never composed them against a **non-empty replay window**. **Every fixture in the section answers a reattach with `headSeq` equal to the `lastSeq` just sent**, so the suite could never disagree with me — §6's rule (*a green suite proves agreement with your fixtures, not fidelity*) landing on the Architect this time, in guidance marked binding.
- **[architect]** **Blocker 2 was in front of me at section open and I walked past it.** The scouting report that opened §7 quoted `NetworkConnectionEndpoint.cs:299-303` — the `attached` reply goes through the serialized funnel *because replay may already be draining* — and I recorded the *conclusion* I wanted from that read (create/attach need separate sockets) while dropping the fact sitting beside it. **Rule: when a source read yields a fact you did not go looking for, record it before acting on the one you did.**

It must add the four tests the section never had, chief among them **a reattach with a genuinely non-empty replay window**, and re-reason `GatewaySessionTests.swift:815-817`, which currently credits the unconditional cursor reset with "healing" the stale-pump corruption — removing the reset removes that incidental healing, and the generation guard is the real protection.
- **[worker]** Remediation done — all five findings. **Cursor** now seeds `min(max(lastSeq, 0), attachedFrame.headSeq)`, with `lastSeq` (what *this* attach sent) threaded through `runPump`/`routeFromPump`, mirroring the host's own `Math.Clamp`. **Pre-`attached` items** are forwarded and counted via a `pendingReplayEventCount` added onto the seed when `attached` lands; `attached` itself still never yielded. **`sessionId`** assigned only on success (chosen over assign-then-roll-back: smaller diff, no window where a forgotten rollback regresses). **`attach()`** now captures and closes the stale connection like `reattach()`, minus the generation bump. Both comments rewritten with `file:line` citations. **`GatewayConnection.swift` not touched.** 345/345.

## 8. UI text loop

- **[architect]** Base: `06c1e35` — the first section with an app-level call site: typed turn input, a transcript that renders the streamed reply incrementally, submission refused and *surfaced* when unattached, and child health plus gateway connection state rendered beside the transcript so a failed turn can be attributed to the right layer.
- **[architect]** **Product Owner decision opening the section (2026-08-09): auto-connect once, manual reconnect.** The app attempts `create`→`attach` exactly once, when the Network Gateway child first reports `healthy` — not at launch, because a cold spawn takes up to `HostRuntime`'s 30s health-check interval to answer and an attach before that fails for a reason that is not interesting. On a drop the UI shows the drop's own reason and offers an explicit reconnect that calls `reattach()`. **No retry loop, no backoff, no give-up policy** — declined for the same reason §7 declined it (no task or scenario specifies the values, and it would stand up a second backoff beside `Supervisor/RestartBackoff.swift`), and with one new reason of its own: an automatic retry against a **refused device key** (`DeviceAuthConnectionRefused`) would loop against a 401 that no amount of retrying fixes. Matches PRD §7.4's posture for the iOS client — *"shows daemon unreachable honestly and queues user input for delivery on reconnect. It does not spin."* — minus the queueing, which is that client's scope, not this one's.
- **[architect]** **Section 8's block plan, four blocks.** B1 `TurnTranscript`, the pure reducer in `GatewayClient` (the four-outcome rule, incremental deltas, the refusal surface) · B2 `SessionCoordinator` in `GatewayClient` (owns the `GatewaySession`, drives the stream, publishes transcript + connection state, exposes connect/submit/reattach) · B3 the app target: `DeviceKeys` wired into `project.yml`, the production `AuthenticatedTransportFactory` constructed for the first time, text input and transcript view — **ticks 8.1 and 8.2** · B4 = 8.3, health and connection state beside the transcript, plus the `.launchNotDecided`/`.executableNotResolved`/`.spawnFailed` host-log gap §5 parked for exactly this task.
- **[architect]** **Shape follows §5's precedent deliberately, and it is what keeps the testability tripwire satisfied.** The package publishes an `AsyncStream` of state; a thin `@Observable` mirror in the app target consumes it and stores the latest value — exactly what `ChildStatusObserver` and `ChildLogObserver` already do for `HostRuntime`. So **`Observation` and SwiftUI stay out of `GatewayClient`** (which must still build for a generic iOS destination under `make dmon-home-ios-check`, and which design D16 shapes for extraction), and **every assertion a spec scenario makes is evaluated by `swift test`**, never by running the app: the incremental rendering in B1, the refusal surface in B1/B2, the connection-state discriminator in B2. The app target keeps only wiring and views — the §4 line, unchanged: *it may hold AppKit-protocol wiring and at-most-once guards; it may not hold a decision a spec scenario asserts on, nor a policy value not derived from a package type.*
- **[architect]** **The silence rule, settled before B1 is briefed — nothing in this UI ever waits on a terminal event.** §7 hands forward four outcomes of a submitted turn, one of which puts **nothing on the wire at all**, and the supervisor's fifth (a turn parked on an unanswered `tool.confirmRequest`) is indistinguishable from it. The rule is therefore structural rather than temporal: **no affordance is gated on a turn completing.** The input is never disabled while a turn is open, no timer marks a turn stalled, and an open turn renders as an honest *"awaiting response"* rather than a spinner that implies progress. A genuinely concurrent submit is answered by the host itself with `error{code:"turnInProgress"}`, which the transcript renders against the open turn — so the failure mode of guessing wrong is a visible error, not a wedge. **`tool.confirmRequest` is deliberately not modelled** (§8 builds no way to answer one, and surfacing *"waiting for approval"* with no approve button is a wedge with a label); it lands with the section that builds the prompt surface, and is owed in `## NEXT`.
- **[architect]** **B1 brief — `TurnTranscript`, the pure reducer (`GatewayClient`).** No task number of its own; it is the package half of 8.1 and 8.2, and it ticks nothing. Deliverable: a value type that folds `TurnEvent`s into an ordered transcript, with the four-outcome rule and the refusal surface built in, tested by `swift test` alone. Explicitly out of scope: `GatewaySession`, any networking, connection state (B2), SwiftUI, the app target, `project.yml`. `TurnProjection` stays **stateless** — B1 adds no state to it.
- **[architect]** **Attribution is positional, and that is a property of the wire, not a simplification.** Streaming events carry **no correlation id** — `core/Dmon.Protocol/Events/Event.cs`'s own doc comment is explicit that only `ResultEvent` subclasses carry the originating command's `id`, and `TurnStartEvent`/`MessageDeltaEvent`/`TurnEndEvent`/`ErrorEvent` are not among them. So the transcript attributes events to the **open turn**, and the thing that makes that sound is the host's own turn gate: `TurnHandler.SubmitAsync` refuses a concurrent submit with `turnInProgress` rather than interleaving two turns' events on one session. **If a later change ever admits concurrent turns, this reducer breaks silently** — it is written down here because no Swift test can observe that day arriving.
- **[worker]** **B1 delivered.** `home/Sources/GatewayClient/TurnTranscript.swift` (`TranscriptEntry` + `TurnTranscript`) and `home/Tests/GatewayClientTests/TurnTranscriptTests.swift`, 13 tests. `Foundation` only; no clock, no `Task`, nothing `async`. One deviation from the brief's type sketch, disclosed and judged: `TranscriptEntry.text`/`state` are `public private(set)`, which a sibling type in the same file cannot mutate, so mutation goes through four `fileprivate` helpers plus a `fileprivate init`. **Reviewer approved it** — the external surface is byte-identical to the sketch, `internal` would leak mutation to the whole module for nothing, and `public` would let a consumer bypass the read-only contract.
- **[reviewer]** **Round 1 — request changes: one blocker, and it is this change's signature shape for the fourth time.** The module doc claimed `TurnEvent.failed`'s comment *"establishes that a submitted turn has four distinct outcomes"* and then listed a set that **dropped** the no-`turnStart` failure — the shape that doc calls *"the most likely outcome on a first run with no provider configured, not an exotic edge case"* — and **promoted** ordinary cancellation, which that same doc explicitly excludes from `.failed` because it reaches `turnEnd`. No runtime consequence (`apply(.failed)` treats every shape identically) — a **false citation** that a future reader would trust. Fixed by dropping the citation framing entirely and stating the operative fact directly, which is the version that cites nothing it must then keep true.
- **[architect]** **Two further findings from my own read of the block, neither reachable from the diff alone — the §7 rule paying out again** (*after a block lands, read it for what its own tests are structurally unable to observe*).
  1. **The refusal surface rendered empty, in exactly the case 8.2 exists for.** `recordSubmissionRefused` put the reason in `.refused(reason:)` but left the notice's `text` as `""`, while `apply(.failed)`'s no-open-turn branch put its message in **both**. Two notice conventions in one type — and B3 renders `entry.text`, so a refused submission would have drawn a **blank bubble**: literally *"failing silently"*, which is the words the scenario forbids. The existing test asserted the state and never the text, which is why a green suite said nothing. Both paths now carry the reason as `text`, and the test asserts it.
  2. **`openTurn` resurrected abandoned turns, and its doc comment denied it.** `entries.last { isOpen($0.state) }` composes badly with this section's own silence rule: submit A, A produces nothing on the wire ever and stays `.awaitingResponse` **permanently** (correct, deliberate); submit B; B ends; the scan then walks back past the ended B and names **A** open again, so the next event folds into a turn that ended long ago. The doc asserted `openTurn` is `nil` "once the most recently submitted turn has reached `.ended` or `.failed`" — **false** in precisely that state. **Fixed as behaviour, not prose:** an explicit `openTurnID`, set by `recordSubmittedTurn` and by both no-open-turn synthesis branches, cleared by every terminal fold. An abandoned entry stops being *named* while staying `.awaitingResponse` — no `.abandoned` state invented, because we genuinely never learned that turn's outcome and permanent-awaiting is the honest rendering.
  - **Worth keeping: it took composing two features to see either one.** Both are single-feature-invisible — the empty notice needs *this reducer* plus *a view that does not exist yet*, and the resurrection needs *the silence rule* plus *a backwards scan*. A diff-local reviewer given only the diff has neither second half in hand. That is the argument for the Architect reading the block, not a redundancy.

On the `openTurnID` change specifically — the one that trades a derived value for stored state — traced every mutation site: set on all three opening paths, cleared in the same branch as every terminal transition, so no window exists where a stale id survives; ids are monotonic and never reused, so a non-`nil` id always names a live entry; and it could construct no two reachable transcripts with equal `entries` but differing `openTurnID`, so its presence in the synthesised `Hashable` introduces no divergence. Confirmed the two new tests fail against the old scan rather than passing incidentally.

- **[architect]** **B2 brief — `SessionCoordinator` (`GatewayClient`).** Deliverable: the actor that owns a `GatewaySession`, drives its inbound stream into a `TurnTranscript`, and publishes transcript + connection state as one snapshot feed — so the app target consumes a stream and stores a value, deciding nothing. Also the package half of the PO's connect policy: the coordinator supplies at-most-once `connect()`, `submit(_:)`, `reattach()` and `close()` **with the guards**; the *trigger* (gateway child reports healthy) is B3's, because it needs `HostRuntime` state `GatewayClient` must never see. The other three (`ChildHealthStore`, `ChildSupervisionStore`, `ChildLogStore`) all live in `Supervisor`; this one lives in `GatewayClient`, which **depends on nothing else in `home/` and must keep building for a generic iOS destination** (design D16, the module's own portability requirement, enforced by `make dmon-home-ios-check`). Extracting a shared publisher would mean either `GatewayClient` importing `Supervisor` — which breaks the portability gate outright — or standing up a fifth module to hold twenty lines of plumbing. **The trigger's premise is that the duplicates can be collapsed into one place; here they structurally cannot.** Recorded now so the section review costs no round on it.
- **[architect]** **The transport's three distinct facts must survive into the published state, not be flattened.** §6 handed forward that the transport reports `.notConnected`, `.closedLocally` and `.closed(code:reason:)` carrying 4400/4404/4409/4500/1009, and that the connection actor propagates them rather than re-wrapping generically — *"that distinction is what lets a caller tell 'superseded by a newer attach' (4409) from 'the core failed' (4500) from 'we hung up', and it is the discriminator 8.3 needs to attribute a failed turn to the right layer."* So B2's disconnect cause carries the `GatewayCloseCode` itself, never a pre-rendered string. **8.3 is the consumer that fact was recorded for; B2 is where it either survives or is quietly lost.**
- **[worker]** **B2 delivered.** `home/Sources/GatewayClient/SessionCoordinator.swift` (`GatewayConnectionState` with nested `DisconnectCause`/`ConnectFailure`, `SessionSnapshot`, the `SessionCoordinator` actor) and `SessionCoordinatorTests.swift`. `Foundation` only. Two self-flagged items disclosed rather than buried: the `describe(_:)` cast-through-`Any` (a compiler "always succeeds" warning the worker judged a false positive), and an `isClosing` race analysis for `close()` racing its own consuming task.
- **[reviewer]** **Round 1 — request changes: four blockers.** Two confirmed hypotheses I handed it, two found independently.
  1. **A failed `reattach()` permanently locked out the only recovery that preserves the session.** `reattach()` guarded `.dropped` but landed on `.connectFailed` when it failed — a state it did not accept — so the second attempt was a permanent silent no-op, and the only remaining legal call was `connect()`, i.e. `session.start()` → a **brand-new session at `lastSeq: 0`**, discarding the resumable one and the whole cursor apparatus §7 spent three blocks on. And `performAttach`'s own doc calls a mid-reattach handshake failure *"the ordinary transient case `reattach()` exists to recover from"* — so **the common path cost the user their conversation.** Fixed by widening the guard to `.dropped, .connectFailed` and letting `GatewaySession`'s own guards refuse the inapplicable case — the same *don't duplicate a gate that already exists one layer down* rule the block already followed for `.notAttached`. Reviewer verified the widening against those guards rather than accepting the reasoning.
  2. **`.dropped`'s doc comment contradicted the switch three lines away — and asserted away the hazard.** It said *"`connect()` is not [legal] (see its own doc comment)"* while `connect()`'s switch listed `.dropped` as legal and `connect()`'s own doc said so in as many words. **Sixth instance of this shape in the change and the worst variant yet:** not merely misdescribing something unrelated, but telling a B3 implementer not to worry about the precise hazard that would bite them. Fixed at the case itself — where a caller choosing between the two verbs actually looks.
  3. **`describe(_:)`'s fallback was asserted in prose and pinned by nothing.** Every test reaching it either short-circuited earlier or exercised the *conforming* double, so the half of the `Any`-cast claim that matters — that a non-conforming error genuinely falls through — was verified only in an isolated package that does not ship. A double proves the good path; only a bare non-conforming error proves the fall-through.
  4. **`close()`'s published outcome was never asserted.** The `isClosing` mechanism exists solely to keep `.dropped(.closedLocally)` from being clobbered by a racing `.streamEnded`, and needs a paragraph to justify itself; no test called `close()` and looked at the result.
  - **⚠️ THIS SUB-BULLET WAS WRONG, AND B3 PROVED IT BY FORCING — see §8's B3 posts.** The "no window exists" conclusion below held only for the actor's *synchronous* regions; it silently generalised that to the **ordering of two independent tasks' resumptions**, which Swift does not guarantee. B3's first post-`close()` test failed at roughly **even odds**. Corrected in code by `close()` awaiting the prior consuming task before it publishes. **The reasoning error is the lesson, not the bug:** both the reviewer and I traced the synchronous regions correctly and then treated that as covering something it does not cover. Original text kept below, struck, because *what we believed and why* is the useful record. ~~**The race analysis itself was confirmed, and one clause of its prose refuted.**~~ **The race analysis itself was confirmed, and one clause of its prose refuted.** `isClosing` is set before the only suspension point and reset with no `await` before the final publish, so no window exists. But the doc implied `consumingTask?.cancel()` stops the loop — **it does not**: a closure-based `AsyncThrowingStream` does not propagate cancellation into `for try await`, so what ends the loop is `finishPump(throwing: nil, …)` finishing the continuation cleanly, and via `close()` the consumer takes its **normal end-of-stream branch, not the catch branch.** Corrected. A paragraph that exists to justify a race must not misattribute the mechanism.

So `((error as Any) as? CustomStringConvertible)?.description ?? String(describing: error)` is behaviourally identical to a plain `String(describing: error)`, for both the conforming and non-conforming case — which is also why neither round's test can distinguish them: **there is nothing to distinguish.** Not a defect and the doc states nothing false (it accurately justifies the `as Any` widening *given* the direct cast). But it means the block carries a paragraph justifying a workaround for a mechanism that does no work. → `## NEXT`, and note it does **not** threaten B3's plan to conform `DeviceAuthConnectionRefused` to `CustomStringConvertible`: that conformance is exactly what `String(describing:)` picks up on its own.

- **[architect]** **THE FLAKE IS IDENTIFIED, and the Product Owner has approved fixing it now in its own block (2026-08-09).** Captured on the reviewer's third gate run — the first time in three sightings anyone caught it in the act:
  ```
  ✘ aSupersededPumpsBufferedBacklogDoesNotCorruptTheStreamOrCursorAReattachJustInstalled()
    GatewaySessionTests.swift:987:35: Expectation failed: await waitForTransport(factory, at: 1) → nil
  ```
  It is exactly the test `## NEXT` suspected. **Mechanism, now known rather than guessed:** `waitUntil`'s default timeout is **2 s** (`GatewaySessionTests.swift:101`), and `waitForTransport` inherits it — so the test is giving the *reattach connection* two seconds to appear while the pump is draining a **50,000-frame backlog** enqueued one line earlier, with `close()` and `reattach()` racing concurrently. **Nothing in the test discriminates on that 2 s**: its actual assertions are about cursor correctness and stream integrity. So this is a **readiness deadline, not a bound doing work** — which is precisely the distinction §5's rule turns on (*widen the gap, do not loosen a bound that a serial execution would also pass*). Widening it keeps the 50k stress intact and loosens nothing that discriminates. Scheduled as its own block after B2 — **PO's call, because it touches closed §7 code**; commit as `fix(home):`, no task number, ticks nothing.

- **[architect]** **Flake-block brief — widen a readiness deadline, and turn a hang-shaped regression into a failing one.** No task number; ticks nothing. Two items, both in test code only, no production source touched.
  - **Checked before briefing, because this change has twice been caught fixing an instance rather than the class:** the sibling test `aSupersededPumpsLateCompletionDoesNotFinishTheStreamAReattachJustInstalled` (`:827`) has the same *shape* — same factory, same `firstCloseDelay: .milliseconds(300)`, same `waitForTransport(factory, at: 1)` under the 2 s default — but **no 50 000-frame backlog**, so it is not exposed to the same degree and is deliberately left alone. The class is not "readiness waits in this suite"; it is **"a readiness deadline shorter than the work the test itself creates"**, and exactly one test creates that much work.
  - **The reviewer's `.timeLimit` nit is being *upgraded*, not applied as filed.** Adding `.timeLimit` to the lockout regression test would be decorative and, worse, self-deceiving: the hang it would supposedly bound is at `await iterator.next()`, a continuation await, which is **precisely the case `.timeLimit` is already documented not to bound** (`tech-debt/swift-testing-timelimit-does-not-bound-continuation-hangs.md`). Applying it as filed would install the exact false-protection claim `## NEXT` already complains about elsewhere. The real fix is to make the regression **fail at an assertion before anything can hang** — check the connection state and the factory's transport count *before* the blocking await.

Widened to **30 s** and recorded the near-miss in the test's own comment. **That observation is the most useful thing this block produced:** a readiness wait that genuinely exceeded 15 s means the 2 s default was never *marginal*, it was wrong by an order of magnitude, and anyone later "tidying" the constant back down needs to see that sentence. Scope held: the 50 000-frame backlog untouched, the pre-backlog `parked` wait left on the default, the shape-alike sibling (`:827`, no backlog) left alone.

It was right to read it that way and right to say so. But a regression test that has never been observed failing under the regression it guards is **exactly the unverified-protection claim this whole block exists to stop shipping**, so I authorised narrowing `reattach()`'s guard, running the test filtered, and reverting.
  - **The live-force immediately found a vacuous assertion in the restructure.** `secondConnectionOpened` compared `factory.count()` against the literal `2` — but the count is already **3** by then (create + first attach + the failed reattach), so **the check that exists to prove "a real connection was opened" would have passed no matter what.** Only the sibling check caught the regression. Now anchored to a `countBeforeSecondReattach` captured immediately before the call.

The cause was B2's `isClosing` mechanism, whose race analysis the reviewer traced in detail and **I recorded as confirmed**. It was not.
  - **The reasoning error, stated precisely, because it is more useful than the bug.** Both of us correctly established that no `await` separates the flag's reset from the publish, so nothing can interleave *within the actor's synchronous region*. We then treated that as covering **the ordering of two independent tasks' resumptions** — which Swift does not guarantee at all. The proof was correct; it just proved a smaller thing than we took it to prove. **A correct argument about the wrong scope reads exactly like a correct argument.**
  - Fixed by construction: `close()` captures the prior consuming task and `await`s its `.value` before clearing `isClosing` and publishing — bounded, because `session.close()` has already finished the stream that task reads. Both gotchas §7 recorded for the analogous `GatewaySession` follow-up hold here (capture before nilling; wait *after* the finishing call). Reviewer re-verified the no-task, double-`close()`, and already-completed cases.
- **[reviewer]** **Rounds 1–3 on the termination path — two blockers I raised as hypotheses, both confirmed, and one it found by forcing.**
  1. **`coordinator.close()` was on the wrong side of `shutdownForTermination()`** — the app killed `ndmon` (provably dead on return: `networkGateway` is `startupOrder: 0` and the only enabled child) and *then* closed the socket to the corpse. Reordered per task 4.5's reverse-dependency rule. **The reviewer also refuted my stated reason for it:** I claimed a close against a dead peer risks hanging; it traced the chain to `task.cancel(with: .normalClosure, reason: nil)`, a **non-blocking** cancel that never waits on the peer, so the peer's liveness changes nothing about duration. The reasons that survive are 4.5 symmetry, an honest causal story (the old order published a severed-socket `.dropped` that `close()` then relabelled `.closedLocally`), and that closing first sends a real close frame while the peer can still receive it — **quieter, not slower.** I had the conclusion right and the mechanism wrong; the comment at the call site carries only the verified reasons.
  2. **The connect trigger could fire mid-shutdown, and cancelling it was not enough.** The loop read no cancellation flag, and cancelling cannot wake a parked `for await` — worse, `shutdownForTermination()` cancels the forwarding tasks that feed `statusUpdates()` **last**, so the stream is *actively fed* throughout shutdown and a stale `.healthy` can genuinely fire `connect()` as the app tears down. Fixed in three layers, the load-bearing one being `SessionCoordinator.close()` becoming **terminal** — the single place where `connect()` and `close()` converge.
  3. **Then it forced the fix and found the same error one suspension later.** `connect()`'s new post-handshake `isClosed` check sat *before* `await session.sessionId` — a genuine cross-actor hop, since that property is not `nonisolated` — leaving a second window in which a completed `close()` is overwritten by `.attached`. It demonstrated this with a temporary delay and a real racing `close()`, then reverted. **Neither of the worker's two new tests could have caught it: both run `close()` to completion and *then* call `connect()`, purely sequentially.** Fixed by reading `sessionId` *before* the single final check, so nothing suspends between the guard and the mutation — a shape the reviewer judged better than the two-check alternative it had itself suggested, because it turns a per-suspension checklist (the thing that missed this window in the first place) into one locally verifiable question: *is there any `await` between the last guard and the mutation?*

The worker built a permanent seam for it (`raceWindowHookForTesting`, `nil` in production, isolated internal setter, invisible to the app target) and verified the new tests catch the bug by moving the check back to its broken position first — **a race test that cannot produce the race is decoration, which this section has twice caught.**

- **[supervisor]** **Request changes — two blockers, both invisible to every block review, and one of them is a product defect the section's own tests structurally cannot construct.**
  1. **`TurnTranscript`'s soundness precondition is enforced nowhere in the composed system, and ordinary use violates it.** Its module doc says positional attribution *"is sound only because the core's own turn gate refuses a concurrent submit"* — a true statement about the **core** and a false one about the **client as composed**. Three blocks each did the right thing in their own frame: B1's `recordSubmittedTurn` unconditionally reassigns `openTurnID`; B2's `submit` records on the strength of `submitTurn` **returning**, which happens when the frame is *written*, not accepted (it awaits no ack); B4's input is **deliberately** never disabled. `openTurn` is consulted by nothing outside the reducer — the supervisor grepped the coordinator and the whole app target. So: send "a", send "b" before "a" ends, and the core's `turnInProgress` refusal — which carries **no correlation id** — lands on whichever entry is open. "a" sits awaiting forever, "b" is marked failed, and "a"'s real reply folds into "b"'s row or becomes an orphan attached to no user message. **This defeats 8.3's stated purpose exactly: the turn layer reports a failure against the wrong turn.** Not exotic — turns run seconds to minutes, Send is never disabled by design, and impatient re-sends are normal for a companion host.
     - **Why no block review could see it:** B1's tests call `recordSubmittedTurn` then `apply` in the right order; B2's integration test enqueues reply frames only *after* `submit` returns, so the suite **cannot construct the interleaving**; B4's diff is a text field. Every diff correct, the sum not. This is the clearest example this change has produced of what a section review is for.
  2. **My "intermittent stall" reframing was also wrong — third wrong diagnosis of the same flake.** It is a **legitimate refusal on one branch of a race the test constructs and then asserts against.** `close()` sets `isAttached = false` inside its synchronous prelude; `reattach()`'s guard `!isAttached` is in *its* synchronous prelude. Two child tasks, one actor, two orderings: close-first → reattach proceeds → transport appears in ~0.35 s; **reattach-first → `.reattachWhileAttached` is thrown, no second transport is ever created**, and `waitForTransport` polls for something that cannot exist until the budget expires. That accounts for every observation *including* both "failed exactly at the mark" points, and for why the rate barely moved as the budget grew. **And it makes the surviving `## NEXT` advice actively harmful:** *"if it fires on CI, widen it again"* is unbounded futility under the real mechanism, and would cost a future investigation the time to re-derive all of this.
- **[architect]** **On being wrong three times about one test, and what the supervisor named as the rule.** 2 s → "slow under load" → widened; 30 s → "intermittent stall" → parked; now → a legal refusal the assertion never allowed for. **The rule: an observation that rules a hypothesis *out* does not thereby rule the next one *in*.** Bimodality genuinely refuted "slowness" — and I then treated the next plausible story as established without forcing it, which is the same *"correct argument about the wrong scope"* error this section already recorded twice, applied to a root cause instead of a guard. **Force the mechanism before recording the diagnosis** — the discipline §8 adopted for guards, now owed to causes.
- **[architect]** **Product Owner decision (2026-08-10), and it was the supervisor that insisted this was the PO's call, not the block's:** a local turn gate creates a lockout the reducer deliberately has no escape from — a turn that puts nothing on the wire leaves `openTurn` set forever, and the reducer is forbidden from inventing a timeout. **Chosen: gate the submit, plus an explicit "Abandon turn" action.** Rejected: escaping only via New/Resume Session (a silent turn would effectively end the session, and Resume tears down a healthy connection just to type again); and an auto-clearing countdown (no specified value, and it reintroduces exactly the guessing the four-outcome rule exists to prevent). **Note this does not contradict B1's refusal to invent an `.abandoned` state:** that refusal was about the reducer *inferring* a turn is over from silence. A user pressing a button is a **decision, not an inference** — the distinction is the whole justification, and it belongs in the new state's doc comment.
- **[worker]** **Remediation delivered.** `submit(_:)` refuses when a turn is open, through 8.2's existing refusal surface, **before touching `session` at all**. New `TranscriptEntry.State.abandoned` + `TurnTranscript.abandonOpenTurn()` + `SessionCoordinator.abandonOpenTurn()` + an "Abandon Turn" control shown only while a turn is open, labelled so it is clear it stops *this host's tracking* and does not cancel anything on the core. `TurnTranscript`'s module doc now attributes the invariant to the coordinator's gate rather than to the core's uncorrelatable refusal.

It proved the necessity by reverting to the post-await ordering and watching its new test fail with exactly the predicted shape (3 entries, an orphaned assistant), then restoring.

It first ran a synthetic benchmark (a bare `AsyncThrowingStream` draining 50 000 cheap comparisons in ~17–54 ms) and formed the opposite worry — that the real pump would fully drain inside the ~300 ms window, silently gutting the test. Then it **measured the real system**: temporary counters in `GatewaySession.routeFromPump`/`close()`, three runs, **0 of 50 000 items drained** by the time the new generation's first item routed, at 309–327 ms. The entire backlog is still outstanding when the new pump installs. **The sequencing preserves the stress; the synthetic estimate was wrong because per-item cost is dominated by actor-hop overhead across two actors, not by the trivial per-item logic.** Instrumentation reverted; I verified `git diff home/Sources/GatewayClient/GatewaySession.swift` is empty myself.
  - Also traced every exit from `submit(_:)` (turn open, write throws, `.notAttached`) and confirmed a converted entry is field-identical to a directly-recorded refusal except for id reuse, which the doc comment itself names rather than glosses. - **[architect]** **What this section actually cost, and what it bought — worth stating plainly for whoever reads this in the archive.** Three tasks, and the tasks were the easy part. **Four defects, and not one was found by reading code:** B1's refusal notice that rendered blank; the flake block's assertion that compared against a literal already true; `isClosing`'s race, which a reviewer traced and I confirmed as sound before a test disproved it at even odds; and the concurrent-submit misattribution, which **no block's tests could construct**, because each block's suite exercised its own frame correctly. Every one surfaced by **forcing the failure** — reverting a fix and watching the guard fail, instrumenting a pump instead of estimating it, driving a real racing `close()` into a real suspension window. **The section's rule, earned five times: for any guard whose job is to exclude a state, force that state once and watch the guard fail — and for any diagnosis, force the mechanism before recording it.** Its corollary, which cost the most to learn: **an argument can be correct about something adjacent to the thing it needs to be correct about, and reads identically either way.**

## 9. Protocol comment correction

- **[architect]** Base: `1ad4580` — two stale forward-looking annotations in the gateway control-frame DTOs corrected to describe what is now implemented.
- **[architect]** **Block 9.1 — one task, one file, comments only.** Both annotations promise a future group: `AttachedFrame.Generation`'s *"issued here but not enforced until Group 6"* and `AckFrame`'s *"generation/dedup logic is Group 5"*. Both are now implemented in `Dmon.Network` — `CommandAdmission { Accepted, Duplicate }` + `SessionHandler.TryAdmitCommand` back the dedup, and `SessionHandler.Attach` evicts/fences/aborts the prior connection with the forwarding loop gating inbound frames on generation.
  - **Scope is exactly the two annotations.** The repo carries ~20 other `Group N` references; the rest are **locative** (which change-group built a thing) and read correctly in present tense — including `SessionHandler.cs:81`'s *"Group 6 enforces fencing"*, which sits in the file that does enforce it. Only these two are **temporal** ("not enforced until", "is Group 5"), i.e. false statements about the present. Nothing else is touched.
  - **The one real hazard is a build break, not a wording call:** `Dmon.Protocol` does not reference `Dmon.Network` (the dependency runs the other way), so a `<see cref="SessionHandler"/>` would not compile under `TreatWarningsAsErrors`. Name the gateway-side types in `<c>` prose instead.

- **[reviewer]** **Round 1 — Approve with a nit, and the nit was the interesting part.** Verified both claims at the scope they assert rather than at an adjacent one: `enforceFencing` is `true` on every production call site (the sole `false` is `RunForwardingLoopForTestAsync`, `internal` and documented as legacy-test-only), so the unqualified *"only the connection holding the current generation may keep sending"* is accurate; and `_admittedIds` is unbounded for handler lifetime and untouched by `Detach`, so the dedup set survives exactly the drop-and-reattach in which a client would resend. **The nit:** the new `AckFrame` prose stated *"the resend is recognised as a duplicate rather than forwarded a second time"* as the universal outcome, but the compensation branch (`NetworkConnectionEndpoint.cs:695-710`) calls `RemoveAdmission` on a failed core write **precisely so** the next resend is *not* a duplicate — it is admitted and forwarded. The guarantee is true in both branches; the stated mechanism in only one.

Directed the fix at the guarantee rather than the mechanism, and ruled `AttachedFrame.Generation` out of the fix (already verified as written).
- **[worker]** `AckFrame` now leads with the guarantee and covers both branches — duplicate recognised and not re-forwarded when the original reached core, admitted and forwarded when it did not, *"at most once and never silently dropped"* either way. Gates re-run green. → @reviewer
- **[reviewer]** **Round 2 — Approve.** Checked the three ways the stronger wording could over-claim. **At-most-once holds by lock discipline, not timing:** `TryAdmitCommand` and `RemoveAdmission` share one critical section over a single `HashSet`, so a resend racing the compensation path serialises either before the `Remove` (`Duplicate`, no write) or after it (`Accepted`, exactly one write) — no window admits both. **Never-silently-dropped is a real client-observable signal, not bookkeeping:** a failed core write closes the socket with a distinct application close code `4500` / `"core write failed"` (the same pattern as fencing's `4409`), with `RemoveAdmission` firing *before* the close, so the client's reconnect-and-resend lands on a clean `Accepted`. **And a duplicate is still acked** (`NetworkConnectionEndpoint.cs:727-735` re-sends `AckFrame` without writing to core), so a client whose ack was lost does not hear silence the second time.
  - **Architectural note, non-blocking → `## NEXT`:** the guarantee's enforcement mechanism — the `4500`/`4409` close-code contract — appears nowhere in `openspec/specs/`. The DTO doc now makes a client-facing promise that a client SDK built from the spec alone could not discover.

- **[supervisor]** **Round 2 — Request changes, and escalate rather than carve a third.** Round 1's blocker is closed: the three surfaces agree, and the two `4500` sites are genuinely two distinct emission points. **But the remediation introduced a fresh false statement of the same shape as 9.1's** — a mechanism true in one branch, stated as universal. `README.md:251` (*"treat a missing ack as 'the core never got this command'"*) and `:274`'s gloss (*"(no ack was ever sent)"*) both equate **client observed no ack** with **core did not receive**. The ack is sent on the connection *after* `WriteToCoreAsync` succeeds, so any drop in that window — the ack's own send failing, a `4409` fence, a dead socket — leaves the core holding the command and the client ack-less. **That is the motivating case for reattach/dedup, not an edge case**, and `:274` asserts the client can *distinguish the two dedup branches* by whether it saw an ack, which is the one inference the design does not support.

That is the correct epistemic shape; the guide restated the contract correctly and then layered a diagnosis on top.

What is verified: unsandboxed succeeds (twice), sandboxed exits 1 with no diagnostic (twice), and `curl` to api.nuget.org returns 200 sandboxed. The mechanism — NuGet cache writes vs egress — is **not determined**, and I am not going to imply it is. - **[architect]** **What a one-task section cost, and the one thing worth carrying out of it.** Three tasks' worth of review on a single comments-only task, and **every round found a real defect in prose that had already been approved**: 9.1's first `AckFrame` rewrite stated a mechanism true in one of two branches; the remediation that fixed the *guide* then re-committed the identical error in a new clause; and my own DEVLOG post asserted a causal chain the build script disproves in three lines. **The pattern: a sentence describing a mechanism reads as true whenever the outcome it produces is true.** Nobody caught any of the three by reading — they were caught by checking the mechanism against the code, which is §8's rule ("force the mechanism, don't sample it") arriving in a section with no code to force. **Its corollary for docs: prose asserting *why* deserves the same scrutiny as code asserting *what*, and a claim about what a client can *infer* is the highest-risk sentence in any protocol document.**

- **PARKED BY THE §8 SUPERVISOR — six items, none blocking, all with their reasoning attached.**
  1. **`ChildSupervisionState` is missing a `neverStarted` state, and the supervisor disagreed with my decline — I accept the distinction.** I declined it on §4's "observation states earn no speculative widening" rule, the same rule that correctly killed the `ChildHealth` split. The difference: the `ChildHealth` split would have created observation surface **with no writer** (`HealthChecker` cannot distinguish timed-out from refused today), whereas `neverStarted` has **three real writers** — the exact `.launchNotDecided`/`.executableNotResolved`/`.spawnFailed` branches 8.3 just edited. Today the row reads *"unknown / normal"* — "wait and see" plus "nothing wrong" — directly above a log line saying the binary does not exist. **A summary that contradicts its own detail is worse than a missing one.**
  2. **The termination budget's derivation no longer describes the path it bounds.** `applicationShouldTerminate` now awaits `coordinator.close()` *before* `shutdownForTermination()`, but `terminationBudget` is still `worstCaseShutdownDuration + 15` and its comment still explains it as covering the shutdown alone. **Correct today** — the supervisor traced the new await and it is bounded — but §7's own `## NEXT` warned about exactly this shape, and the next thing added in front of that shutdown will not be checked unless the comment says what the budget now spans.
  3. **`SessionCoordinator` is the only one of the four actors with no generation guard.** `startConsuming` overwrites `consumingTask` without awaiting or cancelling the prior one, while `GatewaySession` carries `pumpGeneration` for the same hazard. Safe today — but the safety argument is **state-scoped protection against a task-scoped hazard**, which is the precise shape that already failed once here. Fold into the parked `await pumpTask?.value` item.
  4. **Restate §5's extraction trigger as "a fourth *within one module*"** — otherwise it fires again at the fifth and costs another paragraph to decline for the same reason (`GatewayClient` cannot import `Supervisor` without breaking the iOS gate).
  5. **Abandon is not a cancel.** No `turn.abort` is in scope, so the core keeps running an abandoned turn; its later deltas arrive with no open turn, synthesise a fresh entry via `apply`'s no-open-turn branch, and **that entry becomes the new open turn — re-locking the gate** until it ends or is abandoned again. Coherent and honest, and the Product Owner took the trade knowingly; recorded so nobody rediscovers it as a bug.
  6. **Two narrow residuals in the transcript.** `applyNeverProducesTheAbandonedStateForAnyEventVariant` drives all four `TurnEvent` cases but only against an **already-open** turn, so it would miss a regression planted in the no-open-turn synthesis branches. And if `close()` ever raced a `submit` whose write nonetheless succeeded, the freshly-opened turn would stay `.awaitingResponse` forever, since `close()` never touches the transcript — **pre-existing, identical under the old ordering**, and inert only because `close()`'s sole caller is app termination.
- **PARKED FROM §8 B2, with evidence — `SessionCoordinator.describe(_:)` is dead weight.** The reviewer ran a script: `String(describing:)` **already** prefers `CustomStringConvertible.description` when the dynamic type conforms, so the `((error as Any) as? CustomStringConvertible)?.description ?? String(describing: error)` form is behaviourally identical to a plain `String(describing: error)` — which is also why no test can distinguish them. Not a defect, and the doc states nothing false; but it is a paragraph of comment justifying a compiler-warning workaround for a mechanism that does no work. Simplifying deletes both. **Does not affect B3's `DeviceAuthConnectionRefused: CustomStringConvertible` conformance** — that is precisely what `String(describing:)` picks up unaided.
