- **SECTION 8'S BRIEF MUST CARRY THESE — they are what §7 hands forward, and three of them will otherwise be discovered by a wedged UI.**
  1. **A submitted turn has FOUR outcomes and one of them is silence** (see the bullet below). **A renderer that finalises only on a terminal `TurnEvent` hangs.** 8.1 needs a rule for the silence case that does not involve waiting.
  2. **`.failed` can arrive with partial text already rendered** (mid-turn `internalError`, no `turnEnd`) **and can also arrive with nothing rendered at all** (`GetCurrentConfig()` throws 35 lines before `turnStart` — the most likely *first-run* outcome). §8 must not read "nothing rendered yet" as "still running".
  3. **A fifth renderer state, from the supervisor:** a turn parked on an unanswered `tool.confirmRequest` produces no terminal event until answered — **and it looks identical to the silence case.** §7 models no prompts, so §8 is where this first bites.
  4. **8.2's attach-state gate already exists** — `GatewaySession.submitTurn` refuses with `.notAttached`. **Consume it; do not reimplement it in the app shell.** That placement was the standing tripwire's call.
  5. **§8 owns *when* to reattach.** §7 built the mechanism only, per the Product Owner. `reattach()` is refused while a connection is live and returns a **NEW** stream — the consumer re-subscribes.
  6. **The two deferred live proofs land in §8**, because §8 is the first section with an app-level call site. Recipe and the ad-hoc-signing keychain-prompt warning are in the bullet below.
- **✅ ACTUALLY RESOLVED, 2026-08-10 — and the two corrections below are both superseded. Read this bullet, not them.** The mechanism, found by the section supervisor and then **forced** rather than argued: `close()` clears `isAttached` inside its **synchronous prelude**; `reattach()`'s `guard !isAttached` is inside **its** synchronous prelude. The test spawned both as concurrent child tasks, so whichever reached the actor first decided everything — close-first → reattach proceeds → the second transport appears in ~0.35 s; **reattach-first → `.reattachWhileAttached` is thrown and no second transport is ever created**, leaving `waitForTransport(at: 1)` polling for something that could not exist until the budget expired. **It was never slowness and never a hang: the test asserted against a legal outcome of a race it constructed itself.** Fixed by awaiting `close()` to completion before spawning `reattach()`; the 50 000-frame backlog is untouched, and instrumentation confirmed **0 of 50 000 items drained** when the new pump installs, so the overlap the test exists to stress is fully intact. Timeout back down to 5 s against a measured 0.347–0.363 s. **41 consecutive clean full-suite runs.**
  - **⚠️ DELETE FROM §10's BRIEF: *"if it ever fires on CI, widen it again"*.** Under the real mechanism that advice is **unbounded futility** and would cost a future investigation the time to re-derive all of this. §10 inherits nothing from this item now except that the backlog size is deliberate.
  - **Three wrong diagnoses, and the rule that came out of it.** 2 s → "slow under load" → widened. 30 s → "intermittent stall" → parked. Both were *correct refutations* of the story before them and *unforced guesses* at the next one. **An observation that rules a hypothesis out does not thereby rule the next one in. Name the mechanism and force it before recording a diagnosis** — the discipline this section adopted for guards, owed equally to causes.
- ~~**⚠️ CORRECTION, 2026-08-10 — "RESOLVED" WAS WRONG, AND THE DIAGNOSIS UNDERNEATH IT WAS WRONG TOO.**~~ **(Superseded by the bullet above — the "stall" reading was itself wrong. Kept for the reasoning trail.)** The flake fired again during B3's review, **once in seven full-suite runs, consuming the entire widened 30 s budget before failing** (`GatewaySessionTests.swift:1025`, same assertion). Line up every observation and the "slow under load" hypothesis does not survive: at **2 s** it failed ~1 in 3; at **15 s** it failed *at the 15 s mark*; at **30 s** it failed *at the 30 s mark*. **It has consumed whatever budget it was given, every single time it failed** — while a passing run takes 0.35–1.5 s. That is **bimodal: it either completes almost instantly or it never completes at all.** Slowness would show a spread and would thin out as the budget grew; this does not. **So the correct reading is that this is an intermittent *stall*, not a slow operation, and widening the deadline was treating a hang as latency.** The 30 s stands (it costs nothing and a stall now fails in 30 s instead of 2 s), but **it is not a fix, and the remediation block's commit message and the entry below both overstate what was achieved.** What the block *did* legitimately fix is the second half — the vacuous assertion in the lockout test, which was a real defect found by forcing. **Do not widen the timeout again**; the next step is a diagnosis of why the reattach transport sometimes never appears at all, which is `GatewaySession`/`DelayedFirstTransportFactory` territory, not a timing constant. **This is also the second time in two days that a conclusion of mine was overturned by holding the observations side by side rather than one at a time** — the first being the §7 cursor rule.
- ~~**✅ RESOLVED IN §8 by the flake remediation block**~~ **— superseded by the correction directly above; kept because §10 still inherits its last line.** The deadline is now 30 s and 13 consecutive full-suite runs are clean, so this is no longer a live hazard for CI. What §10 must still carry: **a GitHub macOS runner is more loaded than a dev machine, not less**, and the value was chosen against *this* machine's worst case — one observed failure at 15 s on a cold build under 42 concurrent suites. If it ever fires on CI, **widen it again; do not shrink the 50 000-frame backlog**, which is the stress the test exists for. Original entry, kept because its reasoning is what the value rests on: ~~**CARRY INTO §10's CI BRIEF — a fifth item, and it will flake before it fails honestly.**~~ `aSupersededPumpsBufferedBacklogDoesNotCorruptTheStreamOrCursorAReattachJustInstalled` failed **1 run in 3** locally during the remediation review — `waitForTransport` timing out at its 2s budget under load from 41 concurrent suites — then passed on rerun and in isolation. Not a regression (only its fixture's `headSeq` changed), but it is a **50k-backlog stress test** whose own doc comment concedes it is hard to pin, and **a GitHub macOS runner is more loaded than a dev machine, not less.** Widen its budget or shrink the backlog before §10 wires this target into CI — and note the §5 precedent for the right fix: **widen the gap, do not loosen the bound back to something a serial execution would also pass.**
- **CARRY INTO §10's CI BRIEF — now four items, and this one is a correctness issue, not cosmetics.** `.timeLimit` **cannot** bound the two hang-shaped regression tests in `GatewaySessionTests.swift`; only an **external** timeout can. `dmon-home-test` is a bare `swift test` and `ci.yml` has no `timeout-minutes` at all, so **§10 is exactly when this stops being theoretical.** GitHub's default 360-minute job timeout would burn six hours before failing, and neither `timeout` nor `gtimeout` is installed locally, so it needs a dependency or a small watchdog. See `tech-debt/swift-testing-timelimit-does-not-bound-continuation-hangs.md`. **B2's test doc comment still asserts the protection it does not have** — fix it when that lands.
- **OWED, AND NOT CLOSED BY §7 — the two deferred live proofs slip to §8.** B5 built the connect flow but **no app-level call site exists**, so nothing yet constructs an `AuthenticatedTransportFactory` and drives a real socket. Still owed: **a real Keychain round trip across a process restart**, and **whether `Dmon.Network` actually accepts a credential this client provisioned** (the two-language contract — hex digest, field names, timestamp format, header form — which no Swift test can close). The recipe when §8 has a call site: run the app against a live `ndmon` with a populated `devices.json` and no Keychain item, confirm it self-provisions, appends a row and connects; then **revoke that `keyId` and confirm the next attempt refuses by name** with the `security delete-generic-password …` command rather than a bare 401. **Include the legacy-keychain ACL warning** — `KeychainDeviceKeySecretStore` sets neither `kSecAttrAccessible` nor `kSecUseDataProtectionKeychain`, so under ad-hoc signing expect a keychain-access prompt on **every rebuild**; that is not a bug, but unless the recipe says so the person running it will read it as one.
- **BINDING ON §8's RENDERER — a submitted turn has FOUR outcomes, and one of them is silence.** Argued exhaustive by the reviewer, with its reason: every path bottoms out in the turn gate, the loop's cancellation catch, an exception escaping the loop, or an exception escaping everything before `turnStart`. (1) `turnInProgress` — an `error` event **alone**, no `turnStart`, no `turnEnd`; (2) a mid-turn failure — `turnStart` → deltas → `error`, **no `turnEnd`**, so `.failed` can arrive **with partial text already rendered** and the renderer must finalise or discard it; (3) cancellation once `RunTurnAsync`'s loop is running — still reaches `turnEnd`; (4) **cancellation before `turnStart` reaches the wire — nothing at all on the wire, ever.** A renderer that finalises purely on a terminal `TurnEvent` **hangs** on (4). See `tech-debt/turn-submit-can-produce-no-event-at-all.md`.
- **FOLLOW-UP EARNED IN B3, deliberately not gating it — `close()` and `reattach()` should `await pumpTask?.value`.** Converts the **sequential** close-then-reattach case from "empirically resistant to forcing" to provably impossible, with its own simple deterministic test ("`close()` does not return while its pump is still running"). The wait is **bounded** — `pumpTask` blocks on `AsyncThrowingStream.next()`, which the runtime guarantees resolves once `finish()` is called, categorically unlike the external `URLSessionWebSocketTask.receive()` wait `GatewayConnection.close()` refused. **It does NOT replace the generation guard**: `isAttached` still flips before the wait, so a caller reaching `reattach()` by actor reentrancy can still race a live stale pump — which is the shape both existing tests exercise. Two implementation gotchas: capture the task into a local *before* `pumpTask = nil`, and place the wait *after* `connection.close()` (which triggers the synchronous `finish()` that unblocks the loop), or it hangs on live traffic. **If section 7 closes without this, it becomes a `tech-debt/` file rather than being lost with the change.**
- **BINDING ON B4/B5 — `create` and `attach` cannot share a connection** (evidence in the section-7 opening post), so `GatewaySession` takes a **transport factory** and each connection is a fresh one. **B5 injects the authenticated transport through that closure — it needs no change to `GatewaySession`.**
- **⚠️ CORRECTED — the cursor rule I recorded as "SETTLED IN B3" was WRONG, and the correction is binding.** ~~Seed from `attached.headSeq`, +1 per event.~~ That double-counts every replay window: the host sends `headSeq` **and then** replays `(lastSeq, headSeq]`, so seeding at `H` and counting `H − L` replayed events lands the cursor at **`2H − L`**, and the next reattach asks for more than exists, gets silently clamped, and **receives no replay at all**. **The rule is: seed from the `lastSeq` this attach actually sent, clamped to `attachedFrame.headSeq` — mirroring the host's own `Math.Clamp` — then +1 per received frame with no `gw`.** Counted at **yield** in `routeFromPump`; control frames never advance it, structurally, since `if case .event = item` is the only incrementing site. `lastSeq` is **exclusive**, out-of-range is silently clamped, the log is **never truncated**. **There is no marker between replayed and live events** — one server loop serves both, so the "replay seam" is *not observable from the client* and must never be tested for. **And a fact that belongs beside it: a replayed event can arrive BEFORE the `attached` frame** (`NetworkConnectionEndpoint.cs:299-303` — `Attach()` releases the pump's wake before the reply is funnelled), so anything consuming this stream must forward and count pre-`attached` items rather than assuming `attached` comes first.
- **KNOWN AND ACCEPTED — the stale-pump corruption channel.** A superseded pump can, in a narrow reentrant window, increment `lastObservedSeq` for an item it yields into a `nil` continuation — silent *and* undelivered. **The generation guard prevents it, and always was the only thing that did.** Neither manifestation could be forced from outside, across two rigorous attempts (~40 runs on the `finishPump` door; 3,000→1,000,000 frames × 20+ configurations on the `routeFromPump` door, guard removed and re-run 5×). **⚠️ CORRECTED by the §7 supervisor's re-review: the old justification here — that the channel was *unobservable* because `attached` unconditionally overwrote `lastObservedSeq = headSeq` — described a mechanism the remediation DELETED.** The seed now composes (`min(max(lastSeq,0), headSeq)` plus `pendingReplayEventCount`) rather than overwriting, so it **no longer coincidentally papers over a violation of the guard**. The conclusion is unchanged; the reason is not. `GatewaySessionTests.swift:923-947` states this correctly in the code — **this bullet is the copy the sweep missed**, which is precisely the *"scope a sweep by where the fact is duplicated, not by where the fix was"* rule this change earned in §3 and has now been caught by twice. **`## NEXT` is what survives into the archive, so a stale bullet here outlives the code that contradicts it.**
- **RAISED IN VALUE by the remediation — the parked `await pumpTask?.value` follow-up.** The old seed *incidentally* erased any stale-pump cursor corruption on every reattach; the new one does not. Defence in depth is genuinely thinner now — correctly so, and honestly documented — which makes converting the generation guard into a provable property worth more than when it was rated "not a gate on B3".
- **EXTRACTION TRIGGER for `attach`/`reattach`:** they now differ by exactly one line (`pumpGeneration += 1`). The underlying disagreement the supervisor named — *"which connection is current"* living in `connection`, `isAttached` and `pumpGeneration`, which can disagree — was resolved **for that one symptom only**, by duplicating the capture-and-close discipline rather than removing the representable bad state. A third entry point, **or a state enum (`.idle` / `.attaching` / `.attached(c)` / `.dropped(c)`) making "non-nil but dead" unrepresentable**, is the shape that retires both the duplication and the three-way invariant.
- **Minor, shared by both entry points, recorded so it is not rediscovered:** when `performAttach` throws *early* (`makeTransport`/`connect`/`send`), `self.connection` is left pointing at the connection just closed. Harmless — close is idempotent, `isAttached` is false, the next entry closes it again as a no-op — and pre-existing in `reattach()`.
- **Architect's own gate error, caught and recorded rather than quietly fixed.** B2's first `make test` was run as `make test 2>&1 | tail -12`. That does two bad things at once: it discards all but the last 12 lines (**3 of 20 suites visible**, so 17 suites' results were simply absent), and — because the pipeline's exit status is `tail`'s, not `make`'s — **it reports success regardless of whether the tests passed.** Section 6's `## NEXT` already carried the rule *"gate runs now preserve full test output rather than grepping the summary line"*, and I broke it one section later. B1's run was equally masked and only *happened* to show every suite passing. **Re-run with `set -o pipefail`, full output to a file, and the exit code echoed explicitly** — 20 suites, 0 failures, `MAKE_EXIT=0`. Standing rule for the rest of this change: **a gate is not green until its own exit code says so.**
- **A rule earned in B2, and it paid twice in one block: after a block lands, read it for what its own tests are structurally unable to observe, and hand that to the reviewer as a *hypothesis to confirm or refute*, not as a finding.** B2's concurrency blocker was invisible to a green 300-test suite because every test drives one `attach` at a time. Both of B2's blockers were found this way; neither was reachable from the diff alone.
- **Strengthened, second instance: a brief that states a *property* comes back as a *claim* in the source** — and the dangerous variant is a claim pointing **forward** at a block that does not exist yet, where nothing can contradict it until someone builds against it. Briefs state instructions; when a brief names a property, the block that lands it owes a check that the property is the one actually built.
- **7.1's parenthetical scenario claim is narrowed, not met in full by B2** — its *"The host reaches its session through the gateway"* half needs B5's production transport plus §8.1, and its "spawns no core process" half is closed by inspection (`ChildInventory` spawns only `ndmon`). See the section-7 post. **Do not tick 7.1 as covering that requirement.**
- **Section 7's block plan (architect), five blocks:** B1 rename + endpoint defaults (no behaviour) · B2 = 7.1+7.2 create→attach handshake and rejection · B3 = 7.3 cursor + reattach · B4 = 7.4 turn submission + streamed rendering · B5 = the device-key connect flow (no task numbers of its own; closes 6.5's two deferred scenarios).
- **From the section-6 supervisor, dispositions now set:**
  1. **Drop the endpoint default chain** — remove the defaults on `GatewayEndpoint.url` and `WebSocketGatewayTransport.init(endpoint:)` so co-location becomes a **call-site choice**, keeping `defaultURL` as a constant a caller opts into. → **B1.**
  2. **Rename one side of `DeviceCredential`** → **B1**, Swift's side, to `DeviceKeySecret` (Product Owner, 2026-08-07 — see the section-7 opening post for why that name and not `OwnDeviceKey` or `DeviceKeyStore`).
  3. **Pick one composition shape for `DevicesFileReader`.** `DeviceAuthPolicy` takes it **injected**; `DeviceKeyProvisioner` takes a `directory: URL` and constructs its own. **B5 is the third consumer** the supervisor said would settle it — so B5 converts the provisioner to the injected form.
  4. **Decide whether the live `ndmon` acceptance becomes a standing CI job** or stays a one-off — three of the four cross-language rules have no tripwire today. **Still open; needs the Product Owner**, and best decided once B5 has made the live run possible.
  5. **Add the legacy-keychain ACL prompt to the deferred verification.** `KeychainDeviceCredentialStore` sets neither `kSecAttrAccessible` nor `kSecUseDataProtectionKeychain`, so on macOS this targets the **legacy file-based keychain**, whose ACL keys on code signature — with ad-hoc signing, expect a *"DmonHomeApp wants to access your keychain"* prompt on **every rebuild**. Not a defect (an ad-hoc-signed app cannot use the data-protection keychain), but the same shape as the TCC/cdhash trap already recorded, and unless the recipe says so the person running it will read it as a bug. → the B5 verification recipe.
- **Deferred docs pass gains a member (found in section 7's opening read of the wire):** `docs/protocol/README.md:100,113` documents the create frame as `{"gw":"create","profile":"coding"}` rejecting `unknown_profile`; the code uses **`agent`**/**`unknown_agent`** and also emits **`core_timeout`**, which the doc omits. Out of scope for this change.
- **OWED WHEN SECTION 7 WIRES THE CONNECT FLOW — two proofs deferred with the Product Owner's agreement (2026-08-07), not dropped.** Both are unprovable today for the same reason: **`DeviceKeyProvisioner` and `KeychainDeviceCredentialStore` have no call sites**, and the app target does not depend on the `DeviceKeys` product at all.
  1. **The real Keychain path** — `SecItemCopyMatching`/`SecItemAdd`/`SecItemUpdate` and the `AnyObject → Data` cast are never executed by `swift test` (deliberately: CI must not touch the Keychain). Ticked on the section-4 precedent, which covers a thin wrapper over static platform calls whose seam is tested. **A live round trip across a process restart** — write via `store()`, read back in a *later* run — is the specific thing no in-memory conformer can stand in for.
  2. **Whether `Dmon.Network` actually accepts a credential this client provisioned.** This is the whole two-language contract — hex digest, exact field names, timestamp format, header form — and **no Swift test can close it**, because the thing that must agree is a .NET process. The reviewer traced both sides independently and believes them compatible; that is reasoning from reading, not evidence. A golden-file test was considered and **rejected as a false guard** (see the B9 entry): nothing would regenerate it, so a Swift-side rename would leave it passing against a file no longer representative.
  **The moment section 7 has a working connect flow, both become one live verification** — provision against a real `ndmon`, then authenticate — and that single run proves more than any amount of harness built now against code nothing calls.
- **Also owed at that point, from B9's own docs:** whether the eventual caller must **serialise provisioning attempts**. `provision()`'s two precondition checks and `appendEntry`'s read-then-replace are three separate points in time against one file with no locking; a concurrent writer's change would be silently overwritten and nothing would detect it. Unreachable while there is no caller — which is exactly why it is written down now.
- **NARROWED BY B8, NOT CLOSED — the `secretHash` half of the credential-mismatch gap is still open (supervisor).** B8 closed the **`keyId`** half: a held credential whose id is revoked or absent is now refused by name. But a credential whose `keyId` is **active while its `secretHash` in the file has been rotated or restored** still returns `.presentCredential` and 401s at connect with nothing naming the cause — the exact failure the five outcomes exist to prevent. `status(ofKeyId:)` already has `entry.secretHash` in hand and `DeviceCredential.secretHash` is available, so **closing it is a comparison, not a design.** Not section 6's job; do not let the record read as though B8 finished it.
- **Rules earned in section 6, consolidated — these outlived the blocks that produced them.**
  1. **A green suite proves agreement with your fixtures, not fidelity to the system you are mirroring.** B7's five fixtures modelled an active device in a way the real host has *always* treated as inactive, and every assertion passed, because the reader and its fixtures agreed with each other. B8 found two more tests passing for the same reason (a mismatched `keyId` that nothing compared). **A fixture standing in for a real file must be checked against whatever actually writes that file**, not only against the code reading it. B9's round-trip test is the shape that answers this: the file under test was written by the code under test.
  2. **When a comment explains why *another* component behaves as it does, verify it against that component, not against the code beside it — and when one turns out wrong, search for its copies before fixing the instance in front of you.** A false constant-time claim survived three reviews and two language boundaries because each diff-local review asked only "is this diff right?", and copying a wrong sentence faithfully is, diff-locally, right.
  3. **Check each task's parenthetical scenario claim against what its block can actually reach — at brief-writing time, not tick time.** Cheapest check available, and section 6 needed it three times.
  4. **Comment weight should track the risk of the line, not the obscurity of the API** (supervisor). B9 carries 22 lines of correct POSIX reasoning about `close`/`EINTR` on a path no test drives, in a file whose real risk is a two-language field contract.
- **BINDING ON SECTION 7, from B5's teardown reorder: "the stream ended" is not proof "the transport is closed."** `teardown(throwing:)` finishes the consumer's stream **synchronously, before** it awaits `transport.close()` — deliberately, because that is what makes the first entrant's outcome win regardless of which close resumes first (measured: old order 0/50, new order 50/50). The consequence a reattach path must not assume away: on observing stream termination, the previous socket may still be closing. Nothing in scope today depends on the reverse, and `teardownPrefersTheFirstEntrantsErrorRegardlessOfWhichTransportCloseResumesFirst` now guards the ordering itself.
- **BINDING ON 7.3, and the single most important thing section 6 hands forward: the sequence counter increments at *yield* time, never at *receive* time.** `GatewayConnection.close()` deliberately abandons a frame still sitting unconsumed in the transport — near-deterministically, 15/15 in the reviewer's repro. That is safe **only** under this counting rule: an undelivered frame was never counted, so `lastSeq` is honest and reattach-and-replay recovers it, exactly as for any dropped connection. Written into `close()`'s doc comment as well as here. **Its limit, stated so it is not discovered later:** recovery needs a reattach to actually happen — on a final app-quit close there is none, and that frame is gone, equivalent to quitting mid-turn.
- **And the part the rule does *not* settle, flagged by the reviewer before 7.3 is briefed against the sentence: "yielded" and "observed by the consumer" are different events.** An item can sit in the stream's unbounded buffer, yielded and counted, while section 8's consumer has not drained it. `finish()` does not clear buffered elements (verified), so `GatewayConnection.close()` itself is safe — but if a consumer is ever abandoned mid-drain for a reason *upstream of this actor*, a yield-time `lastSeq` counts events the app never processed and the next reattach skips replaying them. **Same silent-desync shape, different door.** So 7.3 owes a decision: does `lastSeq` track yield-availability or actual consumption? "Count at yield, never at receive" is **necessary but not sufficient**, and a reader could easily take it for the complete rule.
- **`close()` is unhangable but not leak-free**, and the two are different claims — see [`tech-debt/websocket-receive-cancellation-leak.md`](../../../tech-debt/websocket-receive-cancellation-leak.md). The orphaned read-loop task on a live socket is deferred deliberately: the closure-based `receive(completionHandler:)` fix cannot be verified without a real socket, and swapping an untestable claim for another is what this change keeps getting caught doing.
- **A proven defect in *closed* section 4/5 code, found sideways** — see [`tech-debt/timeout-race-cannot-bound-uncooperative-work.md`](../../../tech-debt/timeout-race-cannot-bound-uncooperative-work.md). `Supervisor/TimeoutRace.swift` carries the same false comment the `GatewayClient` copy did, and its two call sites (`HealthChecker.swift:50`, `HostSupervisor.swift:787`) were **deliberately not audited** — out of B3's scope. If either raced operation can park uninterruptibly, a supervisor shutdown can hang, on exactly the path sections 4 and 5 spent rounds hardening.
- **New, from B2 and cheap to apply: check each task's parenthetical scenario claim against what its block can actually reach — at brief-writing time, not at tick time.** 6.2's said "all four scenarios" including one that needs the read loop it does not build. Ticking it as written would have recorded a scenario as covered by a block structurally unable to cover it. Twice this section now (6.1's was widened deliberately and correctly; 6.2's was simply wrong), and it is the cheapest possible check.
- **The client depends on the host's inbound discriminator tolerance, not merely offering it outbound.** `.sortedKeys` puts `gw` after `agent` in an outbound `create`. Verified safe against `GetGwDiscriminator`/`ParseAttach`/`ParseCreate` and recorded at the call site in `ControlFrameCodec.swift`. **Anything that ever makes a C# receive path parse a control frame positionally breaks this client silently** — no Swift test can see it, because the Swift side is correct in both worlds. **Sections 1–5 are closed, all supervisor-approved.**
- **New rule, from B1 and stronger than the ones it resembles: a directive phrased as a *property* comes back as a *claim* in the source.** Sections 3 and 5 both shipped comments asserting a story the code did not support, but those were written *about* existing code. B1's overclaim was written *ahead* of the code, in my remediation brief — "make the transition structurally unrepresentable rather than guarded" — and the worker implemented the right thing and then described it in my words. **Neither the worker nor a diff-local reviewer is well placed to catch this**: the sentence reads as a design property, and the person who would normally challenge it wrote it. The tell is available and cheap: only the sites echoing the brief's language overclaimed, while the file the worker commented in its own words was accurate. **So: briefs state instructions, not properties** — "close() must not move a never-connected transport into the closed state" rather than "make it unrepresentable" — and when a brief does name a property, the block that lands it owes a check that the property is the one actually built.
- **BINDING ON SECTION 7 AND 8.3 (was B3's brief, still live):** the transport reports **three distinct facts** — `.notConnected`, `.closedLocally`, `.closed(code:reason:)` carrying 4400/4404/4409/4500/1009 — and the connection actor propagates them rather than re-wrapping generically. That distinction is what lets a caller tell "superseded by a newer attach" (4409) from "the core failed" (4500) from "we hung up", and it is the discriminator 8.3 needs to attribute a failed turn to the right layer. The one fact not reachable offline — a real peer close with a populated `closeCode` — is documented as untested rather than faked; **if section 7 finds a way to drive it without a live host, that comment should be revisited.**
- **Carry into section 10's CI brief (third item now):** `make dmon-home-ios-check` succeeds but emits `IDERunDestination: Supported platforms for the buildables in the current scheme is empty` on a generic iOS destination. Cosmetic — the build compiles the right three `GatewayClient` sources and exits 0 — but in a CI log read after a failure elsewhere it reads as an error, and the person reading it will not know that. Either suppress it or note it in the job. Section 5 landed in six commits: `858fd2e` (5.1), `ee28cd3` (fd inheritance, unnumbered), `97ab848` (5.2), `2045b27` (supervisor remediation), `d9f6426` (toolchain note), `1f9077a` (fixture readiness race).
- ~~**DECIDE BEFORE 6.1/6.2:** whether `GatewayClient` is shaped and named as a shareable Swift client package.~~ **Resolved by the Product Owner 2026-08-06 — shaped as one, extraction deferred, name kept.** See design D16 and section 6's opening posts. What survives as live work: the three no-foreclosing constraints (no `home/` sibling or app-target dependency, no macOS-only framework, host-only behaviour outside the module) and the iOS build gate that enforces them.
- **From section 5, carried into section 6 and beyond:**
  - **The two mechanisms 6.4 and 6.5 need already exist** — `WireVersion.swift` for the version check (it owes only a *mismatch error* type; it carries just `ParseError`), and the same wire-not-regrow rule that section 5 applied to `ActivityAssertion`. Say so in the brief, as section 5's brief did.
  - **`AppDelegate`'s termination path is load-bearing on *process-scoped* resources only.** The activity-assertion release sits in the shutdown task, so if the budget task wins the race it never runs — which is sound *only* because a dead process cannot keep asserting. A later section putting a **non**-process-scoped resource on that path (a file lock, a remote lease, a Tailscale registration — all plausible in section 6/7) breaks it **with no test signal**. The app target has no test bundle.
  - **`isGatewayEnabled` is a one-shot read fixed at `init`**, so "enabled" can only ever mean build/config time, never "actually serving". That matches the spec's wording exactly and was the right call for 5.2. **Section 6 is where it first bites**: an unasked product question is whether a real gateway client failing should release the assertion, and whether a second always-on child should also hold it.
  - **The observer single-construction rule is convention, not construction.** `ChildStatusObserver` and now `ChildLogObserver` both carry "construct exactly one, never from a view" doc comments guarding a real window-reopen subscriber leak — and the app target has no test bundle, so a future view constructing its own leaks with **no compiler or test signal**. Two instances now; **a third makes it worth closing by construction.**
  - **Store-collapse trigger, bounded rather than re-judged each time:** `ChildLogStore` is the third `snapshot + subscribers + updates() + onTermination` actor. The supervisor ruled *leave them* — the duplicated part is ~20 lines of plumbing, the payload semantics genuinely differ, and collapsing costs either an extra actor hop on every log line or `@unchecked Sendable` machinery D14 reserves elsewhere. **A fourth such store is the trigger to extract; before that it is not a judgement call.**
  - **Highest-value pickup, parked deliberately and with the supervisor's agreement — `.launchNotDecided` / `.executableNotResolved` / `.spawnFailed` logs nothing and publishes nothing.** So the most likely real-world failure — **`ndmon` not installed, no launch candidate resolves** — renders as health `unknown`, supervision `normal`, output "No output yet": three panes, no signal. Not a contract violation (a child that never started never wrote to stdout, so the scenario is *unreachable*, not unmet) but it defeats the requirement's stated purpose, *"so failures are diagnosable without leaving the app"*. The mechanism to fix it — `ChildLogSource.host` — was **built in section 5 and pointed at the one branch that isn't a failure** (`.adopted`). Fix is ~3 lines in a branch that already exists. **Right home is 8.3**, the first task that asks the UI to explain a failure rather than name it.
  - **`home/TOOLCHAIN-NOTES.md` now exists** and is where the suspected Swift 6.3.3 `swift_task_dealloc` defect is stated once; both workaround sites point at it. **Needs the Product Owner: whether to file it upstream** — the evidence is close to filable, and not filing means occurrence three starts from zero. The note records that the upstream tracker was **not searched**, which is "we did not look", not "we looked and found nothing".
  - **A readiness signal must be written *after* the thing it signals readiness for.** Section 5 found this in a fixture (`gracefulShutdownTriggersNoRestart`) and section 4 found the same shape in a human verification recipe (`ChildHealth.starting`). **Two instances, two artefact kinds — it is a rule now, not a coincidence**, and it belongs in the brief for any block that writes a fixture or a recipe.
  - **Flagged, deliberately unfixed, different shape:** `ChildSpawnerTests`' `aSpawnedChildDoesNotInheritThisProcessesSignalMaskOrDispositions` uses a fixed 200 ms `Task.sleep` rather than an event-based readiness marker. A timing *assumption*, not an early-signal defect — so not covered by the rule above, and worth hardening on its own terms if it ever flakes.
  - **Resolved by section 5:** the `ChildHealth.unhealthy` "timed out vs refused" split that `## NEXT` parked here — **declined**, because the log pane carries the *child's* output while a health-check result is an observation the *host* makes, so the pane was never the consumer that would force the split. Revisit at 8.3. Also resolved: the duplicated-abstraction hazard for `ActivityAssertion` (wired, not regrown) and the section-4 testability ruling for 5.1 (buffering landed in `Supervisor`; only the view in the app target).
- **From section 4's supervisor, carried into section 5 and beyond:**
  - **The one structural fix it recommended, not blocking but not speculative either.** The deleted process-group sweep's soundness now rests on `HostSupervisor.shutdown()`'s `for` loop always walking every child — true because the loop is not a cancellation checkpoint. Cancellation itself is *safe*: both `withTimeout` outcomes reach a group `SIGKILL`, so cancelling degrades to "kill everything fast". The exposure is someone adding `try Task.checkCancellation()` inside that loop, or rewriting it into a throwing form that bails — then children later in the reverse order are never signalled, **with no compiler or test signal**, because the sweep iterated `states.values` rather than following the loop. **One-line fix when it's wanted:** wrap `await supervisor.shutdown()` in `HostRuntime.shutdownForTermination()` in an unstructured `Task { }.value`, making the walk uncancellable by construction — the pattern already used for the shielded reap. Optionally with a test that cancels `shutdownForTermination()` immediately and asserts a `trap '' TERM` grandchild still dies. `AppDelegate`'s doc comment carries the corrected scoping.
  - **A crashed child's descendants are never group-killed — at any point, not just at shutdown.** `handleExit` clears `spawnedChild` on *every* exit including a crash, without killing the group; the restart then spawns into a *new* group and `shutdownChild` returns early because `spawnedChild` is `nil`, so survivors leak for the host's lifetime. **Pre-existing, not a regression** (the old sweep missed it identically) and not an unmet requirement — requirement 5 says "on exit" — but it is **D6's stated hazard arriving by the crash path**, and now cheap to close: `handleExit`'s non-intentional branch has `child` in hand before it clears state.
  - **Graceful-branch-under-cancellation skips the reap and the state clear.** `handleExit` never runs, so `spawnedChild` stays populated, `.stoppedIntentionally` is never published, and the `SIGKILL`ed leader is never reaped. Harmless at app exit; **matters if `shutdownForTermination()` ever gains a non-exit caller — know this before section 8.**
  - **Two comments still overclaim, named because it is this section's signature shape recurring inside the remediation** — neither worth a round: `worstCaseShutdownDurationIsGracefulTimeoutTimesEnabledChildCount` claims its values discriminate "summing instead of multiplying", but with a single per-supervisor timeout, summing over 3 children *is* 7×3 (its off-by-one discrimination is real, so the test is fine); and `HostSupervisor.swift`'s "the only other case is a fully empty group, where `kill(2)` returns `ESRCH`" — a pgid recycled between reap and kill would not `ESRCH`. Microseconds wide and needs pid wraparound, so not a hazard, but the sentence is stronger than the fact.
  - **The app-target line, set so it does not have to be re-argued in section 5 or 8:** *the app target may hold AppKit-protocol wiring and at-most-once guards; it may not hold a decision a spec scenario asserts on, nor a policy value not derived from a package type.* This sits alongside the section-3 tripwire below, and it is what sent `terminationBudget` back to the package.
  - **The human-facing falsification rule — route to the agent definitions and `openspec-apply`, not just this change.** For a test the observer is the runner; for a verification recipe it is a human, and **absence reads as user error, not system falsity** — a failing test shouts, a failing recipe step is silently absorbed by the person running it. So the rule is *stronger* than for tests, in two parts: every step names the falsifying observation **and** what it looks like when the claim is false; and **before a recipe ships, trace the publisher of every state it asks a human to look for.** Mechanical, and exactly what would have caught the `ChildHealth.starting` step. The recipe is **the one artifact in the loop with no automated auditor.**
  - **Remediation blocks get audited against the original claim, not only their own diff** — three of this section's nine defects were introduced by remediations for earlier instances of the same shape.
  - **The Product Owner's adopted-vs-spawned discriminator is real and worth using:** adopted reports `healthy` immediately, spawned takes ~30s, because `HealthMonitor.run` calls `checkOnce` before its first sleep. Unlike `ChildHealth.starting`, this is an observation the system genuinely makes — fold it into section 5's status rendering.
  - **Test-count note for judging future sections:** 93 of the 103 tests are in `SupervisorTests`, and roughly eight remain enum/model tautologies. Not worth touching; worth not counting as coverage.
- **Carry into section 10's CI brief:** `aHungCheckPublishesFailureWithoutBlockingOtherChildren` now depends on real timing — concurrent ≈150-170ms against a 300ms bound. The reviewer rated ~150ms of jitter headroom *adequate but not generous* for a loaded GitHub macOS runner. If it flakes there, the fix is to **widen the gap** (e.g. 100ms healthy latency against a 250ms bound), not to loosen the bound back to something serial execution also passes — that would restore exactly the defect this block spent a round removing.
- **Decision owed by section 5:** `ChildHealth.unhealthy` still collapses *"timed out"* and *"responded but refused"*. That satisfies the spec ("recorded as failed") and section 4 deliberately left it, but the log pane is the first consumer with a real reason to tell them apart. Decide it there rather than widening `ChildHealth` speculatively — and note section 4's ruling on what may widen it at all: **model facets earn the "built for the full inventory" shelter; observation states do not**, because a new child added purely as configuration can never produce a new observation state without a new writer.
- **Product Owner decision, 2026-08-02 — speech runs IN-PROCESS in Swift, not in a Python/mlx sidecar.** Adopting [`soniqo/speech-swift`](https://github.com/soniqo/speech-swift) (Apache 2.0, SPM, ~1.1k stars / 876 commits) with Silero VAD + Parakeet-EOU + Kokoro on CoreML. **Sequencing decided by the PO: land section 4 under the current ADR, then write the superseding ADR as its own change, informed by a spike.** It binds PRD Phases 2–3, which are outside this change entirely.
  - **This is a clean supersession, not a preference.** Section 3's remediation left ADR-037 D4 / design D7 standing on exactly two grounds: ADR-034's uv-venv pattern, and *"the absence of any in-process Swift MLX path"*. `speech-swift` **is** that path — so D7's last surviving rationale does not weaken, it is gone. It also **resolves the section-3 supervisor's parked question outright**: "the Python speech sidecar has no bucket" (D1 makes `home/` pure-Swift; the uv-venv pattern needs a .NET-package shape `home/` cannot host). In-process makes the question disappear rather than answering it. And it is the strongest available form of D15 — speech follows the host.
  - **Costs to carry into that change, all real, none fatal:** (1) **macOS 15+** is required (`MLState`, Apple's persistent ANE state API) — moves the already-landed macOS 14+ decision in **ADR-037 D3** and task 2.1; (2) `home/` gains its **first external dependency**, and the README's install snippet is `branch: "main"` with no tag — wants pinning to a revision plus a committed `Package.resolved`, and section 10's CI job starts fetching; (3) **ADR-037 D4 is binding**, so per `CLAUDE.md` the superseding ADR must be accepted *before* implementation — and per section 3's own ruling, a reversal is **new normative content, not an amendment note**.
  - **Unverified, and the spike's job:** the README says "powered by MLX **and** CoreML". Parakeet is explicitly CoreML/Neural Engine; **no backend statement was found for Kokoro or Silero**, so "all three on CoreML" is a hypothesis. Also unmeasured: latency on the target machine. `Examples/DictateDemo` is the closest reference — macOS menu-bar streaming dictation, live partials, VAD-driven EOU, Parakeet-EOU-120M + Silero VAD — and is very nearly dmon-home's dictation path already. Model weights download from HuggingFace on first use into `~/Library/Caches/qwen3-speech/`, with `offlineMode: true`, so **no Git LFS problem** — the weight-storage question that the `Dmail` ONNX precedent raised does not arise.
  - **Blast radius inside *this* change is two lines:** `ChildInventory.speechSidecar`, and the clause naming "the speech sidecar" in the supervision spec's "child model accommodates the full inventory" requirement. **Deliberately left in place** — the spec as written still names it, 4.1 must satisfy the spec it was written against, and removing a child is a value deletion, which is precisely what 4.1's model claims to make free. When the ADR lands, that claim gets tested for real.
  - **Deferred docs pass gains a member:** PRD §4.4 (Silero VAD host-side via ONNX Runtime + CoreML EP) and PRD Q4's recorded answer both change. `speech-swift` supplies VAD itself, so the ONNX Runtime dependency PRD §4.4 assumes is replaced by one dependency rather than two.
- **`.process` health checks are still unimplemented — `HealthChecker` returns `.unknown` for them without executing anything.** Section 4 established the rule they must follow when they land: **run-to-completion-and-inspect-exit, never a persistent spawn/adopt target**, or a monitor gains a launch path through the back door and 4.1's "illegal state unrepresentable" guarantee has a hole in it. `ChildInventory.tailscale` is the one descriptor declaring `.process`, and the Product Owner's Option-B monitor decision deliberately left both facts standing.
- **Deferred docs pass now has three members** (all out of scope for this change): the hard-coded ADR count (fix is *deletion*, not maintenance), the ADR-013 row/file status mismatch, and **`.serena/memories/adrs.md`** — a tracked six-ADR summary with **no consumer** (the agent definitions state there is no Serena MCP in this project). Same argument as the count: a derived duplicate nobody maintains and no reader benefits from. An orphaned ADR summary is the exact trap the index nearly became, and worse, because nothing will ever prompt anyone to update it.
- **A second by-design ADR duplication, named but not actioned:** `.claude/agents/{worker,reviewer,supervisor}.md` restate binding ADR constraints as review checklists (13, 13 and 10 distinct ADRs). Nothing is stale from section 3 — they are .NET-engine constraints and none touches ADR-037 — but they duplicate ADR content by design and are maintained by `dmons:update-scaffold` rather than by change work, so they cannot drift *from* a change and will drift *behind* one silently. This needs its own rule, not an extension of the index rule: the trigger is **a binding .NET-side ADR changing**, not any ADR gaining a decision.
- **Testability rule for the section-5 and section-8 briefs (supervisor ruling).** The untested app-target surface is **acceptable as it stands** — `home/App/DmonHomeApp/` is three small files and `MicrophoneAuthorizationModel` wraps static platform calls, so a seam around a system framework would be more machinery than the thing it guards, and an app-target test bundle costs a second scheme plus a CI change. The mitigation is not "add coverage later", it is **holding the shell discipline design D4 already states**. Both growth surfaces carry package-testable behaviour that need not land in the shell:
  - **5.1** — the log pane's *buffering* is what the spec asserts ("Output survives a child restart"). That belongs in `Supervisor`; only the SwiftUI view belongs in the app target.
  - **8.1** — the *attach-state gate* is what the spec asserts ("Input is refused when not attached"). That belongs in `GatewayClient`.
  - **Tripwire, checkable per block rather than deferred:** *if a spec scenario's assertion can only be evaluated by running the app target, the design has drifted* — and the response at that moment is a new package or an extension of an existing one, **not** a retrofitted app-target test bundle.
- **Convention worth adopting beyond this change — two halves, one judgement and one mechanical.**
  1. *(reviewer, section 3)* When a decision is **resolved or qualified** by a later one, add a **backward pointer at the resolved decision's own site**, not only a forward pointer from the resolver. design.md marked D7 "Resolved (D15)" in the topology table while leaving D7's own rationale prose stale; the section-1 loopback drift was the same shape.
  2. *(supervisor, section 3)* **Adding or renumbering a decision in any ADR requires touching that ADR's `adr-index` row in the same commit.** Fixed and enumerable rather than a judgement call, and it is the **only artifact in the repo that duplicates ADR decision content by design** — so the only one that can silently fall out of date this way. Half 1 does not catch it, because the index is not a decision site.
  The generalisation behind both, and the real lesson of this section: **scope a sweep by where the fact is duplicated, not by where the fix was.** Searching for stale wording cannot find a *missing addition* — that is why a clean `grep` for the old text coexisted with an index row that omitted an entire decision.
- **Architect boundary, named so it does not erode (supervisor).** My unreviewed `Makefile` comment reposition was judged proportionate, but the reasoning that justified it ("one line, a worker round is wasteful") reads identically for a change that *does* alter behaviour. The line: **comments and whitespace, disclosed in the DEVLOG — anything that changes what a recipe executes goes to a worker.** The `tasks.md` blank-line fix is the same category.
- **Now binding on two Product-Owner recipes, not one:** `tasks.md` 3.3 **and** 4.7 both hard-code paths derived from `-derivedDataPath home/.build-xcode` and `-configuration Release`. Switching to Debug for a faster UI loop points both recipes at a path that no longer exists, and it breaks **in the Product Owner's hands mid-verification**. A rule, not a risk: **change either flag and update both recipes in the same block.**
- **Ad-hoc signing, still live for any future app-target verification:** TCC keys grants on bundle id **plus cdhash**, so the grant is discarded on every rebuild. Any authorisation-status UI reverting to `.notDetermined` after a rebuild **must not be read as a bug** — and 4.7's recipe notes the related cost, that launching the raw binary inside the bundle (needed for the `DMON_NETWORK_PATH` override) forfeits the bundle's grant.
- **Section-10 brief must carry:** GitHub's macOS runners **do not ship `xcodegen`** — 10.1's job needs `brew install xcodegen` before it can call `make dmon-home-app`, which is otherwise the right target for it to call since it is the only path that compiles the app shell. Also weigh dropping `-quiet` in CI: errors still print, but failure diagnostics are thinner than you want in a log read after the fact.
- **The duplicated-abstraction hazard, now a standing check rather than a per-section note:** before briefing any block, ask whether the mechanism it names **already exists and needs wiring rather than regrowing**. It has been right three times running — 5.2's assertion (`Power/ActivityAssertion.swift`), 6.4's version check (`GatewayClient/WireVersion.swift`, referenced by nothing but its own tests until B5 wired it), and B6's `secretHash` reused by B9 instead of a second hashing site.
- **Carry into the section-10 brief:** word the `area-map.yml` comment (task 10.2) as **"non-.NET paths"** rather than "both Swift packages", so D7's Python speech sidecar does not require a third edit when it lands. Also: 10.1's CI job must build the **app**, not just the packages — that is the whole point of the `dmon-home-app` target.
- **Architectural notes parked by the supervisor:**
  - **RESOLVED 2026-08-06 (design D16) — `GatewayClient`'s placement may not deliver ADR-037 D2's own rationale.** The supervisor called this exactly right, and the Product Owner answered it "same code": the module is shaped for extraction now and carved out by the change that answers ADR-028 D5's `dmon-swift` question. Original note kept below because its reasoning is what the extraction change inherits. D2 rationale 4 — "the local and remote clients become the same client, over the same protocol, tested once" — is load-bearing for the whole gateway-client stance. But D1 puts `GatewayClient` in `home/` as a *local* SPM package of the macOS host, and the personal iOS client (in scope per ADR-012) is a separate product that cannot depend on it there. ADR-028 D5 left `dmon-swift`, the prospective Swift *client* SDK, as an open question and this section walked past it. **Decide before task 6.1/6.2** whether `GatewayClient` is shaped and named as a shareable Swift client package, or whether rationale 4 should be read as "same protocol", not "same code". Cheap to carve out of `home/` later in SPM terms; not free in ADR terms.
  - ~~**The Python speech sidecar has no bucket.**~~ **Resolved by the Product Owner's 2026-08-02 in-process-Swift decision above** — the question disappears rather than being answered, since there is no sidecar to place. Kept as a pointer because the *reasoning* survives: `home/` is a pure-Swift bucket (D1), and the ADR-034 uv-venv pattern needs a .NET-package shape it cannot host, so any future Python-shaped dependency hits the same wall.
  - **The prospective artifact amendment may point at the wrong ADR.** ADR-037 marks ADR-025 D10 / ADR-028 D6 prospectively amended, but **ADR-035 D3/D7** is now the operative package→family map and `package-publishing`'s "App-artifact release family" is the standing requirement. Those are what actually move when `dmon-home` ships an artifact.
- **Needs the Product Owner:** **PRD §8 says "Do not set `AllowNonLoopbackBind`"** — flatly contradicted by the split-topology row in design.md, which notes a split *requires* opting into it (ADR-036 fail-closes an empty key store on a non-loopback bind). Unlike the other two split-topology rows (provisioning, speech location) this is a **security posture the Product Owner owns**, not a technical unknown. Does not block any section; must be settled before a split is attempted.
- **Open questions:**
  - **PRD Q1** — whether `mlx_lm` initialises Metal from a `LaunchDaemon` context. Does not bite while the app is foreground-launched, but constrains the always-on story. Needs a live test before any daemon split. Now also gates any revisit of Q5 (below).
- **Resolved:**
  - **Q5 — device-key provisioning ownership** (Product Owner, 2026-08-02): `dmon-home` **self-provisions**; the `ndmon` provisioning-verb alternative is not taken, so no prerequisite change lands ahead of section 6. Task 6.5 stands as written, and its pinned-digest test is what contains the two-language hash coupling. Recorded in design.md D13/Q5 with the corrected rationale: safety here rests on **process position, not network position** — unsandboxed, same user as the `devices.json` owner, loopback-only listener — *not* on the host being on a trusted LAN. If the host is ever split into a `LaunchDaemon` under a different principal, or the gateway takes a non-loopback bind (which ADR-036 fail-closes), this lapses and provisioning must be revisited.
- **Known future topology (Product Owner, 2026-08-02):** `dmon-home` and the back-end may later run on **separate machines on the same LAN** (host on a Mac mini in a living space; models on a larger machine elsewhere). Decision: **record, do not foreclose, do not build.** Sections 1–10 stay co-located. Captured in design.md → "Known future topology" with a per-concern table. Architect error caught here: design D2 said "loopback WebSocket" where PRD §7.4 already says *"reached by hostname, not assumed to be local"* — D2 now says **configured gateway endpoint**, and the PRD's stdio fallback is marked contingent on co-location (you cannot spawn a core over stdio on a machine you are not running on). Block 1.1–1.3 was stopped mid-flight to correct ADR-037 D2 before it landed with the narrowed wording. Two rows to reopen before any split is attempted: **device-key provisioning** (self-provisioning is impossible cross-machine; needs the `ndmon` verb) and **speech sidecar location** (audio is where the person is; memory is on the other box).
- **Outside this change — ADR-034 has no record of the Apple Silicon constraint.** Confirmed by grep: no `arm64`, `metal`, `unified memory`, `apple silicon`, `x86` or `intel` anywhere in it. This is the **root cause** of task 3.4 — the requirement that makes Intel impossible existed in reality and nowhere in writing, so nothing enforced it. ADR-037 D5 now records it for `dmon-home`, but ADR-034 governs the MLX runtime itself (`Dmon.Providers.Mlx`, the Daemon's triage/escalation pair, the future speech sidecar), so the next consumer that isn't `dmon-home` — a sidecar bucket decision, CI runner selection, the release matrix asking which artifacts are arch-restricted — hits the same silence and re-derives the same fact. **Own one-file change, instrument already chosen: an in-place amendment note on ADR-034.** That one *genuinely is* framing-only under the ADR-028 precedent — it states a physical fact that was always true and changes no decision — which is the instructive contrast with the arm64 blocker, where the same instrument was wrong. Keep it separate from the deferred docs pass: ADR-013's status label and the hard-coded ADR count are stale metadata; this is a **binding constraint that is currently unwritten**.
- **For the section-10 brief:** `ARCHS` is now a cross-surface invariant with two enforcement points (`home/project.yml` and the `Makefile` guard), and 10.1's CI job will be a third reader of it. The `lipo` guard living in the recipe means 10.1 **inherits it free** — do not add a parallel check.
- **Outside this change, needs its own investigation — now with a second, *unidentified* sighting (2026-08-06, during section 6 B2).** One full `make test` reported `Failed: 1, Passed: 612` in `Dmon.Core.Tests`; the re-run and three further dedicated runs passed clean, roughly one failure in eight runs that day. **Which test failed was not captured** and it has not fired since, so whether the two sightings share a cause is **unknown** — do not merge them into one story without evidence. What the second sighting does establish is that an intermittent failure in `Dmon.Core.Tests` is **recurring rather than a one-off**, which is what changes this from a curiosity into something worth a session. **Next occurrence will be diagnosable:** gate runs now preserve full test output rather than grepping the summary line. The original sighting: `WizardEngineTests.InvalidChooseOneAnswer_RePromptsStep` (`test/Dmon.Core.Tests/Rpc/WizardEngineTests.cs`) failed once under `make test` on 2026-08-02 and passed standalone and on rerun. Exercises `core/Dmon.Core/Rpc/ProviderSetupHandler.cs`. Unrelated to `dmon-home-foundations` — no block here touches .NET source. Worth a look on its own terms rather than being written off as flaky: an invalid answer intermittently failing to re-prompt could be an ordering or async-timing defect in the wizard rather than a test artifact.
- **Nits / deferred:**
  - `adr-index` frontmatter and `CLAUDE.md` both hard-code an ADR count, which drifts every time an ADR lands (it was already wrong before this block — "35" was the file count). Now correct at 32. Supervisor's verdict on disposition, which I accept: deferring it out of this section is right, but **the fix is deletion, not maintenance** — it is a derived value stored in two files, it has been wrong on two of its last two touches, and no reader benefits from it. The future docs pass should replace it with "Summaries of every accepted ADR", not re-derive a number.
  - **`make clean` cleans neither Swift tree** (supervisor, pre-existing): it is `rm -rf build/`, which misses `daemon/Daemon.App/.build/` as well as the new `home/.build/` and `home/.build-xcode/`. The new targets are therefore *consistent* with existing behaviour rather than introducing an inconsistency — noted only so it is a known gap rather than a discovery.
  - **ADR-013 status mismatch** (reviewer's note, pre-existing): the `adr-index` row says *"(Superseded by ADR-022.)"* but `docs/adrs/ADR-013-agent-profiles.md` reads `**Status:** Accepted`. The ADR-009 pair agrees across row and file; ADR-013 does not. Does not affect the 32-count. Fold into the same future docs pass.
  - **New precedent to watch** (supervisor, no action now): ADR-037 D1 says the bucket "materialises with its first member, which is the requirements document" — the first case in the repo of a **non-project file** counting as a bucket member under ADR-025's memberless rule. Contained to `home/` as written, and `monorepo-layout`'s rule text is unchanged, but the sentence is quotable if the rule is ever tested elsewhere (a stray `middleware/README.md`). Worth a look during the docs pass.
  - No release artifact for `dmon-home` — deferred until the app does something worth shipping (design D10).
- **Carry-forward:** dmonium (`daemon/Daemon.App`) must stay untouched and shipping for the whole change; its retirement is a separate change at parity (design D9, which carries the costed blast radius).
